# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum VII

## Funding proposal package for FP259

### Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Adapting tuna-dependent Pacific Island communities and economies to climate change";

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or fiscal position holder;

c) Secretary's assessment;

d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

e) Recommendations from the accredited entity to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

f) Gender documentation.

This document is prepared and transmitted for consideration by the GCF Board at a Board meeting. It is published on the GCF public website in that context, which does not imply a personal opinion. Decisions taken in relation to this document and updates to it if any will be made publicly available in accordance with applicable GCF policies.

A.2.1 Executive Summary

Climate change problem: This GCF Regional Tuna Programme (RTP) is designed to work with and support the governments of some of the most climate vulnerable countries in the world to build resilience and adapt to two major threats posed by climate change to tuna fisheries: 1) the decline in fish stocks due to ocean warming, which will have a direct impact on coral reef ecosystems and the redistribution of Pacific tuna resources due to the impacts of ocean warming.

Two critical challenges are identified for this GCF Regional Tuna Programme for two reasons. First, coral reefs have traditionally supplied much of the fish needed to provide the recommended protein intake required for domestic nutritional security (hereafter "food availability"), given the ease of access and abundance of reef fish in many coastal communities. Second, climate change is expected to increase human and animal biodiversity throughout the region. A gap between how much fish should be consumed by coastal and urban populations will emerge as a result of these two factors, with increasing demand for tuna from countries emerging in several PICS, driven mainly by population growth. The projected future degradation of coral reef ecosystems will further exacerbate this problem.

Second, of the 14 participating countries have significant dependence on access fees from industrial fleets fishing for tuna in countries' exclusive economic zones (EEZs). Participating countries receive >60% of their (non-grant) government revenue from these fees. The decline in fish stocks will reduce the amount of fish available to support government preparedness and post-disaster recovery. Current modelling indicates that under continued high GHG emissions, 15-20% of tuna biomass will be lost by 2030. This loss is expected to be even more severe if climate change causes tuna biomass redistribution to the east (and poleward to some extent) due to ocean warming. These losses will result from a combination of reduced fish stocks and increased competition for access in this high-value region.

Theory of Change and Key Programme Interventions:

4. The Programme's Theory of Change describes how the Programme will reduce impacts of climate change on the Pacific's most important resource by applying the priority interventions identified by the participating countries.

5. By strengthening national programmes to support Fish Aggregating Devices (FADs) in fisheries waters (A1), increasing access for small-scale fishers to FADs (A2), and improving FAD infrastructure (A3), national capacity to access tuna for coastal communities will be increased (Output 1). By delivering more tuna to coastal communities, the food security of these communities is improved. This will provide new livelihood opportunities for bycatch (A2) in urban communities, where there will be an increased supply of fish from industrial tuna fishing (A3). The Programme will also support the development of a National Tuna Strategy (NTS) (A4), which will serve as an economic planning tool for participating countries to understand and adapt to the shifting distribution of tuna stocks. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 5

## Harnessing the potential of bycatch available from transshipping and unloading operations by industrial tuna fishing vessels in regional ports to supply more fish for urban and peri-urban communities.

Small enterprises are already capitalising on this opportunity, but there is a need to scale up these efforts to provide greater economic opportunities for selling low-cost fish unsuitable for canning (bycatch) to urban communities. However, there is considerable potential for further development of the bycatch market in urban areas and it would be useful for fisheries agencies to: i) assess the potential supply of bycatch and the best ways to handle fish so that it feeds a larger number of people; ii) develop a marketing strategy for bycatch; iii) provide training on handling fish using the Advanced Warning System (AWS) described below in order to ensure that this potential is maximised under climate change.

### Developing an Advanced Warning System (AWS)

The AWS will serve as an economic planning tool that allows countries to plan ahead and make informed decisions about their tuna fishing activities. The AWS will provide a more realistic picture of the future state of tuna stocks, which will help fisheries agencies to better manage tuna resources in line with national policies.

### Key benefits

- 333 FADs across the region.
- Training and equipping small-scale fishers for safe and effective use of these FADs.
- Conjunction with national FAO programmes expected to deliver significant improvements in access to markets, particularly for women and children from five countries where transshipping operations are taking place.

### Improvements following the supply and distribution of bycatch

- Total 250,000 men, women, and children from five countries will have access to additional fish.
- In the five smallest participating countries, communities are targeted, ranging in size from 30,000–18 million fish per year.

### The AWS will enable nine tuna-dependent Pacific island economies

- To understand future trends and extent of tuna redistribution with much greater confidence.
- This information can be used as an economic planning tool by participating countries to better manage their tuna resources in line with national policies.

### The AWS will also benefit from the region's exclusive fishing areas outside the equatorial tuna fishing areas, which will also benefit from the AWS. The AWS will enable these countries to determine whether projections from preliminary modeling indicate that the biomass of tuna could increase in their EEZ due to redistributed fish.

### Information provided by the AWS will be used for decision-making and planning decisions required to sustain the productivity of the industrial fisheries across the Western and Central Pacific Ocean (WCPF), and the ecosystem that underpins these fisheries. A key indicator for the effectiveness of the AWS is the extent to which the WCPF Commission, the Pacific Islands Forum Fisheries Agency (PIFFA) and the Australian Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organisation (CSIRO) as Technical Partner grantees.

### The Programme will be implemented in close coordination with the 14 participating countries by the Pacific Community (SPC) as the Executive Entity.

B. PROJECT PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words or less) (2 pages)

#### Programme Region

The Oceania region in the WCPG includes some of the world's smallest countries surrounded by an area of ocean that is much larger than their land surface area (Figure 1). Contemporary Oceania is generally grouped along ethnographic and cultural lines as "Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia". The 14 countries participating in the CIF are all located within this region. Despite their small size, they share a common history of colonial rule, similar geographic proximity, and relatively small economies/populations; these countries have an established track record of cooperation on climate change issues and shared development objectives. Key information for the 14 countries is provided in Table 1 and further details are in Annex B2, Chapter 6 and 7.

The following pages provide a brief introduction to each country with a summary of their history, politics and governance, maritime claims, biogeography, population (including ethnic composition), agriculture and fisheries sector, and economics.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE B

## For coral reefs, current and projected degradation is driven largely by increasing sea surface temperature (SST) and acidification (see Annex 2, Section 2.3), with well-documented examples of thermal bleaching and acidification undermining the physical core of reef habitats. In addition, degraded core reefs are more prone to erosion and sedimentation, which can further degrade the structural complexity of reefs.

## Ocean warming is affecting the distribution of the region’s rich tuna resources. In the WCPFC, the abundant skipjack tuna (Katsuwonus pelamis) has shifted northward in recent decades as SSTs have increased. The Pacific warm pool and the Equatorial divergence (see Annex 2, Chapter 1, Section 3). This convergence zone is a key habitat for many species of tuna, including skipjack.

## Preliminary modeling indicates that skipjack tuna and the other tropical tuna species in equatorial areas are highly likely to shift progressively to the east as the ocean continues to warm and, to a lesser extent, into subtropical waters. The projected shifts will be most pronounced in the western Pacific Ocean. Skipjack tuna is projected to exclude from the exclusive economic zones (EEZs) of tuna-dependent FAs in equatorial waters by 2050 (range: 199–384 days). This projection is based on a model run for the period 2009-2018. Under RCP 4.5, projected losses average 3% (see Annex 2, Chapter 2, Section 2.9.4.2.a.7.).

## The implications of climate change for the availability of nutritious food for Pacific island people are profound.

## Food availability impacts

### 1. Implications of climate change for the availability of nutritious food for Pacific Island people are profound.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 9

## Scale fisheries provide 50-90% of dietary animal protein for coastal communities. By 2035, population growth and the negative effects of climate change on coral fish production will create demand for an additional 75,000 tonnes of tuna annually to meet projected food security needs.

### The rich tuna resources of the region are the most practical choice for filling this gap in fish supply. Importantly, climate-driven shifts in the distribution of tuna biomass are not expected to alter this conclusion; the tuna remaining in each country’s EEZ is expected to be sufficient to meet domestic demand and export commitments. In addition, apart from imports, there are few alternative sources of high-quality animal protein due to the limited potential for aquaculture.

### The region's tuna resources are not overfished nor subject to overfishing due to exemplary management by PICs, and no problems are anticipated in using tuna to provide most of the additional 75,000 tonnes of fish needed. Just this year, the Pacific Islands Forum Fisheries Agency (FFA) has approved a new regional tuna catch limit.

### Communities need additional support to catch and distribute tuna because these fish are usually found at significant distance from the coast. To make a turnpike of national food systems and increase the food availability and access for coastal communities, it is necessary that tuna be landed in ports and distributed through cold chains efficiently and safely; and ensure that the supply of tuna bycatch for urban communities, delivered by industrial fishing vessels, is increased and not discarded as feedlots and transferred or utilised their fish further to the east.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 16

## Economic impacts

PICS have already done much to sustain the economic benefits they receive from tuna. Regrettably, climate change is likely to reduce these benefits further. In response, the Pacific Islands Forum established a fisheries management system to deal with the effects of ENSO and climate change on the distribution of tuna within their combined EEZs. This mechanism – the Pacific Tuna Agreement (PTA) – vessel bytess Scheme (VBS) enables vessels to fish in areas outside their home EEZ up to 10% of the time, regardless of where the tuna are caught within their combined jurisdictions. The VBS is widely regarded as a successful example of international cooperation and has been extended beyond its original scope.

The present proposal would allow PNA members to access tuna from the high seas for up to 5 years at the all-present day benefits that PNA members receive from tuna as the fish move progressively from their contribution EEZs into the high seas.

20. Preliminary modelling indicates that climate-driven redistribution of tuna threatens to undermine the economies of the PNA member countries and the Cook Islands, which obtain an average of 35% (range = 4-70%) of their total (non-tuna) export earnings from tuna exports. The projected loss in tuna catches for these countries under RCP3.5, significant redistribution of tuna from the EEZs of these countries to the high seas is likely to occur. Consequently, the average annual tuna catch per country would be reduced by 10% (range = -24% to +6%) and 70% (range = -89% to +35%) under RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 respectively.

21. Under RCP3.5, the projected loss in tuna catches per country would be reduced by an average of 10% (range = -24% to +6%). The projected loss in tuna catches per country is significantly greater under RCP4.5 and RCP8.5, with losses ranging from a reduction of 70% (range = -89% to +35%) to an increase of 10% (range = -24% to +6%).

22. Such a significant reduction in government funding would have direct impacts on vulnerable populations in these countries and the Cook Islands, which rely heavily on tuna for their livelihoods.

Tuna redistribution may also affect employment in the region, where tuna fishing and processing provide ~25,000 jobs.

The priority adaptation for the tuna-dependent economies involves ensuring they have improved data, forecasts, and projections on climate-driven tuna redistribution. This will allow countries to negotiate to retain access to the tuna resources that are projected to remain within their EEZs under RCP3.5.

For the 'subtropical' Pacific Islands (Fiji, Niue, Samoa, Tonga, and Vanuatu), preliminary modelling indicates that the more modest tuna resources that occur in their EEZs could increase? For these economies, adaptations centre on:

## B

On capitalizing on any significant opportunities. To implement effective adaptations for both categories of Pacific Island economies, more reliable information on the timing and extent of tuna redistribution is essential.

### The 34 PICs participating in the Programme have an extraordinary dependence on coral reef fish for food and on tuna for income generation. In addition to their economic importance, tuna are a critical source of protein for many people at severe risk from climate change. Given the extensive social and economic dependence of PICs on tuna, the GCF Regionally Significant Project (RSP) is designed to provide support in three key areas: 1) strengthening the resilience of regional significance because tuna are migratory fish species shared by PICs. Therefore, reliable information is needed for effective adaptation planning at both national and sub-national levels. This includes across all relevant jurisdictions, and from G20 to high-seas areas. The GCF RFP is also relevant at the national level because adaptive capacity of PICs varies widely due to differences in tuna species composition, variations in fishing gear contents, and the wide differences in population size and location of Pacific Island nations. These adaptations centre on three key areas: 1) strengthening the resilience of regional significance; 2) increasing the use of fish aggregating devices (FADs™); and increasing leveraged investment of tuna and bycatch transshipment vessels.

### The Programme supports the following goals of the Framework for Resilient Development in the Pacific: An Integrated Approach to Address Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management (FRDP), endorsed by Pacific Island Forum leaders in 2013. These include: 1) strengthening the resilience of PICs at national, sub-national, and local levels against managing risks caused by climate change within social and economic development planning processes; and 2) strengthening the capacity of PICs to manage the impacts of climate change on their economies.

### The Programme will also build resilience to climate change by supporting the following important management frameworks and tools that are prerequisites for the fisheries sector in the region (see Annex A4 for additional national climate plans and interventions):

- Western and Central Pacific Fisheries Commission (WCPFC) Resolution on Aspirations of Small Island Developing States (SIDS) to the WCPFC.
- The Commonwealth Resolution 2018-01 on Climate Change as it Relates to the WCPFC.
- Regional Roadmap for Sustainable Pacific Fisheries © designed to improve the sustainability of tuna resources, add value to tuna catches, increase employment, and provide better access to tuna for food availability in PICs. It also aims at reducing the environmental impact of fishing (e.g., bycatch and discards; nets to reefs) for tuna.
- A new Roadmap for Coastal Fisheries – Pathways for Change ©: an innovative regional approach to maintaining the benefits of small-scale fisheries in the face of declining coastal ecosystems and associated fish stocks.
- The Parties to the Nauru Agreement (PNA) Vesey Day Scheme that supplies 95% of tuna caught in the region.

### Nationally Determined Contributions: 10 of the 14 participating countries have included the need for adaptations to the effects of climate change on the ocean and coastal marine habitats in their NDCs ©.
- The Forum Fisheries Agency’s Climate Change Strategy adopted by the Forum Fisheries Committee in August 2013, which includes the implementation of a suite of industrial tuna fisheries assessed by FFA member countries.

The following text provides background information on coastal and tuna fisheries, their management, and the importance of fisheries as a basis for good nutrition in the region. See Annex B2, Chapter 1 for comprehensive background information.

# National and regional fisheries policies and institutional arrangements

Three organizations serve in pivotal roles in coordination of policy relating to coastal and oceanic fisheries and the provision of technical advice to support decision-making:

## The Pacific Islands Forum Fisheries Agency (FFA)

The FFA is a sub-regional intergovernmental organization for the Pacific island countries and a territory (it includes Australia, New Zealand, and Tokelau). The purpose of the FFA is to strengthen national capacity and regional cooperation in the management of fisheries resources. The FFA has 18 Member States and 2 Associate Members. Regional Fisheries Management Groups within FFA are the Parties to the Nauru Agreement (PNA), comprised of Federated States of Micronesia, Kiribati, Marshall Islands, Palau, Papua New Guinea, Solomon Islands, and Tuvalu.

## Pacific Islands Forum (PIF)

The PIF is a regional intergovernmental organization for the Pacific island countries. The majority of PIF Member states are parties to the Nauru Agreement. PIF has 21 member states and 3 associate members. PIF works together to cooperatively manage tuna fisheries within their EEZs, with the primary tools being the Nauru Agreement (PNA) and the Pacific Islands Forum Fisheries Agency (FFA). The Secretariat of the Community SPC has provided scientific and technical development support to 27 country and territory members since 1980. The PIF also provides a forum for countries to discuss regional fisheries issues, share information on fish stocks and healthy and manage their resources in a sustainable way. The organization's priorities are elaborated in its 2020-2030 Strategic Plan (SPC, Chapter 5). See A4: Additional information on FFA, PNA and SPC.

## The Secretariat of the Pacific Community (SPC)

The SPC convenes a Heads of Fisheries Meeting to discuss, for PIF Member States and Associate Members, issues related to fisheries. It is also the regional forum for discussion and priority-setting in fisheries management and policy, and for joint consideration of oceanic and coastal fisheries, where all SPC Members participate. HOF reports to SPC's governance bodies (the Pacific Islands Forum Council and the Pacific Islands Forum). The Secretariat in Port Vila provides strategic and policy support to the Regional Fisheries Minister’s Meeting.

# Coastal fisheries

Coastal fisheries are those that are resident within EEZs and are managed nationally without formal multilateral collaborative management. Regional cooperation in relation to coastal fisheries focuses on sharing information and lessons learned at the national level for application in fishery development and management situations in neighbouring countries, as well as through regional workshops and training.

Community-based resource management (CBRM) approaches are receiving increasing attention for the management of small-scale fisheries resources. These approaches have been promoted by non-governmental organizations (NGOs) across the region largely because of the predominance of traditional management systems, poor formal governance structures for coastal fisheries management and under-resourced central government institutions in many Pacific Island countries.

CBRM is more likely to be successful when it is based on a strong foundation of community support, supported through governance reforms undertaken by governments to regulate and manage resources. CBRM can also be effective where there are well-established local customary tenure systems. These institutions are more effective when resource users are themselves actively engaged in decision-making.

# Estimates

Thirty-eight estimates of harvests of coastal pelagic species in the WCPFC are approximately 4,000 t annually. Thirty-two estimates of harvests of oceanic pelagic species in the WCPFC are approximately 125,000 t annually. These estimates are based on limited data and do not include small-scale fisheries which make significant contributions to national economies which is largely undocumented. Efforts to estimate the value of small-scale fisheries in


---
**Page 17**
---

The WCPF indicate that they may be undervalued by a factor of five.* Historical catch and effort data are sparse but in some regions, catches may have been higher in the past than they are today, emphasizing the historical importance of these stocks for food security.

Most coastal fisheries are characterized by small-scale, relatively low cost, fishing methods. A considerable amount of fishing takes place from the shore or in shallow waters without the use of fishing vessels. Where vessels are used, they may be small and powered by outboard motors or diesel engines. These vessels (which we refer to as "small vessels") were used for commercial purposes (fishing for tuna, mackerel, sardines, anchovies, and other species) but also for subsistence fishing.

Tuna fisheries

The migratory nature of tunas in the WCPF means that fishers operate across many jurisdictions (national and high seas) and consequently, the regulatory framework is multi-layered. The Convention for the Conservation and Management of Straddling Fish Stocks and Highly Migratory Fish Stocks (CMSF) provides a global legal framework with international agreement supporting decision-making, which is adopted by consensus in the form of Conventions and Resolutions. The CMSF was replaced by the WCPF in 2014. The WCPF has developed its own rules, known as CMMs formally adopted by the WCPF throughout the Convention Area.* The Commission also adopts Resolution (Res) 30/2015 on the conservation and management of tuna stocks in the Pacific Ocean. This resolution ensures compatibility between measures adopted by the Commission and the national rules and regulations which apply in countries.

The WCPF ensures international cooperation on tuna fisheries. Since entering into force in 2004, acting on the advice of its subsidiary bodies,** the WCPF has added numerous CMMs. The primary CMM Pat regulates the management of purse seine and longline fisheries for skipjack tuna (Katsuwonus pelamis) and other species. Its predecessors, CMSM 23/2013 defines permitted activities in the purse seine and longline fisheries through provisions on catch and effort limits, timing of catch and fishing areas.

In relation to climate change, the WCPF has developed a model called SEADAPTION (see Box 4.1). The findings from the Pacific Climate Change Science Program (SCS) (5C11; SC12-03; and SC9-3) on the projected impacts of climate change on tuna distribution, larval numbers and stock biomasses, indicate that the WCPF needs to build resilience into its management strategies. The SCS also highlights the need for a more integrated approach. Article 30/2(2)c of the Convention requires the Commission to ensure there is no disproportionate burden of conservation action on developing States and Territories.

## Contribution of nearshore caught fish to local diets

### Availability of high-quality, subsistence production data across the region is patchy and as a result of limited sampling, estimates for per capita consumption by coastal rural populations range from 30–118 kg per person in Melanesia, 62–115 kg in Micronesia, and 50–140 kg in Polynesia. In urban areas, the average per capita fish consumption is lower than that of rural areas, but even in urban centres it is often lower than in rural areas; it usually still exceeds the global average of ~20 kg per person each year.

### Contribution to local diets

In 2013, estimates for the coastal artisanal and subsistence fisheries production were reviewed. On the basis that the total catch from these fisheries was estimated at 548,796 tonnes in 2013, this would equate to a per capita production of 13.8 kg annually was estimated for coast fisheries across 22 Pacific Island Countries and Territories (PICTs). This estimate is based on the assumption that the coastal artisanal and subsistence fisheries contribute significantly to PICT economies, livelihoods and dietary welfare which is of significant concern to PICCs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## Analysis of Household Income and Expenditure Survey (HIES) data also shows that fish provides 51-84% of animal protein in the diet in rural areas, and 27-63% in urban areas, across the region.### The importance of fish is reflected by the fact that 90% of Pacific islanders consume fish at least once a week, or who grow tilapia in ponds.

#### In six of the 14 participating countries (Kiribati, Nauru, PNG, Samoa, Solomon Islands and Vanuatu), coastal fisheries are expected to be able to meet the future demand for fish and the gap between the fish required and the fish expected to be available from coastal fisheries will be substantial. In another five of the participating countries, the gap is projected to increase over time. The remaining three countries (Fiji, Papua New Guinea and Tuvalu) are expected to have a large gap in the future.

#### Present and emerging gaps in fish supply and the potential for tuna to fill this gap.

##### It is essential that new sources of fish are utilised to meet the growing need for high-quality dietary protein in most PIFS. An important opportunity is the allocation of a relatively small proportion of the rich tuna resources of the tropical Pacific to coastal fisheries, which would provide an additional source of food and income for many people living on or near the coast.

##### In relative terms, the percentage of the region’s tuna catch needed from 2035 to fill the gap in domestic fish supply is small, i.e., <6%. The greatest quantities of tuna will be required in PNG, Solomon Islands, and Kiribati (See Annex 1).

##### The RTP will implement two approaches to utilise tuna to fill this gap in fish supply. The first is to increase opportunities for coastal fisheries to access oceanic pelagic fish resources through the deployment and management of appropriate fishing gear and equipment, including by improving access to tuna and bycatch transhipped or utilised by industrial fishing vessels in Pacific ports.

### Related initiatives in the region

##### In preparation for the programme, regional and national initiatives were surveyed, and projects related to fisheries management and development implemented or were in planning stages of relevance to the GCF Programme. The projects reviewed were a balance of single-country (bilaterals), groupings of between 2 – 7 PIFS, and regional arrangements and multi-lateral initiatives. Regional arrangements included participation by the majority of the membership of SPC and FFA.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 17

## B

### 8.2 (a) Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

#### Goal statement

At its highest level, the Theory of Change (ToC) presents a statement of the overall goal and intended impact of the Regional Tuna Programme, as follows:

**IFP Island governments are supported to implement effective programmes for assisting coastal communities in adapting to climate change more broadly, and are provided with improved information on climate change-driven redistribution of tuna.**

**Then Pacific Island nations will be transformed to become more resilient to key climate change threats facing the fisheries sector:**

* **REGULARLY updated IFP island governments will be better informed and equipped to make optimal use of the fisheries resources on which they depend for food, livelihoods, and economic development.**

#### 54. The Programme is designed to overcome the barriers identified below to bring about a paradigm shift in the way tuna stocks, the most valuable food resource across the vast Pacific region, are assessed, managed, and utilized:

* **(1) Lack of information on climate change-driven redistribution of tuna:**
    * This results from a lack of understanding by IFP island governments of the impacts of climate change on tuna migration patterns.
    * The Programme will provide IFP island governments with improved information on climate change-driven redistribution of tuna, thereby enabling them to better adapt their fisheries management practices.

* **(2) Lack of capacity for adaptation:**
    * This results from a lack of understanding by IFP island governments and coastal communities in the region about how to adapt to climate change.
    * The Programme will provide IFP island governments with improved information on climate change-driven redistribution of tuna, thereby enabling them to better adapt their fisheries management practices.

* **(3) Lack of capacity for adaptation:**
    * This results from a lack of understanding by IFP island governments and coastal communities in the region about how to adapt to climate change.
    * The Programme will provide IFP island governments with improved information on climate change-driven redistribution of tuna, thereby enabling them to better adapt their fisheries management practices.

#### Structure of the Programme

The Programme is designed to be implemented within two broad components:

* **Component A - Adaptation to harness tuna to improve food availability of Pacific Island communities as coral reefs are degraded by climate change:**
    * This component includes sets of activities designed (1) to strengthen and invest in existing fisheries management institutions, including the development of a network of nearshore, anchored FADs to enable fishers in coastal communities to catch and process tuna and other pelagic species; (2) to provide training for IFP island governments on how to adapt their fisheries management practices to climate change; (3) to support the development of new livelihood opportunities as populations grow and human populations expand; and (4) to design and implement tuna transport mechanisms that will provide urban and peri-urban communities with greater access to bycatch from industrial fishing fleets for domestic food availability.

* **Component B - Adaptations to reduce risks to Pacific Island economies from climate-driven tuna redistribution:**
    * This component includes a set of activities to establish a region-wide Advanced Warning System (AWS) to gather


---
**Page 21**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 18

B

and analysis of data needed to determine future abundance and distribution of tuna more accurately as stocks respond to ocean warming due to climate change. The evidence generated will support the preparation and negotiation of a new tuna conservation, development and management (TCDM) plan for the Pacific region.

The TCDM plan will address the need to shift tuna stocks and associated economic benefits regardless of the redistribution of the fish. The data from this research will also be used by the Pacific Community (SPC) and other regional fisheries bodies in the Pacific to continue ensured effective management of tuna stocks and industrial fishing under impacts of climate change.

### 57. While Components A and B address different critical impacts of climate change on fisheries in the Pacific, they are interdependent and need to be considered together. The TCDM plan will provide a framework for the design of transport operations to provide additional tuna to urban communities under Component A, and the AWS will support the development of new fishing grounds under Component B.

The TuC (Figure 8) illustrates the logical flow between programme activities and outputs, and how these will lead towards achieving the overall objective of the Programme. The TCDM plan is a critical element in this process as it will bring about greater resilience to climate change threats, which is the overarching goal of the Programme.

### Risks, barriers, and assumptions

Before assessing the risks and barriers for the Programme actions in the results chain, it is necessary to consider the risks, barriers and assumptions that have been identified, and the implications for Programme design and implementation. Risks and Assumptions are external factors over which the Programme has little control, but which may impact on its ability to deliver its objectives and results. Barriers are the obstacles; the GCF RSP is designed to address to achieve its objectives and targets.

### 61. A list of all uncertainties that have a negative impact in the design and implementation of the Programme:

- Natural disasters (R3) and climate change disrupt regular economic, social or institutional functions (R1).
- Global or national economic or political situation becomes unstable (R2).
- Large-scale climate driven natural disasters significantly disrupt tuna fisheries (R3).
- Weather patterns become more extreme and unpredictable (R4). Management of these risks leads to increased uncertainties in managing redistributed tuna (R4).

### 61. The actions to be undertaken as part of the Programme are designed to overcome identified barriers:

(i) To increase access to and acceptance of tuna to increase food availability in coastal and urban communities; and (ii) those that prevent governments from better managing tuna stocks and industrial fishing under impacts of climate change.

#### 1. Barriers to use of tuna to improve food availability

Costa Rican communities have not been able to access sufficient tuna to offset the effects of climate change-induced coral reef degradation and population growth on per capita fish consumption. The barriers are (i) lack of awareness among coastal communities about the benefits and capacity to scale-up the use of the nearshore FAIs that are designed to help coastal communities catch tuna; (ii) lack of access to markets for tuna products in urban areas; (iii) lack of knowledge on how to use the TCDM Programme; provide training in safe and effective FAD-fishing methods and post-harvest processing - ensure that existing fishers have the skills, capacity and resources needed to participate effectively in the conservation and recovery plans for small-scale fishing communities (Annex 26, Technical Study 4); crop-life natural FADs - use of FADs to catch tuna and pelagic fish; (v) lack of knowledge on how to use FAIs (e.g., by using a net or smoking) to increase the storage life of FAIs caught tuna and pelagic fish. Women have a primary role in the post-harvest processing of tuna, but they are not involved in decision-making processes related to tuna fishing and smocking; to increase the storage life of FAIs caught tuna and pelagic fish.

#### 2. Barriers to improved management of tuna stocks

The barriers are (i) lack of awareness among coastal communities about the benefits and capacity to scale-up the use of the nearshore FAIs that are designed to help coastal communities catch tuna; (ii) lack of access to markets for tuna products in urban areas; (iii) lack of knowledge on how to use the TCDM Programme - provide training in safe and effective FAD-fishing methods and post-harvest processing - ensure that existing fishers have the skills, capacity and resources needed to participate effectively in the conservation and recovery plans for small-scale fishing communities (Annex 26, Technical Study 4); crop-life natural FADs - use of FADs to catch tuna and pelagic fish; (v) lack of knowledge on how to use FAIs (e.g., by using a net or smoking) to increase the storage life of FAIs caught tuna and pelagic fish. Women have a primary role in the post-harvest processing of tuna, but they are not involved in decision-making processes related to tuna fishing and smocking; to increase the storage life of FAIs caught tuna and pelagic fish.

#### 62. Second, despite existing regulations mandating that transshipment of tuna catches caught by industrial purse-seine fleets must occur in Pacific Island ports, many governments have not been able to invest in supply chains to deliver high-quality products to markets in urban areas. This is a barrier to the development and implementation of women's leadership and management skills for women and contribute to empowerment of women in the local fishery sector (see Annex 26).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 19

## Future risks of fishing activity: Patterns of transhipment in regional ports are already affected by the ENSO Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and will be intensified by climate change. Unless reliable information on the volume, timing, and location of tuna landings is available, it will not be possible to ensure that transhipping facilities need to distribute tuna to urban communities effectively. In addition, local utilization of tuna and other fish species in coastal areas may be reduced as a result of increased competition for resources between coastal and urban-periurban markets. As for FAD-caught fish, women play a prominent role in processing and distribution of the catch.

### 63. Third, there is a lack of awareness among both coastal and urban communities about the immediate climate change impacts on tuna landings and prices. This means that they need to diversify their income sources including more tuna for sale to ensure nutritious food availability.

### Barriers to national economic security: The governments of tuna-importing Pacific Island Countries are heavily dependent on imports of fish from other tuna EECs (high-seas areas) for the livelihoods and well-being of their economies. However, inadequate information for governments about the timing and extent of tuna movements is a fundamental barrier to ensuring that these countries can continue to meet their obligations under the WCPFC. This limits the ability of regional management bodies (e.g., WCPFC) to continue to sustainably manage the catch of individual species in the region. The lack of information also means that there are no effective measures for fishers caught in their waters. For example, the existing fishing restrictions designed to contain operation of purse-seine vessels in certain areas and times may not be sufficient if tuna movements change as a result of climate change. Such changes could occur in international waters. To ensure that the effects of climate change are increasingly integrated into regional fisheries management strategies, it is essential for WCPFC member countries to adopt the recommendations of a Climate Change Resolution adopted by the WCPFC in 2019 (Annex B, Chapter 1). However, measures to secure the future of tuna for PICs under a changing climate remain to be identified and implemented. For example, it is essential that the WCPFC and its member countries adopt the recommendations of the Agreement on the Conservation and Management of Highly Migratory Fish Stocks in the Western and Central Pacific Ocean, which will require more accurate information about the timing and extent of tuna movements as well as the development of new fishing technologies. The fisheries that have been driven from PICs EECs to the high seas by ocean warming will require more accurate information on the timing and extent of tuna movements.

### 64. The subregional TPCs are also expected to benefit from development of the information system described above, as well as from the implementation of the recommendations in the WCPFC Climate Change Resolution (Annex B) to evaluate adaptations to capitalize on opportunities, e.g., increased industrial fishing and tuna processing. To provide a more complete picture of the potential impacts of climate change on tuna landings and prices, however, it is essential that TPCs also consider the following: The key weaknesses in existing modeling approaches need to be addressed. Essential improvements to models include the development of better methods for predicting tuna movements (e.g., improved integration of information on tuna connectivity, tuna stock structures and meso-scale oceanography), improved assessment of climate change impacts on tuna landings and prices, and improved understanding of potential forings for all GMD emission scenarios. Further details of barriers preventing adaptations to secure the necessary contributions from sea to food security in PICs are provided in Annex B, Chapter 4.

68. Of the assumptions listed above, the first two reflect large-scale events which could potentially affect the implementation of the Programme. The latter eight are more narrowly focused and Programme-specific, with more direct relevance to the activities described below.

The Programme will monitor these assumptions and implement adaptive management as necessary. Programme activities will be designed in such a way that they can be easily adapted if assumptions change or new information is obtained on acting on them, developing and implementing actions to overcome challenges and barriers toward improving and achieving the Programme’s objectives.

**Results Chain: activities to deliver programme outputs and outcomes:**

* **Component A:** Adaptations to harness tuna to improve food availability of Pacific Island communities as coral reefs are degraded by climate change.
    * The purpose of this component is to develop adaptive measures to better harness the potential of tuna to improve food availability for communities across the Pacific island region.
    * The first set of activities under Component A is aimed at strengthening national programmes to expand the use of FADs to increase catches and reduce costs. This will be achieved by providing technical and logistical support, increasing the capacity of women to extend the shelf-life of catches through training in post-harvest methods. The beneficiaries for this set of activities are residents of coastal communities across all 14 participating PICs.
    * These activities are:
        + Implement technical and logistical support to strengthen National FAD programmes (Activity 1.1).
        + Augment the capacity of women to extend shelf-life of tuna catches through training in post-harvest methods (Activity 1.2).
        + Strengthen post-harvest practices and improve market opportunities for FAQ-caught fish (Activity 1.3).

    * Successful implementation of this set of activities leads to achievement of Programme Output 1: Increased nutritional security, food availability and income generation in coastal communities across all PICs through increased empowerment and awareness of women in the FAD priority value chain through dedicated training and capacity building.

* A second set of activities under Component A is also designed to provide greater access to tuna and other pelagic fish for urban and peri-urban populations. This will be achieved by providing technical support, training, and equipment during transshipping and unloading operations. Because transhipment and unloading ports are in larger population centers, this activity set will increase the availability of fish to urban and peri-urban populations. This set of activities include:
    + Implement strategies to deliver more transshipped and unloaded bycatch and tuna to urban/peri-urban communities (Activity 2.1).

## B

### 71. Set of activities will result in achievement of Output 2: Increased supply of bycatch and tuna from industrial fishing vessels (Activity 2.2)

### 72. Taken collectively, the two sets of activities implemented as part of Component A are designed to ensure that coastal communities and urban / peri-urban communities have better access to tuna and other fish species which help in their livelihoods.

## Climate Change Adaptations to reduce risks to Pacific Island economies from climate-driven tuna redistribution

### 74. Current modeling and projections indicate that the effects of climate change on sea surface temperatures can cause a significant reduction in catches of tuna stocks across the Pacific. As a result, these stocks will be found less frequently, and in reduced numbers, within the EEZs of the Western Pacific nations where they currently occur.

### 75. Negative impacts will be felt by Western Pacific nations whose government revenues are dependent on tuna being caught within their EEZs.

### 76. Recent advances in close-in mark-recapture fisheries assessment tools™ are expected to deliver a more accurate understanding of the status and spatial structure of tuna stocks in the WCPF. When this information is combined with climate change projections, coupled with the development of an advanced warning system (AWS) for modeling, it will provide the foundation for creating an Advanced Warning System (AWS) to forecast climate-induced changes in the distribution of tuna biomass among the EEZs of PIFCs and between EEZs and high-seas areas.

### 77. Creation of the AWS will depend in part upon genetic analysis of tuna tissue samples collected across the WCPF during a series of research cruises. The development of the AWS will also require that the necessary scientific information for conducting the sampling to gather the necessary scientific information be realized (Annex 26, Technical Bulletin).

### 78. Data gathered through the AWS will be disseminated through reports, notifications, and advisories, which will be issued to the target countries at regular intervals. Scientific input on the design and implementation of the AWS will be sought from the WCPF Commission and its Working Groups. The AWS will also provide a basis for the Reports of the Committee. AWS data will also be continued for strong management of tuna fisheries by the WCPF in the context of climate change.

### 79. The AWS will provide better information on the extent of negative impacts to tuna-dependent Pacific Island economies from tuna redistribution and the need to develop international legal arrangements to retain the historical level of catches within their EEZs.

### 80. Successful negotiations based on the AWS will depend upon high-level country and regional representatives having a clear understanding of how the AWS can be used to develop legal arrangements to retain historical socio-economic benefits from tuna stocks in their EEZs. The AWS will also provide a basis for the development of several targeted training modules, plus provision of technical and legal support for the drafting of multilateral agreements.

### 81. The activities included within Component B are:
- Develop and deliver an Advanced Warning System (AWS) for tuna redistribution (Activity 3.1).
- Assess the impact of tuna biomass redistribution identified by the AWS on national economies at all levels (Activity 3.2).
- Provide AWS-related training to national institutions to equip them to engage in regional and international negotiations to retain historical socio-economic benefits from tuna stocks within their EEZs and high-seas areas (Activity 3.3).

B


The measures needed to enable the tuna-dependent economies to the socio-economic benefits previously derived from their tuna resources, regardless of climate-driven redistribution of the fish (Activity 3.3).

81. Successful implementation of these activities will lead to achievement of Output 3: science-based forecasts and projections of tuna stocks and other ecosystem indicators in the Pacific region (Output 3.3.1). Achievement of this Output will lead to Outcome 2: strengthened capacity of tuna-dependent Pacific Island nations to benefit from benefits from tuna stocks which are redistributed as a result of climate change.

**Results Chain: co-benefit**

82. Co-benefits are important additional or ancillary benefits that are expected to result from successful implementation of the Programme's activities. In addition to the two-outcomes described above, it is expected that two co-benefits will be achieved:

- **Co-benefit 1:** Improved livelihoods of vulnerable communities in participating countries. *Beneficiaries of improved livelihoods will be fish processors and transmigrant vendors.* This co-benefit is aligned with GCF Adaptation Results Area ARA: More resilient coastal communities.

- **Co-benefit 2:** Strengthened management of industrial tuna fisheries by regional and national institutions. The data generated by the AWS will inform the development of future WCPFC CMAFs and improve the ability of regional and national institutions to manage tuna stocks in a manner that is consistent with climate change.* This co-benefit is aligned with GCF Adaptation Results Area ARA 4: Ecosystems and Ecosystem Services.

**Achieving paradigm shift**

83. As illustrated in the ToC diagram (Figure 3, below), at the highest level, all outcomes and co-benefits will contribute to the overarching goal of the Programme, supporting the achievement of a paradigm shift where PICs are transitioning from climate vulnerability to climate resilience.

84. In climate adaptation programmes, the overarching impact and paradigm shift may not be realized during the life of the programme. The paradigm shift is expected to be achieved when the enabling environment has been deployed over a longer timeframe than the Programme implementation period. To achieve paradigm shift, the Programme must establish the enabling environment that is needed to achieve the transformation to improved climate resilience.

- Output 2: Increased fiscal availability for vulnerable countries

- Barrier 1: Limited access to finance for climate change adaptation and mitigation programs
- Barrier 2: Lack of political will or commitment from national governments
- Barrier 3: Insufficient technical capacity at the national level
- Barrier 4: Inadequate institutional arrangements in developing countries

- Assumption 1: Developed countries are willing to provide financial support for climate change adaptation and mitigation programs

- Assumption 3: There is sufficient technical capacity at the national level to implement climate change adaptation and mitigation programs
- Assumption 4: Institutional arrangements in developing countries support effective implementation of climate change adaptation and mitigation programs

B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

Programmes are designed to fit within the objectives and outcomes of the GCF. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 25

## Activity 1: Provide technical and logistical support, including equipment, to implement National FAD programmes.

85. Expanding the use of anchored, artisanal fish aggregating devices (FADs) will provide the infrastructure needed for increased access to tuna for local required for food availability (access to high-quality food/nutrition security) of vulnerable coastal communities (Annex 26, Technical Study 2), due to degradation of coral reefs and projections of sea level rise. The use of FADs in small-scale fisheries in the Pacific region, particularly in Pacific island countries will provide a greater number of small-scale fishers with the opportunity to catch tuna for their own consumption or sale. A total of 14 sub-activities are proposed under this activity. Each sub-activity is described below and summarized in Table 3, Annex 26.

86. The 14 participating countries, more than 300 FADs will be deployed and routinely phased as needed under the following sub-activities:

### Sub-Activity 1.1: Audit progress towards requirements for scaling-up National FAD Programmes in the 14 participating countries

87. The sub-activities of Activity 1 are detailed in Annex D2, Chapter 4. SPC will conduct these sub-activities with technical assistance from contracted consultants or services providers, and SPC will provide safeguards to national governments to support the participation of national fisheries agencies in this Activity.

The sub-activities under Activity 1.1 are:

* **Sub-activity 1.1.a:** Audit progress towards requirements for scaling-up National FAD Programmes in the 14 participating countries.

## Sub-activity 1.2: Develop workplans for scaling-up National FAD Programmes based on the audit.

### Sub-activity 1.1c: Revise national policies and regulations to identify barriers to the equitable and sustainable use of FADs.
### Sub-activity 1.1d: Design and implement capacity development activities to augment the skills of national staff in implementing FAD programmes.
### Sub-activity 1.1e: Design and implement a gender-responsive consultative stakeholder engagement strategy for national fisheries agencies to utilise in consultations with communities to identify suitable FAD deployment sites.

#### Sub-activity 1.1f: Procure materials to expand and maintain national FAD programmes.
#### Sub-activity 1.1g: Enable small-scale fishers to catch tuna and other pelagic fish around FADs that integrate traditional knowledge.
#### Sub-activity 1.1h: Improve the capacity of national FAD programmes to effectively collect and utilize catch data.

### Activity 1.2: Augment national safety-sea initiatives

88. The Programme will emphasize safety at sea, because once FADs are deployed a considerable distance from shore, small-scale fishers may be exposed to conditions that could lead to fatalities or accidents due to fishing within lagoons and relatively sheltered inshore coastal waters. This Activity will ensure that the risks for small-scale fishers using FADs can be minimized by providing them with improved vessel safety designs and practical options for evaluating the suitability of small vessels currently used for fishing around FADs and providing governments with improved vessel designs, and practical options for regulating and promoting construction/procurement of such vessels. This will include:

- Establishing enabling conditions for national meteorological services to provide improved weather forecasts and early warnings to assist small-scale fishers.
- Supporting procurement and delivery of boat safety equipment linked to vessel registration where practical.

The sub-activities included in Activity 1.2 are:
#### Sub-activity 1.2a: Conduct a needs analysis for improved vessel safety for small-scale fishers using FADs.
#### Sub-activity 1.2b: Establish the enabling conditions for national meteorological services to provide improved weather forecasts and early warnings to assist small-scale fishers.

#### Sub-activity 1.2c: Support procurement and delivery of boat safety equipment, linked to vessel registration where practical.

### Activity 3.1: Strengthen post-harvest practices and improve market opportunities for FAD-caught fish.
89. This Activity will provide training and support to coastal community representatives in post-harvest methods for tuna and associated species caught around FADs, and to develop supply chains and market opportunities for the sale of these products. The training will be provided by national fisheries agencies and/or local communities catching tuna and other pelagic fish for subsistence consumption. Design of the training programme will include traditional knowledge among small-scale fishers in the use of FADs. National fisheries agencies and MIMES receiving tuna from FADS, SPC, with support from subcontracted consultants or services providers, will conduct training on a variety of processing methods, including traditional knowledge among

approaches, for example, increasing the supply of ice, drying, smoking, salting, pickling and canning and be tailored to meet the specific needs and conditions prevalent in the target communities. The training will be developed with input from local stakeholders and will include information on best practices for improving the roles of men and women in beneficiary communities. Officials from the National Fisheries Agencies will be trained on how to implement the GCF-funded project, including the use of cold storage facilities and MSMEs in post-harvest processing. National Fisheries Agencies will provide GCF-funded post-harvest equipment from SPC to priority communities via Programme subgrants, in compliance with GCF requirements.

The four sub-activities under Activity 1.3 are:

* **Sub-activity 1.3.a:** Provide training for subsistence households and MSMEs to improve preservation of FAO-caught fish using post-harvest methods, e.g., drying, smoking, and bottling, incorporating traditional cooking practices.
* **Sub-activity 1.3.b:** Provide coastal communities with basic equipment to apply post-harvest methods, including practical options for cold storage where appropriate.
* **Sub-activity 1.3.c:** Identify and promote market opportunities for FAO-caught fish.
* **Sub-activity 1.3.d:** Conduct communication campaigns to raise awareness of coastal communities about the impacts of climate change on coral reef fish and the need to consume more FAO-caught tuna for community food availability.

Output 2: Increased supply of bycatch and tuna from industrial fishing operations for urban and peri-urban communities.

**Activity 2.1:** Implement strategies to deliver more transhipped and unlanded bycatch and tuna to urban and peri-urban communities.

90. This activity is designed to identify and address weaknesses in the supply chain for fish that come ashore from industrial tuna-fishing vessels while they are in port. These fish can provide urban and peri-urban communities with a reliable source of fresh, high-quality fish at affordable prices if they can be delivered throughout delivery and sale. These fish include bycatch caught by purse-seine fishing which are not discarded but rather sold to local markets or exported as frozen products. The supply chain for these fish is separated from the high-quality tuna when these vessels tranship their catch to carrier vessels in regional ports where the fish are then landed, processed, and either re-exported or distributed to port. The main transhipping ports where this Activity will be implemented are in FSM, Kiribati, Marshall Islands, Solomon Islands, and Tuvalu (PNG; see below [Annex 26. Technical Study 5]).

91. The opportunity for trade in bycatch from purse-seine vessels has arisen with WCPFC's requirement that transhipping vessels must deliver all bycatch to the carrier vessel or port of call within 7 days, which can meet fish consumption needs of rapidly growing urban populations in some countries, e.g., Solomon Islands and PNG. The supply chain for these fish is separated from high-quality tuna when these vessels tranship their catch to carrier vessels in regional ports where the fish are then landed, processed, and either re-exported or distributed to port. This activity provides the opportunity to improve food availability indirectly through use as a major ingredient in feeds for chickens and pigs and directly by improving the quality of such for market gardening in atoll nations. At all cost, ensuring that the volume of bycatch available and tuna on sale will deliver significant socio-economic benefits to urban communities.

92. The sub-activities in Activity 2.1 will identify and address weaknesses in the supply chain for bycatch and tuna to urban and peri-urban communities. This activity is also effective where it is delivered to carrier vessels in Lae, Madang, and Weewak. However, it is already distributed effectively to nearby areas and the volume of bycatch available and tuna on sale will deliver significant socio-economic benefits to urban communities.

Programme is not planning to include PNG in Activity 2.1.1 now; however, to be included in Activity 2.2 FFA will support the implementation of this Activity. SPC will enter into a grant agreement with FFA and CSIRO to support implementation of this activity, and sub-contracted service providers (specialising in marine food

## Sub-activities included in Activity 2.1 are:

### Sub-activity 2.1.a: Ensure the supply of bycatch and tuna available for offloading at transhipping and unloading operations.

### Sub-activity 2.1.b: Evaluate the projected shortfall in the supply of fish needed for nutritional food availability in urban and peri-urban communities by 2030 and in following decades.

### Sub-activity 2.1.c: Use the Advanced Warning System (AWS) (see Activity 3.1.1 below) to assess the implications of potential substitution for transhipping and unloading activities across the region.

### Sub-activity 2.1.d: Build a database of bycatch and tuna species with analysis on current and future transhipment and unloading of bycatch and tuna.

### Sub-activity 2.1.e: Develop procedures and regulations to increase availability of transhipped and unloaded bycatch and tuna available for the gap in food supply.

## Activity 2.2: Strengthen/develop post-harvest practices and improve market opportunities to distribute bycatch and tuna from transhipping and unloading operations to urban/peri-urban communities.

This Activity will provide training and support in post-harvest methods, and expanding markets for bycatch and tuna from transhipping and unloading operations. This activity will also develop and implement measures to extend the shelf life of the fish, and add value to bycatch and tuna sold to MSMEs. This activity differs from Activity 2.1 because it focuses on post-harvest processing (as opposed to pre-landing or pre-harvest processing) and access to infrastructure and essential services available to MSMEs in urban areas.

### Training design will commence with a needs analysis to determine the needs of the MSMEs within the region, including their capacity for post-harvest processing. This activity will provide training on post-harvest processing methods required by MSMEs and provide some of the equipment they need to process fish. The different roles played by men and women in post-harvest processing activities will be taken into consideration when designing the training programme, with a focus on ensuring that it is delivered in an inclusive manner.

The range of post-harvest methods available for bycatch offloaded from purse-seine vessels during transhipping or unloading operations includes: (i) freezing; (ii) salting; and (iii) brining. Salting and brining are the most common methods used to preserve fish, because the fish from purse-seine vessels will have been stored in brine.

### Activity 2.2 includes four sub-activities expected to increase demand for bycatch and tuna from transhipping and unloading operations: (i) a market research component; (ii) an awareness-raising campaign on the benefits of bycatch and tuna (Annex B, Technical Study 6); and providing governments with fish market information. The fourth sub-activity will be to provide training for MSMEs in urban areas on how they can sell the fish which are sold to FFA’s Fisheries Development Division, under technical direction of SPC, will support the development of markets for bycatch and tuna from transhipping and unloading operations with introductions to local networks and responsible government agencies, including market authorities.

## The sub-activities included in Activity 2.2 are:

### Sub-activity 2.2.a: Provide training for urban communities to improve/develop post-harvest processing techniques for bycatch and tuna from transhipping and unloading operations.

## Component B:

Output 3: Improved forecasts and projections for climate-driven tuna redistribution which facilitate effective adaptations for all stakeholders.

### Activity 3.1: Develop and deliver an Advanced Warning System (AWS) for climate-driven tuna redistribution.

This AWS will deliver an Advance Warning System (AWS) on tuna redistribution using the latest in data analysis, forecasting, and communication technologies to provide early warning of changes in tuna distribution patterns. The AWS will be a flexible tool for countries to develop practical adaptations that protect their economies from disruptions due to increased competition with other fisheries or reduced access to tuna biomass away from their EEZs due to climate change (see Activities 3.2 and 3.3).

Significant co-benefits can also be realized through the AWS, including long-term sustainability of Pacific tuna stocks and oceanic biodiversity through improved stock assessments and GMAs adopted by the WCPFC and the VMC. The AWS will be developed in collaboration with key partners such as CSIRO and FFA and sub-contracted consultants and service providers (PMAD and other relevant institutions) to ensure that the AWS is robust, reliable, and sustainable over time.

### Sub-activity 3.1a: Transition existing fisheries and ocean monitoring systems to produce higher-resolution forecasts of climate-driven tuna biomass redistribution.

### Sub-activity 3.1b: Establish baselines and indicators for quantification of climate change impacts on distribution of tuna biomass.

### Sub-activity 3.1c: Develop and collect a suite of physical oceanography and micronutrient data to inform the development of climate-driven redistribution of tuna biomass models.

### Activity 3.2: Assess and socialize the impact of tuna biomass redistribution identified by the AWS on national economies at all scales.

To use the advanced Fisheries and Conservation Management Measures (CFMMs) that secure national and regional benefits derived from the tuna industry, this Activity will develop and couple a fleet dynamics model of the Pacific tuna fishery with an AWS to simulate short-term and long-term changes in the productivity and locations of Pacific tuna fisheries under future climate variability and climate change scenarios and model associated economic impacts. This information will be used to inform policy makers about the need for early warning systems, including an advance warning on the implications of impending climate extreme events and long-term guidance on the impacts of climate-driven tuna redistribution for Pacific Island economies. The economic and fleet dynamics modeling,

The 15th Pacific Community Heads of Fisheries meeting (HOF) stipulated that the GCF programme have strong country-led governance (SCG), the sole Executive Entity represents the interests and priorities of its Member States in all aspects of the Programme implementation, including financial management and reporting section**. To ensure responsibilities are clearly understood during Programme implementation, HOF15 requested the SCG to develop a clear accountability framework for the GCF programme and its implementing Programme activities. This is consistent with GCF's practices of segregating the roles of AE and EE and ensuring that the EE has final approval authority over any project-related decision or decision adopted by the Programme’s governance bodies.

### Accredited Entity

1. Conservation International Foundation (CIF), through its CIF Agency, will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Programme. The CIF GCF Agency will be responsible for oversight of this Programme as defined in the Agreement and any other relevant documents. This includes providing overall strategic direction; administrative monitoring and supervision (through reporting, audits, and periodic site visits); and review and approval of project proposals, implementation plans, and financial statements. CIF GCF Agency will also provide support and guidance to the EE as mutually agreed upon; monitoring the achievement of Programme results and Outputs; reporting to the GCF; and Programme closure and evaluation. ## B

### Executive Entity (EE)

The Green Climate Fund Programme Unit (GCFPU) will be the Executing Entity (EE) for all activities for the Programmes. GCFPU will be responsible for execution of the Programme, management and monitoring of sub-award agreements with sub-awardees partner organisations, Programme countries, and service providers receiving GCF funds and their associated financial reporting. The GCFPU will also ensure that the programmes are implemented in a manner to optimise alignment with national policies and contributions to achieve programme outcomes, GCF goals and priorities.

### 3.03.1 SPIC

SPIC is an international intergovernmental organisation established by treaty (Canberra Agreement) in 1947. SPIC is governed by its 27 member countries to service the development, scientific and technical needs of the Pacific region. The SPIC Secretariat is located in Suva, Fiji. SPIC has a long history of working with GCF and will participate in this Programme. SPIC's headquarters is located in Noumea, New Caledonia, and it has regional offices in Suva (Fiji), Port Moresby (Papua New Guinea) and Honiara (Solomon Islands). SPIC works across more than 20 sectors, addressing sustainable economic development, sustainable natural resource use and environmental protection. SPIC is a GCFSP-registered implementing partner for the following programmes:

* **Pacific Women Lead programme through SPIC's Human Rights and Social Development (HRSD) division:** AUD$58 million; 2021–2036; 10 PC(Ts)
* **Climate Change Flagging & Climate Science for ensuring Pacific Tuna Access:** USD 30 million; 2022-2027
* **Pacific European Union Marine Partnership Programme:** USD50 million, 2018–2025; for 14 PC(Ts)
SPIC's Fisheries, Aquaculture and Marine Ecosystem (FAME) division has been delivering fisheries, aquaculture and marine ecosystem management services to the Pacific region since 1973. The fisheries and aquaculture resources of the Pacific region are resilient and managed sustainably for economic growth while ensuring food security and environmental protection. SPIC is a GCFSP-registered implementing partner.

### 3.04.1 GCFPU will execute the Programme in line with its policies, procedures and GCF requirements when using grant funds. Where appropriate, GCFPU will also enter into a variety of GCF-compliant legal arrangements for this Programme. These may include agreements with other entities to meet specific GCFSP or GCF requirements with organisations serving as Implementing Partners (IPs) for the Programme (see details below).

### 3.05.1 SPIC's execution of this Programme will be led by a FAME Division, which led design of the Programme. The FAME Division will provide technical and operational support to the GCFPU. The FAME Division will have full-time Programme staff and will be supported by technical expertise from FAME's Coastal Fisheries and Aquaculture (CFA) and Marine and Ocean Fisheries Programmes (OPP). FAME's current programming budget is approximately AUD$30 million.

### 3.06.1 GCFPU will establish and host a central Programme Management Unit (PMU) as well as other full-time Programme staff to support the execution of this Programme. The PMU will be led by a Programme Director, who will be responsible for Programme delivery and coordination with all stakeholders. This responsibility is shared equally between two GCFSP-Dependent Directors, who each lead such functions on direction and management for one Component, as well as technical and operational staff dedicated full-time to the Programme.

### 3.07.1 The PMU will be responsible for executing the Programmes and all of its components. PMU will be responsible for, inter alia:

* Coordinating, liaising with, and providing secretarial services to the Programme Steering Committee
* Liaising with and managing relationships with implementing partners
* Managing progress and financial reporting (including on co-financing) to the AE and GCF

The EE has final authority regarding the usage of the GCF Proceeds; SPC, as the Executing Entity, has the final authority in decisions regarding the implementation of all activities as well as the use of the GCF Proceeds.

**Co-funding Partners**

**15.ND0** and private-sector industrial fishing companies will provide in-kind co-financing support to the Advanced Warning Systems activity in Activity 3.1 by providing observers, vessel time, and data. SPC will enter into a Letter of Agreement with each party for the provision of such services. The EE may enter into Letters of Agreement without funding from each entity. The EE and both Technical Partner Grantees shall be responsible for all costs (staff time and goods & equipment) as well.

**Other Implementing Partners**

* **15.PC0:** SPC will also engage with relevant regional or technical organisations or entities for technical guidance or other collaboration, but which will not receive any GCF funds or resources. Examples include Pacific Community (SPC), the University of the South Pacific, and the Secretariat of the Pacific Regional Environment Programme (SPREP). SPC may enter into Letters of Agreement with such entities, with no transfer of funds; to define respective roles and responsibilities.

* **15.PC1:** SPC and Participating SPC Member Grantees will work extensively with local beneficiary communities in project implementation, including by providing training, particularly in Output 1. As part of this training, beneficiaries may be provided with goods and services (e.g., equipment, tools) to support their activities related to the beneficiary communities. Transfer of any such goods will be documented by SPC’s Assets External Transfer Form (AETF). Such goods will not be considered as GCF-funded assets unless they are transferred on terms or use of GCF-funded goods. Disposal and/or handling of all durable assets acquired with GCF proceeds must be managed through a disposal plan consistent with GCF policy and AMA clause 23.03.

**Programme Governance**

* **15.C:** The 14 PCs will engage in the Programme through their designated national fisheries agencies (ministries, departments, or statutory authorities). GCF NDAs will ensure that activities implemented by the Programme align with national priorities and policies. SPC will provide technical assistance to the PC as required throughout Programme implementation and will provide reporting on the status of Programme implementation at least annually. The EE will report to the GCF Board on progress towards achieving the Mission or Miot to govern national-level Programme implementation with PCs during Programme inception.

The national fisheries agencies may establish an inter-ministerial National Coordination Committee (NCC) that will, inter alia: review annual work plans and receive quarterly reports on progress and expenditure. In such cases, the NCC should be informed about the Programme Steering Committee (PSC) meetings and provide input into the PSC’s decisions.

### Programme Implementation Unit (PMU)

123. The PMU will be headed by a Programme Director who will report to the PMU Manager.
124. Programme implementation will be headed by a Programme Steering Committee (PSC) that will meet at least once annually throughout Programme implementation and reach decisions by consensus. The PSC will consist of representatives from each of the 14 participating countries, including the Chairperson and the Programme Director. The principal functions of the PSC will be to provide strategic guidance and support adaptive management of Programme implementation; review and evaluate reports, discuss problems encountered in implementing the Programme, and make recommendations for addressing them; and inter-governmental coordination of, or contributions to, Programme activities.
125. Regional oversight will be achieved through annual reporting on Programme achievements and issues arising to SPC’s Heads of Fisheries (HoF) meetings and to FFA’s Forum Fisheries Committee (FFC). At all PSCs are represented by a representative from the HoF and FFC. The PSC will coordinate national and regional reporting to these forums and give key outcomes to the HoF.

### B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

126. PICs collectively contribute <0.003% of global GHG emissions each year but are recognized as the most vulnerable countries in terms of climate change impacts on coastal and marine ecosystems and impacts on coral reefs and redistribution of tuna. At the same time, the 14 participating countries have relatively small Gross Domestic Products (GDPs) and limited financial resources to adapt to the limited financial resources at their disposal.
