# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 37

## Component A

### 135. During Programme design and consultation, transition arrangements that will apply to key elements of Component A were discussed with participating countries. The outcome was a commitment by all 14 participating countries to establish a Transition Fund (TF) as an integral part of the Programme. The TF is responsible for implementing all national activities, including data collection, within the fisheries departments as Programme implementation proceeds.

The transition involves the Programme providing full support for such activities during the first three years of the Programme and then scaling back its involvement over the next two years to a level that will allow the participating countries to take on responsibility for these activities. The TF will be funded by contributions from the participating countries, with an initial contribution of 10% of the total cost of the Programme (US$24 million) in year one and scaling up to cover 50% of the total cost in years three and four.

The TF will also provide for a transition period during which the Programme will continue to support the development of national fisheries management systems, including the establishment of an independent fisheries authority. The TF will be managed by the Secretariat on behalf of the participating countries and will be subject to annual financial audits conducted by the World Bank.

### 136. The Programme will facilitate a series of train-the-trainer capacity building exercises in FAD programme management, including construction, deployment, and maintenance. By building national and regional capacity for the design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting on FAD programmes, a non-discriminatory exit of the Programme will be facilitated as local experts assume full responsibility for national FAD programmes.

The TF will also provide for the development of a comprehensive set of guidelines and procedures to support the design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting on FAD programmes. These guidelines will include best practices in data collection, analysis, legislative reviews, and regulation drafting to strengthen the delivery and utilisation of bycatch and tuna transferred or unloaded from commercial tuna fishing vessels for local consumption. Together, these training exercises and guidelines will ensure that participating countries are able to sustain their national FAD programmes.

### 137. In nine of the participating countries, the Programme's FAD-related activities will be hampered with the effects that the World Bank PROPER project is also making to strengthen national FAD programmes (See Annex A). The TF will provide for a phased approach in which access to FADs and other resources will increase over time. This will allow participating countries to gradually increase their capacity to design, implement, monitor, evaluate, and report on FAD programmes.

### 138. For both national FAD programmes and bycatch interventions, the Programme will support government engagement with reviews and policy updates that are expected to enhance the effectiveness of activities during the Programme implementation period and sustain these interventions once the Programme is completed.

## Component B

### 139. For Component B, the Exit Strategy is institutionally firmly established. The Programme AWSs will build on and complement existing national fisheries management systems in order to ensure that decisions are made relating to the management of tuna stocks in the context of climate and ecosystem variability and change. This will be achieved through a combination of measures including the development of fisheries management scenarios, SEAPDOM/SEADOM, and the use of SPC as the primary tool for providing analysis and advice on fisheries management.

### 140. The WCPFC currently provides financial support for the SEAPDOM model and will continue to do so as the model is improved and delivered through the AWS. This support is captured in WCPFC's Resolution 2019-02, which states that the WCPFC will provide financial support for the development of the SEAPDOM model until 31 December 2024.

The Programme will continue to provide technical assistance and capacity building support for Component B-type scientific activities augurs well for the gradual integration of the work supported under Component B into the Commission's long-term work programme and budget. Current support includes:

- **Lombardi, P., Soriano, S., Elliot, J., and Hampton, J. 2008. SEADOM V2: a spatial ecosystem and population dynamics model with application to tuna fisheries in the South Pacific Ocean.** (15 pages)
- **WCPFC/SC40/2009/WP.30.** (16 pages)

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 38

* Approved budgets for 2022-2025 for scientific work supported by WCPFC include strengthening of the Pacific tissue bank (US$12,000 in total for the period 2023-2025).
* Continuing regional tuna tagging operations (US$2.19 million for the period 2023-2025).
* Scientific work for close-kin mark-recapture (CKMR) method for tuna stock assessment (US$46,000).

The Exit Strategy will be developed in consultation with industry and other stakeholders to ensure that the scientific advice provided by the Programme is both relevant and useful. The strategy will also address the future funding of the Programme and the maintenance of the AWS. Industry partners will collect AWS data during Programme implementation and are expected to continue doing so after the end of the Programme. Continued data collection will improve the resolution and accuracy of the AWS over time. As the Programme progressively produces advice that is both of improved resolution and predictive accuracy, industry will be encouraged to adopt this advice in its management practices. An Exit Strategy will also address the future funding of the AWS. In the medium to longer term, financial contributions by industry to initiatives under Commission auspices may provide a sustainable source of revenue for the AWS. Although such a policy would potentially take more than 5 years to be debated and agreed to in the Commission, there is an opportunity to consider this option before the conclusion of the WTP and to contribute significantly to the Programme's Exit Strategy.

### (b) Other financing arrangements and contributions (max. 3 pages, approximately 0.5 page)
The exit strategy (see section B.6.) for the Programme includes transitioning costs related to national FAD programmes after the Programme ends. It is estimated that national FAD programmes will continue in the Programme ends. GCF funds will be used to cover the following costs: a) $1 million USD ($), per year, for 2014 participating countries for the first two years of the Programme and a scaling up for subsequent years; b) $350 thousand USD ($), per year, for the remaining salary in each of these years, and the government covering 100% of the costs. ### D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**145.** For millennia, coral reef fish have been a cornerstone of nutrition in the Pacific Island region. The effects of climate change on these fish populations are expected to have severe implications for the food availability of coastal communities in PiCs.

**146.** To address this challenge, Component A of the RTIP will assist in growing Pacific Island populations to their full potential by increasing the food availability for 560,000 individuals. FADs enable coastal communities to fish for tuna and other large pelagic fish (herring/fish) year-round in safe and effective ways—something that will be progressively more important in increasingly unpredictable climatic conditions. The RTIP will also support the development of a regional market for tuna products to continue to grow. For detailed information on the level of benefit (number of fish meals provided per beneficiary) please refer to Annex 23.

**147.** The Programme’s activities related to improving the distribution of bycatch and tuna are expected to increase the food availability for 290,000 individuals by providing up to four additional tuna meals per beneficiary per month in urban areas and up to six additional tuna meals per beneficiary per month in rural areas. This will also support commercial longliners in some ports (for example, Honiara, Majuro, Port Vila, Suva, and Nuku'alofa) also offer potential for increased catches of tuna and bycatch for local consumption, albeit at a lower level than the offloading of bycatch from purse-seine vessels.

**148.** Climate-driven redistribution of tuna threatens to undermine the economies of the PiN member countries and Cook Islands. The RTIP will support the development of climate information services (CIS) in these countries that can cause an average of 20% (range 10-30%) reduction in purse-seine catch from the combined EEZs of the nine tuna-developing countries (TDCs). This is equivalent to a loss of 5.8 million tonnes of tuna per year (range $40–$140 million) at today’s prices. Even under a more optimistic RCP 4.5 scenario, losses are projected to be between 3% and 27%. The RTIP will support the development of climate information services in these countries, with fewer government resources available for health, education, disaster preparedness and post-disaster recovery. This will enable PiN member countries and Cook Islands to better prepare their communities to use improved climatic information in decision-making (investment options, national harvest strategies for tuna, etc.). The RTIP will also support the development of climate information services that can be used within their combined EEZs. By delivering improved data through the AWS, the Programme will impact 2.5 million indirect beneficiaries (Annex 23).

**149.** Using the RTIP’s integrated approach to FADs, the CGI’s Integrated Results Management Framework and core indicators for adaptation is detailed in section E and will be demonstrated by attainment of the following targets in the 14 participating countries (or in subsets of countries where indicated) by the end of the first year:

- Deployed FADs: 528 FADs deployed within three years. Of these, 300 FADs were deployed during years 1–3 and will be replaced with new FADs during years 4–7.
- Total number of beneficiaries: 690,000 individuals (Annex 26, Technical Study 1)
- Improved livelihoods for 5 million nutritionally tuna meals (±50 for adults and 75 for children) are expected to be provided for 560,000 people across the region each year by strengthened national FAD programmes. This will also support commercial longliners in some ports.
- Total number of beneficiaries: 13 million nutritionally tuna meals from improved distribution of bycatch to urban and periurban communities (Annex 26, Technical Study 1)
- Improved livelihoods for 300 small-scale fishers using the 333 FADs deployed by the Programme, and the members of their households, totaling 15,000 people (co-benefits) (Annex 23)

## D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### The transition to making tuna a cornerstone of nutritious food availability for PICS across the region represents a fundamental paradigm shift in the way that tuna is consumed over the coming decades, under what is termed an “alternative scenario”. The availability of nutritional animal protein (fish) per capita is projected to decline significantly in PICS due to climate change and other factors. This will have profound implications on food security, nutrition, health, population growth, and, ultimately, consumption of lower-quality processed foods and the related increase in non-communicable diseases.

### Although forecasts will be improved to enable small-scale fisheries to avoid hazardous sea conditions when pursuing tuna with FADs, strong warnings that are not feasible to resolve may occur (Annex 2B; Technical Study 4) and will also benefit communities within a 10 km of the coast in all 13 countries.

## Replicability

The Programme will support the replication of FAO technology and experience in improving the utilisation and dissemination of bycatch and tuna from transshipping and unloading operations by:

- Enhancing technical and financial capacity and strengthening institutions at provincial, national, and regional levels.
- Conveying lessons learned from the implementation of FAO activities and sourcing of bycatch tuna from transshipping and unloading operations into knowledge and communication products and services and disseminating these through a range of channels including workshops, training courses, publications, and knowledge transfer as part of its awareness-raising activities.

Through these mechanisms, it is expected that FAO interventions, which will be carried out in a set of pilot sites initially (e.g., the Philippines, Indonesia, and Thailand), will target PICs (see Scale above), and possibly Pacific territories within the region and/or beyond the region.

As a region-wide effort, the AWIS will cover all 14 participating countries of the Programme. Given the large scale of FAO’s activities in this region, it is expected that the AWIS are applied in supporting multi-lateral agreements for access rights to tuna, will be rolled out in rounds, i.e., with some countries coming on-line earlier than others and with a focus initially on those countries already participating in the Programme. The AWIS will also apply to those countries coming online in later stages. Eventually it will have region-wide application, including for Pacific territories and the model may be replicated by small island developing states in other regions.

## Sustainability

The Programme will sustain the use of FADs by ensuring that they are adopted by countries as part of their national infrastructure for marine food availability, and that the operation and maintenance of expanded national FAD programmes is included within the country’s fisheries budget. The AWIS will be designed to ensure that it incorporates measures to ensure quick recovery and re-deployment of FADS following cyclones and other extreme weather events. The AWIS will also include a mechanism for the rapid deployment of new technologies and outreach which are built into the Programme will also help to improve overall sustainability.

The Programme will help sustain institutional technical capacity around FADs within Ministries of Fisheries in member countries, through training workshops on the use of FADs, including how to effectively deploy FADs, deployment, and maintenance. These trainings will increase the technical knowledge and expertise within the fisheries sector and among other relevant stakeholders such as extension officers, research institutions, and specialists to assist participating countries at the national scale, and the workshop budget is designed so these key personnel can be trained in-country by FAO experts or local trainers.

Depending on the context of the participating country, these measures can also be supplemented by (among others):

- Public private partnerships (PPPs) between commercial fishing companies and governments to provide technical and financial assistance with FAD deployment and maintenance as part of company Corporate Social Responsibility programmes.
- Creating awareness about harmful actions that destroy / damage FADS via community-based FAD rules.
- Promoting models for community contributions to maintaining the national FAD infrastructure, e.g., through fisher association-based fees.

**Page 48**

16\. Greater access of bycatch and tuna to urban and peri-urban communities will be sustained through national regulations and improvements in transhipping and unloading operations and supply chains. These operations, in turn, are supported by the implementation of the RTF and the associated monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system for fishing and unloading operations to urban and peri-urban communities, and processes of surplus bycatch for export to neighboring countries having shortages in fish supply. As an example, assistance could be provided to MSMEs and PPPs in developing market-based climate-resilient resources management systems supported by the United Nations Global Fund Development (UNCDF).

The sustainability will be strengthened through the promotion of strong economic and political alignment, brought about through the binding multilateral agreements which will give the rights of access to tuna resources for the participating countries. The RTF is also aligned with the UN Fish Stocks Agreement (FSA), the IATTC, the WCPFC and the Inter-American Tropical Tuna Commission (IATT) to ensure that tuna harvests can be sustained even when fishing pressure increases in some areas. This will contribute to high access areas further to the east outside the combined jurisdictions of PNA member countries.

### D.3 Sustainable development (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The interventions to be implemented under the RTF are focused on producing a range of positive economic, social, environmental, and gender-responsive development results, which are captured under the Programme’s two core objectives: Co-benefit 1: Improved livelihoods and access to markets; Co-benefit 2: Increased ecosystem services availability, by promoting reliable access to tuna and other fish as a locally available source of nutritious dietary protein for urban and peri-urban communities.

For Co-benefit 1 (improved livelihoods), positive results will include the following:

* **Increased livelihood diversification and access to markets:** thus providing opportunities to boost household incomes in rural and untargeted urban communities.
* **Increased economic empowerment:** through greater participation of women in fisheries activities across the spectrum of value chain activities (i.e., harvesting, handling, processing, distribution, and marketing).
* **Increased food security for urban and peri-urban communities:** through increased access to tuna and other fish as a locally available source of nutritious dietary protein.

For Co-benefit 2, the expected results will include increasing the sustainability of ecosystem services by improving the management and sustainability of oceanic tuna and other pelagic fish stocks across the Pacific. The Programme has been designed to promote greater gender equality and equity in fishing supply chains across programme countries. Three gender-related outcomes have been identified in the Gender Action Plan (see Annex 08):

* **Increased capacity of women in the FAD tuna fishery value chain focusing on post-harvest tuna processing and marketing activities.**
* **Increased awareness of gender roles and gender dimensions in the coastal fishery communities managing marine resources.**
* **Increased leadership and influence of women in FAD tuna fishery value chain decision-making.**

The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) system of the Programme will ensure that the co-benefits are monitored through a series of indicators, which will be reported to the respective Heads of State or Government Ministers and other public authorities. These efforts will be coordinated through the Heads of Fisheries (HoF) of the respective countries and/or their designated national implementing entities. The M&E system, and the indicators to be monitored, are described in Annex 11 and Annexes 23.

The Programme is fully consistent with the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), and will support the realization of the following goals and targets:

* **Details regarding the SDGs and associated targets are articulated in: United Nations. 2015\. Transforming our World: The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development.**

## D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
163. PICs are among the countries that are most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. In a recent survey conducted as part of the Notre Dame University Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GENI), nearly 200 countries worldwide were asked about their vulnerability and capacity to adapt to climate change. The results show high vulnerability by considering six life-supporting sectors: food, water, health, ecosystem services, human habitat, and infrastructure. In this survey, the top three most vulnerable sectors are food security, water resources, and health. The needs of countries in the Programme fall within the most-vulnerable quartile. The needs of this group of countries to combat the effects of climate change and build resilience are great.

### Absence of adequate financing
164. The participating PICs are generally small economies which lack adequate financial resources to develop robust systems to support well-coordinated national FAD programmes, as well as a regional AIMS aimed at gathering critical information on the status of fisheries in the region. In many cases, these countries have limited capacity to redistribute funds due to climate change. The PICs are among a group of countries that are heavily reliant upon overseas development assistance and international aid for their development.

165. The proposed Programme will help to provide the initial financial support needed to strengthen national FAD programmes, and to increase delivery of bycatch and tuna from longlining and unloading operations, to improve access to markets, and to reduce post-harvest losses. This will allow participating PICs to focus on building capacity for cost-identifying costs for incorporation in national budgets to sustain activities over the long term.

### Need for strengthening institutions and capacity
166. Knowledge of the impacts of climate change and direct impacts on coastal and urban populations needs to be strengthened further in the PICs participating in the GCF Programme. The general public in these countries, which are often small island developing states (SIDS), is not aware that climate change is a major threat to their livelihoods or that the damaging impacts that climate change is having on coral reef ecosystems and their resident fish species. These countries also often struggle to obtain the financial resources needed to build the teams needed to manage the diverse range of issues related to climate change.

167. Prominent challenges are the need (i) to understand the potential adverse impacts of fishing activities on targeted fish stocks, (ii) to ensure that the benefits from fisheries development align with sustainable development goals and that the socio-economic benefits from sustainably managed fisheries are recognized and shared equitably, and (iii) to create opportunities for greater participation of women and disadvantaged groups within the fisheries sector to improve their livelihoods. The Programme will address these challenges.

### Economic and logistical high (dependence on limited resource base)
170. New countries participating in the RFP are considered as Small Island Developing States (SIDS). The SIDs are characterised as limited in their economic development due in large part to the small size of their economies. In the case of the PICs, this condition is further exacerbated by their remoteness from major markets and the high

## DIVERSIFICATION OF THEIR ECONOMIES
For example, 9 of the 14 participating PICs are considered tuna-dependent economies, reflecting their reliance on this single resource for their economic well-being.

### THE BUDGETARY CONSTRAINTS OF PICs
Make it difficult for these countries to adequately finance a range of vital social services and other development priorities, including fisheries management and related services. These systemic factors cannot be fully addressed through any single assistance programme. However, the RTF will provide an important opportunity to address some of these constraints.

### THE DEVELOPMENT OF A REGIONAL AIRS
The Programme will support the main source of revenue generation for participating countries, which is the tuna fishery. This will enable countries to maintain or improve their potential to collect licensing fees from industrial tuna fishing operations.

## D.S. COUNTRY OWNERSHIP (MAX. 580 WORDS, APPROXIMATELY 1 PAGE)
### COUNTRY OWNERSHIP IS FUNDAMENTAL
To the design and implementation of the Pacific Island RTF. Considering the interdependencies between climate change impacts on marine ecosystems, economic, and social improvements in response to the effects of climate change on coastal fisheries and the redistribution of tuna biomass in the waters of PICs, a regional approach is required for the development of an effective adaptation strategy at both national and regional stakeholders is required to ensure the sustainability and success of the proposed interventions. The engagement with national and regional stakeholders will be critical throughout the conceptualisation and development of the Funding Proposal and will continue throughout the implementation of the Programme.

### IMPLEMENTATION OF THE FUNDING PROPOSAL
Considerable efforts were made on a frequent and regular basis to engage and consult with the Heads of Fisheries (HoF), the NDAs, and a wide range of stakeholders living and working in coastal communities. The engagement process was designed to ensure that all stakeholders understand the structure of the Programme; (i) contribute to the identification and design of interventions to assist their sustainable development and adaptation to climate change; and (ii) provide feedback on the funding that will be provided to support the key actions needed to enhance their capacity to adapt to climate change. The views and requirements of national stakeholders have been critical in shaping the framework and activities of this programme.

### IN-DEPTH COUNTRY ENGAGEMENT
In-depth country engagement has focused attention specifically on how the Programme aligns with national NDCs, development priorities, and existing adaptation strategies. In addition, the Programme is designed to implement their NDCs. 10 of the 14 participating countries have included the need for adaptation to the effects of climate change on the ocean and coastal marine habitats in their NDAs. Appendices 2-8 of the Feasibility Study Chapter provide a summary of the key findings from the national stakeholder engagement, including strategy documents in each participating country. Annexure A provides further detail on climate policies and strategies.

### IMPLEMENTATION ARRANGEMENTS
It is important to note that national governments, through their heads of fisheries (HoF), determined the implementation arrangements for this program, selecting SPC as the lead Executing Entity (EE) of the Programme. The EE will be responsible for implementing the programme in close collaboration with all stakeholders as their regional technical agency. SPC uses its extensive technical capacity to support sustainable development and climate change adaptation activities across the Pacific region. As the EE, SPC serves its members by intervening and harnessing the nexus of climate, ocean, land, culture, rights, and good governance. The EE will work with national governments to ensure that the Programme is designed to respond with urgent collective action to the threat of climate change.

### THE PROGRAMME IS REGIONAL
Given the profound economic dependence of Pacific Island countries on tuna, and the close diplomatic, economic, and social ties between them, it was agreed to be both regional and national in scope. The Programme is regional in significance because tuna are migratory fish species shared by PICs and international waters. However, the programme will also have a strong national focus at the country level because adaptations to increase access to food for food availability must be customised for each country, due to varying contexts and the differences in population size and location of Pacific Island Nations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 48

## The framework of the Programme, together with the key components and activities, are designed to support countries' national efforts to embed climate resilience at the core of their planning to maximize the sustainable use of tuna resources in a changing environment.

### Pacific Island Countries have demonstrated their commitment to strengthening resilience to climate change through the development of National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). These NAPs are aligned with the Pacific Regional Integrated Approach to Address Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management (PRIDM) [15]: i. Strengthened adaptation and risk reduction to climate change, including managing risks caused by climate change in fisheries and aquaculture development; ii. Planning processes that integrate climate change considerations into disaster risk management, response and recovery from natural disasters caused by climate change.

### The Programme will support the implementation of NAPs through a comprehensive series of agreements which reflect regional priorities for managing regional fisheries resources and are aligned with the Programme investments and goals (see Annex 1: Chapter 2, Section 3.1). These include:

- Western and Central Pacific Fisheries Commission (WCPFC): Regional Agreement on Climate Change as it Relates to Fisheries in the Western and Central Pacific Fisheries Commission Resolution (2015-01)
- Western and Central Pacific Fisheries Commission (WCPFC): Regional Agreement on the Aspirations of Small Island Developing States and Territories (Resolution 2006-001)
- Regional Agreement on the Conservation and Management of Tuna Resources: to improve sustainability of tuna resources, add value to tuna catches, increase employment, and provide better access for tuna to nutritious food available in coastal communities (e.g., through the development of a regional market for small-scale coastal reefs to tuna)
- A Regional Agreement for Coastal Fisheries - Pathways for Change[16]: An innovative regional agreement to maintain the benefits of small-scale fisheries in the face of declining coastal ecosystems and associated fish stocks
- The Parties to the New Agenda (PRM) Vessel Day Scheme, which supplies 95% of tuna caught in the region

### The HoFs and NDAs in each of the 14 participating countries have acknowledged the high level of stakeholder engagement that has been achieved through the Programme. This was supported by a wide range of stakeholders including national agencies, NGOs, CSOs, private sector, academia, associations, and community groups, comprising over 1,070 individual stakeholders within the 14 partner countries were consulted and received an overview of the Programme’s objectives and activities. The Programme has been designed to ensure that women are represented in a proportionate manner; women were involved in the consultation process, the total number persons consulted was 25% female. In addition, the Programme has ensured that all stakeholders have had access to the Programme, stakeholders were provided with opportunities to share their views and stakeholder feedback has been sought throughout the development of the Programme. The Programme has also ensured that key stakeholders in Programme development and implementation, ensuring that Programme components and activities incorporate gender equality principles and practices. For more information on how to engage with HoFs and NDAs please see Annex 7 for more information on the stakeholder engagement process.

### The participating countries that have delivered their no objection letters (NOLs) have duly followed their respective no objection procedures as established by each country. The delivery of the NOLs represents the culmination of consultations, discussions and collaboration with the countries, setting a foundation for effective implementation of the tuna adaptation programme.

162. Annex D of the Funding Proposal provides Economic Analysis (EA) and Financial Analysis (FA) of the Programme and alternative options under a variety of scenarios. Annex O3 includes the Excel files for the EA and FA of the Programme. The economic analysis (EA) is conducted from the perspective of the national economies of the 14 Participating Countries.

The results of the economic analyses are summarized in Table 5-6 and indicate an overall Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) of 9% on USD basis, with a Net Present Value (NPV) of USD 30.1 billion at a discount rate cost ratio for the overall Programme of 4:26. The combined result for the Programme comfortably exceeds a 9% EIRR, and the NPV is positive even if the assumptions are relaxed to reflect more realistic levels of some key assumptions (e.g., changes in fish catch from FADs, average fish price, changes to future investment in FADs, average FAD life, changes to Capital and Operational Expenditures of up to +20%). It also provides analyses for various scenarios.

154. The financial analysis (FA) considers the financial implications of the ongoing activities developed under the Programme on government costs and revenue for each of the 14 Participating Countries, and the increase in costs that will be incurred by the governments as a result of the implementation of the AWG and the BWC. The total cost to the GFCF is USD 53 million at the end of the GFCF programme, increasing to USD 18 million per year in 2050. The government expenditure on FADs will be approximately USD 47 million by 2050. The revenue generated from the sale of fish will be minimal because the role of government is limited to providing support through its regulatory functions and ensuring that the AWG and BWC are implemented in a timely manner. The cost to governments for the completion of the Programme through their membership of the regional organizations that develop and use the system will be approximately USD 10 million per year, increasing to USD 25 million by 2050, or USD 38 billion over 50 years, equating to ~$200 per country for each of the 14 PICs. The administration of the AWG will be the responsibility of the regional agencies acting on behalf of the PICs – FFA, WCPFC and SPC.

167. The actual cost of the Programme is expected to be approximately USD 5 billion over the lifetime of 14 PICs with an extraordinary dependence on tuna for food availability and/or economic development. Component A (approximately USD 20 million) will be fully funded by the GFCF, while Component B (approximately USD 38 billion) will be paid by governments to 2050. The estimated cost of this benefit is $65 per person over a seven-year period, approximately $12 per person per year. The proposed mechanisms for sustaining national FAD Programmes and distribution of tuna from harvested fish are expected to continue beyond the lifetime of the Programme.

The actual cost of the Programme will be borne by countries to prevent estimated combined losses of government revenue of USD 50 billion (range ~USD -140 billion) per year at today’s value by 2050. These estimates will be adjusted as uncertainty in the tuna modelling is reduced through development of the AWG. Even so, the benefits for tuna-dependent communities and economies are expected to far outweigh these costs.

The Programme will also provide a grant request component B with a relatively low number of years. Annex O3 examines the payback period under various scenarios and concludes that the payback period is less than 10 years in all cases.

- Increased resilience (adaptation)

E.2 GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max. 300 words)

The Programme will contribute to a paradigm shift from climate-induced food insecurity to improved resilience of communities by strengthening their capacity to adapt and respond to climate change impacts for Pacific island countries that have traditionally high levels of fish consumption under a changing climate.

- National FAD programmes to increase access to tuna for coastal communities as the supply of fish from coral reef ecosystems declines due to ocean warming and acidification.
- The Programme will also contribute to improved resilience by providing new opportunities for small-scale fisheries in Pacific island countries that are expected to occur as industrial fishing effort moves further to the east due to ocean warming and consequent redistribution of tuna stocks. These vehicles will provide a means for communities to access these new resources, which would otherwise be inaccessible.
- The Programme will also contribute to improved resilience by providing new opportunities for small-scale fisheries in Pacific island countries that are expected to occur as industrial fishing effort moves further to the east due to ocean warming and consequent redistribution of tuna stocks. These vehicles will provide a means for communities to access these new resources, which would otherwise be inaccessible.
- The Programme will also contribute to improved resilience by providing new opportunities for small-scale fisheries in Pacific island countries that are expected to occur as industrial fishing effort moves further to the east due to ocean warming and consequent redistribution of tuna stocks. These vehicles will provide a means for communities to access these new resources, which would otherwise be inaccessible.

The Programme paradigm shift will also be delivered through establishment of an advanced warning system (AWS) to inform tuna-dependent countries about the distribution of tuna in advance of fishing effort. The AWS will provide reliable and timely information on where tuna are expected to be found at any given time, enabling island nations to secure equitable access to tuna resources as climate alters the distribution of tuna; and (ii) provide the foundation for revisions to co-management arrangements that will allow small-scale fishers in Pacific island countries to retain access rights to the shared fisheries resources they underpin their economies by increasing their capacity to negotiate for solutions founded on science, equity, and justice. The AWS will also help to ensure that more equitable outcomes are achieved for a vulnerable region and its populations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 52

## Replicability

### (1) Adoption of FADs for nearshore fishing for tuna and other pelagic fish species is currently well below the level needed to supply the amount of food required for good nutrition of growing populations.

#### Medium
- Preliminary modeling of the responses of tuna to the warming ocean and its sources of uncertainty. Current models show that mixed stocks of tuna forms one large mixed stock across the entire tropical Pacific Ocean and it is not possible to identify which tuna species will respond to ocean warming.
- The AWIS reduces uncertainty in the response of tuna to ocean warming and is built as a 'living' system, enabling it to be continually improved as the modeling is progressively refined.

### (2) The Programme will support the replication of FAD technology by:
- Building technical and financial capacity, and improving access to information at provincial, national and regional levels
- Facilitating the implementation of FAD activities into existing fisheries management systems and services and disseminating them widely through training, awareness raising campaigns, community practices of problem and knowledge transfer as part of its awareness raising activities

#### (1) The application of FAD technology results in targeted coastal communities fishing for tuna and other pelagic fish species, leading to increased catches, revenues, and employment, and increased household income. Successful models of FAD deployment and use are replicated across the region. Lessons learned are used to correct weaknesses as they are identified.
- Public awareness of declining coral reef fish stocks, and the benefits of FADs for increasing food security in coastal communities across the region

#### (2) The programme reduces uncertainty in the modeling of the responses of tuna to ocean warming by building a 'living' system on identifying the stock structure of each tuna species. This will lead to better management of tuna stock, including better information on the impact of ocean warming on tuna prey, and increasing


---
**Page 56**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 53

## Sustainability

### Medium

(1) National FAD programs are currently supported in an ad hoc manner without a clear strategy to strengthen and develop these programs, which could provide financial support from government budgets for the future.

Transhipment regulations do not adequately address the available needs of urban populations due to the distribution of pelagic stocks due to climate change.

(1) The vital contributions of fish to the nutrition of Pacific Island people are increasingly threatened by overfishing and FAD programmes, supported by ongoing transhipment operations and mechanisms that provide for improved food security in urban communities by improving transhipment from industrial fishing operations.

### Medium

The programme will sustain the use of FADs for ensuring that they are adopted as part of the national fisheries management strategy, and that the maintenance of expanded national FAD programmes is included in national development plans and recurring budgets. Within the context of the participating country, these measures will be supplemented by:

- Partnership arrangements (PPAs) between commercial fishing companies and local communities;
- Financial assistance with FAD deployment for small-scale fishers;
- Corporate Social Responsibility programmes;
- Creating legislation to procure actions that support the implementation of community-based FAD rules; and
- Increasing financial contributions to maintaining the national FAD programme, including association-based fees.

Improvements to transhipment operations and logistics will lead to a greater distribution of tuna bycatch to urban communities will be facilitated. This will provide new marketing opportunities for MSMEs and PPPs to distribute fish from industrial fishing vessels in peri-urban communities, and/or process surplus

## To implement National FAD programmes

countries is a practical way of providing a greater number of small-scale fishers with the opportunity to catch tuna and other pelagic fish species (hereafter tuna) efficiently and safely. This Activity will support countries in developing and implementing national FAD programmes that will be piloted to increase access to tuna in each country and region. The pilot phase will last for 12 months, after which the programmes will then be strengthened to the level required to achieve the desired results. The programme will include procurement of materials, equipment, training, and capacity building needed to ensure that FADs are appropriately constructed, deployed, utilized, and maintained; and that fishers are equipped to harness the full potential of FAD safety.

Across the 14 participating countries, 330 FADS will be the focus of this Activity. In addition, 250 FADS will be procured for use in the Tuna Fishery Management Programme (TFMP) Activity. Furthermore, train-the-trainer sessions will be conducted to ensure that fishers are equipped with the skills and knowledge required to construct, deploy, retrieve, and manage FADs effectively.

Activity 1.2: Augment national safety-of-sea initiatives

The Programme places a strong emphasis on safety at sea, given that some FADs will be up to 7 km offshore and the experience of existing small-scale fishers encouraged to use FADs is limited. The objective is to reduce the risk of reef fish within lagoons and relatively sheltered inshore coastal areas being caught by small-scale fishers operating outside FADs.

1.2a. Conduct a needs analysis for improved vessel safety designs for small-scale fishers using FADs.
1.2b. Establish the enabling conditions for national meteorological services to provide weather forecasts and warnings suitable for small scale fishers.
1.2c. Support procurement and delivery of safety equipment, linked to vessel registration where practical.

Activity 1.3: Strengthen post-harvest practices and improve market opportunities for FAD-caught fish

This Activity will be designed to promote existing postharvest methods for tuna and associated species caught around FADs in the region. The main aim is improving the handling of tuna fish from FADs so as to extend the shelf life of the fish, and reduce waste during processing.

1.3a. Provide training courses for households and MSMEs to improve post-harvest practices using existing methods such as drying, smoking, salting, and boiling.
1.3b. Provide coastal communities with basic equipment to apply post-harvest methods for FAD-caught fish for cold storage where appropriate.

1.3c. Basic equipment for drying, smoking or boiling FAD-caught fish will be provided in selected sites.
1.3d. Market opportunities for FAD-caught fish for MSMEs are identified and tested.

## Output 2: Increased supply of bycatch and tuna from industrial fishing operations for urban and peri-urban communities

### Activity 2.1: Implement strategies to deliver more transhipped and unloaded tuna and bycatch directly to underprivileged communities

This activity is designed to identify and address weaknesses in the entire supply chain that has led to catch arising from industrial fishing operations being wasted or lost, particularly for bycatch and tuna in urban and peri-urban communities with a notorious source of food insecurity. The project will focus on improving the capture of seine fishing, i.e., fish that is not caught by tuna cameras, which are used to monitor tuna catches. This will be achieved through vessels training their catch to carrier vessels in regional ports, particularly those in Fiji, Tonga and Vanuatu.

#### 1.3c: Identify and promote market opportunities for FAD-caught fish

- 1.3d. Integrated, multi-media campaigns that raise awareness of the impacts of climate change on tuna and bycatch fisheries through fishing as an alternative food source, in targeted coastal communities.
- 2.1a. Assessments of the availability of bycatch and tuna.

- 2.1c. Projected changes in location of transhipment and unloading activities across the region.
- 2.1d. Build national capacity to conduct patrols and inspections during transhipment and unloading of bycatch and tuna.

#### 2.1e. Develop procedures and regulations to increase availability of transhipped and unloaded bycatch and tuna where needed in the region

- 2.1f. Provide training for urban communities to develop/implement post-harvest practices and extend shelf life

## Component B - Adaptation to reduce risks to Pacific Island economies from climate-driven tuna redistribution

### Output 3: Improved forecasts and projections for climate-driven tuna redistribution which facilitate effective adaptations for all stakeholders

#### Activity 3.1: Develop and deliver an advanced weather and climate information system (AWCIS) for climate-driven tuna redistribution

This activity is designed to harness the latest in big data technologies, machine learning algorithms, and satellite imagery to develop an Advanced Weather Information System (AWCIS) that provides high-resolution forecasts and projections of climate change impacts on the distribution of tuna biomass. This will enable Pacific Island countries and territories to make informed decisions about how best to adapt their economies from disruptions to their economic and global migration options.

#### 3.1.1: Transition existing fisheries and ocean management models to a high-resolution model suitable for ECE analyses of climate change impacts on tuna biomass

#### 3.1.2: Establish baselines and indicators for projection of climate change impacts on distribution of tuna biomass

#### 3.1.3: Enhance collection and curation of global phytoplankton and zooplankton data

### 8.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words)

The Programme’s Monitoring and Evaluation Plan is designed to measure the following (see Annexes 11 and Annex 23 for more detailed information):

- Progress towards accomplishment of Programme-level outcomes and co-benefits, as articulated in the Programme ToC and Logframe; including Programme contributions toward achieving Fund-level outcomes, as measured by RMF core indicators (see Sections E.3, E.4 and the co-benefits included in section E.5); and
- Progress towards accomplishment of AIMS-specific outcomes and co-benefits.

Programme-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the monitoring, evaluation, accountability, and learning (MEAL) systems and processes utilized by SPC – referred to hereafter as MEAL. Additionally, monitoring and evaluation for the Programme will be complementary to the applicable government monitoring and evaluation systems and policies of the various participant countries.


---
**Page 77**
---

The Programme monitoring and evaluation team staff will be responsible for designing studies to refine baselines and collect data for mid-term and final calculation of indicators for GCF-level core indicators and outcomes (as defined in the RMP) and Programme-level results and indicators.

### Monitoring

189. The primary responsibility for day-to-day Programme monitoring and implementation rests with the Programme Director, supported by the monitoring and evaluation team.
- **Programme inception phase**: The Programme Director will be responsible for ensuring that all necessary documentation is prepared in advance of the reception process to ensure the efficient implementation of the Programme. A Programme inception workshop, involving C, SPC, FFA, CGIRO, senior representatives of the host country, and other key stakeholders (e.g., PNG) will be held within the first 6 months of Programme implementation.
- **Overall objectives**: The overall objective is to ensure that the Programme is implemented in a manner consistent with the RMP and the GCF’s core principles.
- **Assist the Programme team and stakeholders to understand and take ownership of the programme implementation approach, objectives and outcomes and discuss any changes required**.
  - Discuss the roles, support services and complementary responsibilities of the Programme team and the national government ministries including reporting and monitoring requirements.
  - Review the logical framework, re-assess baselines as needed, and discuss reporting, monitoring and evaluation roles and responsibilities and review / elaborate on the Monitoring & Evaluation plan.

190. The Programme Director will ensure that the indicators included in the Programme logic result frameworks are monitored and reported regularly. Programme interventions including activities and outputs, will be monitored as well as the achievement of higher-level programme results. The Programme M&E system will cover two levels: (i) monitoring at the project level against investment criteria and Programme-wide performance; (ii) Monitoring and evaluation will be implemented and included in the agreements between the CGI-Agency or AE and the Executing Entity (EE), SPICs, Agreements between SPCs and Partners (FFA, PNG) and the CGIRO will include similar obligations.

### Evaluation

190. The Programme’s mid-term/end evaluation process will include an internal impact evaluation and an independent process evaluation.
- **Internal evaluations**: will take place to inform adaptive management of the Programme and capture overall impact, respectively. The evaluations will rely on key questions (to be initially developed during inception planning) to respond to the performance and impact of the Programme’s completed activities and will include assessment against GCFID, CGIRO, SPC, FFA, PNG and CGIRO indicators.
- **Independent evaluation**: will be conducted by an independent evaluator. The evaluation will assess the relevance of the intervention, its overall performance, and coherence in climate finance delivery; gender equity and inclusiveness; innovation and potential for paradigm shift; country ownership; coherence of climate finance; and any unintended building blocks and unexpected results (positive and negative). The Terms of Reference for the evaluations will be developed in consultation between the EE and AE.

192. Overall, evaluation will contribute to accountability and learning by reviewing emerging evidence on the performance and the impact sectoral Impact of the project.
- **Internal evaluation**: will provide information to inform adaptive management and support evidence-based decision-making.
- **The midterm evaluation will be instrumental in contributing - through operational and strategic recommendations – to improving implementation, setting out any necessary corrective measures for the remaining period of the Programme. The final evaluation will assess the relevance of the intervention, its overall performance, as well

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

**F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)**

Selected Risk Factor 1 Large-scale, climate-driven natural disasters that significantly disrupt tuna fisheries.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | High | Low |

Description
Natural disasters are a common occurrence in the Pacific Islands region and can disrupt plans to increase access to tuna and associated pelagic fish for food availability, as happened in Vanuatu in 2015 during Cyclone Pam. Any such disruption will have an adverse impact on the livelihoods of small-scale fishing communities who may be overwhelmed by associated recovery efforts, which in turn may decrease the ability of communities or governments to participate in Programme implementation causing delays and/or cancellations.

However, natural disasters are likely to have a high impact in the areas where they occur, but the overall risk to Programme implementation is considered to be low because most disasters are likely to be localised and have a relatively short duration relative to the implementation period of the proposed programme.


Mitigation Measures
The EE will work with partners to assess potential risks of natural disasters, actively manage the risks of Programme activities, and change the impacted interventions as needed. Depending on the circumstances and the nature of the disaster, the EE may need to adjust its approach in order to continue supporting access to tuna and associated pelagic fish for food availability or supporting post-disaster recovery efforts in coordination with other partners.

The EE will also work with partners to reduce the climate risks from dangerous weather and natural disasters, especially storms and cyclones. Activity 1.2 is focused on improving national safety-at-sea initiatives, including improvements to meteorological and natural disaster forecasts to inform small-scale fishers and coast communities of impending extreme weather events. In addition, in collaboration with relevant government agencies and coastal communities, SPC will assist the national fisheries agency to build resilience to these natural disasters. This will be done by supporting the development of a National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS) for tuna fishing. The EE will also work closely with partners to ensure that access to tuna is not reduced due to extreme storm surge can be quickly resolved (see Activity 1.1 described in section B:3). Procurement of FADs will be subject to the usual due process and recommendations to reduce risks.

Selected Category Risk Factor 2 Large-scale ocean closures

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Local | Low | Low |

Description
There are multiple prominent entities supporting tuna migrations more of their EEZs as take Marine Protected Areas and this can be expected to reduce fishing operations within these zones. For example, reductions in fishing effort by commercial fleets in the waters of some countries due to the SBT initiative. *For the purposes of this assessment, it is assumed that an increase in fishing effort will not have a significant impact on fish availability of some urban communities. It can also be expected to affect the full potential of the ARI's at assist countries to optimize sustainable tuna catches, taking access fees and other socio-economic benefits derived from tuna caught in these areas.*


---
**Page 80**
---

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The EEZ will work with participating countries to ensure consultation and overall communication, including all relevant government ministries and civil society stakeholders, through a dedicated focal point related to the Ministries of Environment which oversee the planning of national-scale Marine Protected Areas and marine spatial planning processes to ensure sustainable fisheries management.

The EEZ will also oversee multiple activities related to an audit of national legislation that may need to be updated, created, or revised to effectively support strategic tuna fishery goals and supply chains for FAO-caught fish and bycatch. The EEZ will work with participating countries to raise awareness about the importance of addressing this risk through communication campaigns and direct engagement with partner organisations and ministries. Further, the EEZ will provide technical assistance in developing national legislation that is consistent with the Programme.

### Selected Risk Factor 3 Ineffective coordination between Regional Fisheries Management Organisations leads to ineffective management in managing tuna stocks

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Low |

**Description**

Technical Study 8 prescribes "Develop a preliminary framework for joint management of redistributed tuna stocks by WCPFC and IATTC, providing an overview of potential issues that will need to be considered by both RFMOs as the increased interaction between them may lead to new challenges in their respective areas of responsibility. This includes governing the operation of the RFMOs and formal arrangements supporting their cooperation in relation to science, conservation and management." The implementation of this measure is likely to face significant governance and management challenges that will require enhanced cooperation, particularly in the context of future negotiations for the redistribution of tuna stocks between WCPFC and IATTC. This could lead to a situation where both RFMOs are managing a different RMO. The impact of ineffective coordination would be less effective management of redistributed tuna stocks in the long term.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The legal frameworks of the two RFMOs governing tropical tuna in the Pacific Ocean encourage and enable cooperation on various issues. There can be a specific conservation and management measure created that mandates the type of cooperation required between WCPFC and IATTC, including the establishment of joint working groups or task forces that made climate change a permanent item on the Commission agenda (and the agenda of its subsidiary bodies) to ensure that both RFMOs are aware of each other's activities. This will begin shaping around a framework that will support strengthened, informed, and effective cooperation.

SPC has existing MOUs with both RFMOs and will facilitate coordination and communication on the status and requirements for tuna stocks as determined by the RFMOs.


### Selected Risk Factor 4 Global or national economic or political situation becomes unstable, disrupting the Programme

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | High | Low |

**Description**

Political risk in the countries participating in the Programme is characterised by possible change in government personnel in key positions or changing management of relevant government agencies or ministries. The main risk is that political disruption and changes in personnel and/or government structures could cause delays in Programme operations.

While the probability of such disruption is high given the number of participating countries in this Programme, the overall impact of such disruption/delays over the lifetime of the Programme is considered to be low and geographically limited.

# Mitigation Measure(s)

The AE has a long history of working with the region and is well positioned to continue supporting adherence to GCF structures as the Programme continues. The 14 participants are all former members of NPC in their regional technical agency. It is expected that they will continue to work closely with the AE and NPC to ensure alignment with GCF policies, including AMILCFT.

Government ministries and agencies from all the participating countries have been involved in the Programme design, implementation, and monitoring. Consultations organized to provide input to the Funding Proposal development process. The Programme activities have been designed to address government priorities related to climate change in the fisheries sector and to ensure alignment with GCF policies, including AMILCFT.

Based on its 70 years of experience working in the region, the AE can swiftly respond to unexpected regional or national challenges. For example, SPC could quickly shift the timing and location of workshops and training events disrupted by unforeseen circumstances.

## Selected Risk Factor 5 Prohibited Practices, Including ML/FT

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Low | Medium |

### Description

This large-scale programme involves a wide array of actors in 14 countries—including the EE, implementing Partner organizations receiving GCF funds, participating SPC member country governments receiving GCF resources, and various other stakeholders. The AE has several procedures and controls to identify and mitigate the risks of prohibited practices. To comply with GFCF standards, the AE rigorously screens all CIs to ensure compliance with international sanctions lists. It also requires EE programme staff to undergo training on PP (including red flags) and how to report potential violations. The AE has a suite of policies related to ethics, fraud, and anti-corruption and a detailed Anti-Money Laundering policy. These policies are part of project monitoring by the AE, including compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices, including AMILCFT.

As EE, SPC has a suite of policies related to ethics, fraud, and anti-corruption and a detailed Anti-Money Laundering policy. These policies are part of project monitoring by the AE, including compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices, including AMILCFT.

## Selected Risk Factor 6: Unrealised or delayed co-financing

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Select High |

### Description
Component B relies on continued and ongoing oceanic data collection from a large area of the tropical Pacific Ocean. To collect the oceanographic data, successful collaboration with commercial fishing vessels that operate in EEZs and/or within the WCPFC CP12/04-06 areas is required to support the implementation of Component B of the Programme. This co-finance will occur in years 2-6 and there is a low probability this co-finace would not materialize in time.

Although SPC has pre-established relationships with many key industry players operating in the Western and Central Pacific region, the companies could consider this collaboration as an operational burden, given that commercial tuna fishing vessels are required to collect all data needed to build the AWS. Additionally, there may be legislative barriers in some countries that would prevent the collection of oceanographic data by commercial fishing vessels.

### Mitigation (B)egasures
CI and SPC conducted outreach to multiple companies operating commercial tuna vessels in the WCPFO at side events of the Western and Central Pacific Fisheries Commission Meetings in December 2022 and 2023. Industry was briefed on the AWS Project and its potential benefits for their operations, and a number of key industry leaders and captains have commenced a proactive engagement exercise to formalize collaborative arrangements with SPC to collect oceanographic data from commercial tuna vessels as part of the AWS Project. SPC will work with the support of the International Seafood Sustainability Foundation (ISSF) in aims to formally engage at least eight companies operating commercial tuna vessels in the WCPFO region, and to encourage these vessels to operate as "ships of opportunity" to collect oceanographic data necessary to support Component B of the Programme. The details for this exercise were discussed, and agreed, in the margins of the December 2023 WCPFC meeting in Cook Islands.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 80

The Pacific region and the rest of the world has recently experienced high inflation. Although the IMF forecasts that inflation is going to continue for Asia-Pacific region during 2024 and 2025,1 the risk of inflation is considered a significant risk factor in the context of the SPC financial management practices and by CI through the regular Programme financial reporting and submission of financial statements.

The Pacific region is particularly vulnerable due to dependence on external markets, the small economies of island states, limited foreign currency reserves, susceptibility to global supply chain disruptions, exchange rate fluctuations, and high import dependency.

Higher-than-expected inflation would increase costs across the whole programme, making it harder to achieve Programme targets within the set Programme budget.

## Mitigation Measures

To mitigate the impacts of inflation, the budget has been prepared assuming an inflation rate of 2.5%, in line with SPC practices. The inflation rate used is in line with central bank targets for participating countries over the medium term (3-10 years). The budget will be reviewed and adjusted as necessary to ensure that it remains consistent with its standard SPC financial management practices and by CI through the regular Programme financial reporting and submission of financial statements.

The impact of inflation on costs should be assessed and applied through adaptive management of the Programme by the EE in consultation with the AE.

### Selected Risk Factor 9: Health crises cause extended disruption to regular economic, societal, or institutional functions

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | High |

#### Description
Unanticipated situations like the COVID-19 pandemic have led countries participating in their borders in a complete lock-down manner. For multiple years, COVID-19 made travel to the region impossible and caused significant delays in activities and other engagement work. The impact of the pandemic is limited and subject to change, which could have an impact on certain national and regional workshops or Activities planned by the Programme.

A significant increase in the spread of COVID-19 or other infectious diseases could lead to restrictions on movements of people across borders, causing delays in Programme activities and other engagement work. Delays could also be caused because of limits on the implementation of in-person capacity-building/activities/experiences or online/distance learning opportunities.

## Mitigation Measures

The EE and Implementing Partners will continue to adaptively manage, including anticipation of different COVID-19 scenarios and appropriate sequencing of activities to permit continued progress. There will be strict adherence and compliance with the requirements of national health authorities and World Health Organization (WHO) guidelines for travel and other related matters.

As a result of COVID-19, all the partner organisations have procedures and guidelines related to highly infectious diseases, and recent experience in implementing measures such as social distancing, provision of personal protective equipment and project-related safety and security measures.

As part of the Programme’s strategy and sustainability beyond its funding period, the governments of the 14 participating countries are required to pay for assisted staff salaries from years 5-6. There is a risk that the governments may not fully cover their share.

Should the governments fail to fulfill their commitments, there may be adverse impacts on the implementation of Component A, resulting in delays in implementation and achievement of the Component’s objective. It may also affect the sustainability of the Programme beyond its funding period.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Failure to secure strategic partnerships or maintain collaborative agreements to maintain programme initiatives beyond the Programme’s funding period will have a negative impact on the overall success of the Programme at the sub-regional region. However, because the 14 participating countries and their regional agencies are experienced in assessing and maintaining such arrangements, and given alignment arrangements to minimize adverse implications, the risk is low (see also para 14 in the Exit Strategy section).

The commitment by countries to provide staffed initial resources during implementation and beyond the Programme period of performance will be captured in the agreement to be developed between SPC and each participating country (Section 5.2).

### Selected Risk Factor 10 - EE Capacity to Implement the Programme

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | High |

**Description**

This multi-country Programme is large and complex by nature, and the EE has not managed or executed a single project of comparable scale. Potential risks associated with the EE’s capacity to implement the Programme include cost controls, delays in implementation, and ultimately failure to achieve adaptation impacts.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

C1 has conducted due diligence and capacity assessments of SPC as the EE, including financial management and procurement capacity, and SPC is implementing improvement/enhancement strategies including a risk register for monitoring and managing risks associated with the EE’s capacity to implement the Programme. The EE has managed a variety of large-scale projects concurrently across its varying divisions which could potentially impact on the ability of the EE to manage this Programme effectively in terms of progress and required adaptive management measures if delays to implementation or other issues are encountered.

### Selected Risk Factor 11 - Partner Capacity

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

**Description**

Full capacity assessments of Technical and SPC Member Grantees will take place during implementation of the Programme. Should any partner be found to have insufficient capacity, consequences include delays to implementation of sub-activities due to the need to engage alternative grantees.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Technical partners were selected for their track record in specific sectors, and prior engagement with SPC. In addition, grantees’ financial management capability will be assessed during the implementation period prior to the end of year 2. If a grantee is found to have insufficient capacity, SPC will engage alternative grantees on behalf of SPC Member grantees; if financial management / procurement capacity is insufficient, SPC will procure goods and services on behalf of the participating country.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### Summary of Risks
- **ESS 1: Environmental and Social Assessment, Management and Monitoring**
  - Based on the detailed description of Programme activities, the review of relevant documents, and the results from the GCF ESS screening, the Programme is assigned a Category C risk rating for this Programme. ESS screening finds that there are no adverse environmental and/or social risks and/or impacts.

#### Mitigation Factors/Measures or Management Strategies
- An Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) is determined to be an appropriate instrument for this Programme. The ESMP has been developed in accordance with the GCF’s ESMP Guidelines, and it has not yet been determined (such as FAD deployment site selection). The ESMP includes a detailed description of the Programme, including the Engagement Plan and Indigenous Peoples’ Plan; has been designed to address potential risks and/or impacts without the need for additional site-specific screening.


---
**Page 86**
---

### ESS 4: Indigenous Peoples

At the regional level, the 14 participating countries are characterized by a diversity of customs, customary laws, norms, languages, and traditions meeting the broad GCF definition of indigenous peoples.

However, at the Programme intervention level, the communities are considered overall, considered to be homogenous in language, culture, and ethnicity. This means that Programme benefits or impacts will not adversely or differently affect any group within the GCF definition at the Programme site level.

#### Low Risk

Affected People are considered at a community level encompassing marginalized and vulnerable groups and individuals. The Programme is using FADs (Floating Aquaculture Devices) (FPC) using traditional customer processes is integrated into the ESMIP process. An IPP was not required for this Programme as FPC has been incorporated in the design of the FADs, which are a key activity design and through the required processes of the ESMIP.

This is considered low risk given the inherent cultural norms and practices that prevent any adverse impacts from taking place within communities without community support after approval.


### ESS 5: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

The Programme is using a FAD system designed by SPC and will be security anchored to a mooring (or up to 7km offshore in limited circumstances) in 3 participating countries water for some species. The FADs are also equipped with GPS tracking devices to enable the monitoring of the FADs and prevent FAD loss through damage from large shipping vessels). FADS are designed to be robust, durable, and resistant to corrosion by salt and sharks. FADS will also be installed with a GPS tracker to enable the monitoring of the FADs and prevent FAD loss (through damage from large shipping vessels).

No specific management plan is required for this standard as SPC FAD design has been specifically targeted at these environments of the PIs and recovery of lost FADS built into activity design. This is considered low risk given the small artisanal nature of the programme, and that the design by SPC have specially targeted their FAD design to be suitable for these environments.
