### G2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately |1 page)

| G2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately |1 page) |
|---|
| **G2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately |1 page)** |


---
**Page 88**
---

194. Annexes The Programme countries, apart from market sales, fisheries are generally considered a male domain (given men’s dominance as fishers). Although FADs improve the availability of fish*, they can only be directly used by those who have access to boats (mostly men). The Programme is designed to take a holistic view of the value tuna chain, which includes women. Women play an important role in all aspects of tuna fishing and processing. In many countries, women feature more prominently, and which is also directly impacted by a transition to tuna FAD fishing. Women’s roles in tuna fisheries are diverse and include: managing household resources, managing fishery resources, and local food availability. While women and other vulnerable groups have an important stake in fishery programmes, they often lack the necessary information on how these programmes will affect them, and are excluded or overlooked, limiting their access to the benefits and use of such programmes or projects. Such vulnerable groups can face barriers that prevent them from accessing resources and services to gain better equality.* They often have little if any to no say regarding decisions around the use of marine resources.

195. A Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan have been completed during Programme development to identify key gender-related issues and challenges, and to inform the design of the Programme. The assessment was delivered by Annex (B). Data were collected from primary and secondary sources through the 14 Programme countries. The gender assessment identified gender risks pertaining to:

- Discrimination against women in access to resources, especially for active fishers using the FADs and workers experiencing safety risks related to the processing and marketing of fish.
- Lack of achievement or consideration of women’s roles and contribution within the tuna fishery value chain (e.g., lack of inclusion of women in decision-making processes, lack of awareness of gender equality or opportunities for women).
- Inadequate infrastructure to address women’s specific needs within the fishery value chain, particularly with respect to safe and hygienic workplaces and processing/storage facilities.
- Discrimination against women in access to resources, especially for active fishers using the FADs and workers experiencing safety risks related to the processing and marketing of fish.
- Lack of achievement or consideration of women’s roles and contribution within the tuna fishery value chain (e.g., lack of inclusion of women in decision-making processes, lack of awareness of gender equality or opportunities for women).
- Inadequate infrastructure to address women’s specific needs within the fishery value chain, particularly with respect to safe and hygienic workplaces and processing/storage facilities.
- Discrimination against women in access to resources, especially for active fishers using the FADs and workers experiencing safety risks related to the processing and marketing of fish.

196. Given the results of the assessment, and noting that each country has different levels of infrastructure, resources, culture, and gender equality, and that gender equality goals vary, the following broad strategies are proposed to address gender disparities in all participating countries:

- Improve awareness of gender and related gender dimensions in all levels of the Regional Tuna Programme to ensure that women’s roles and contributions within the tuna fishery value chain are recognized. This includes gender dimensions and roles among relevant Programme stakeholders and the broader coastal community in the region.
- Support and encourage women’s participation in key local decision-making and implementation structures, processes and outcomes related to the Programme. This may include facilitating leadership skills development and providing training on gender equality.
- Collect sex-disaggregated data and information as a basis of planning and monitoring Programme outcomes, including gender dimensions.
- Link gender equity outcomes to the broader RTO outcomes, indicating how these contribute to the overall Programme goals and objectives. This will help ensure that gender equity is integrated into all aspects of project design and practices, and successes related to gender-responsive FAD deployment. This type of material is useful for the countries in the region to build a stronger coastal community to influence and support greater uptake of gender-responsive considerations in coastal FAD projects.

*See Chapter 1, in: Regional Report: Feasibility of scaling-up National Fish Aggregating Device (FAD) programs at all tuna fishing grounds in the Western Central Pacific Ocean. Prepared by the Secretariat of the Pacific Community for submission to the Green Climate Fund (GCF), October 2023. United Nations Children’s Fund, UNICEF, Australia, GIZ.
*See Chapter 1, in: Regional Report: Feasibility of scaling-up National Fish Aggregating Device (FAD) programs at all tuna fishing grounds in the Western Central Pacific Ocean. Prepared by the Secretariat of the Pacific Community for submission to the Green Climate Fund (GCF), October 2023. United Nations Children’s Fund, UNICEF, Australia, GIZ.
*Pacific handbook for gender equity and social inclusion in coastal fisheries and aquaculture: gender study and social inclusion handbook

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 86

## Provide sustained capacity building on post-harvest techniques, including processing and marketing, with a focus on women given their primary role in this part of the value chain: linking awareness, resources, and support services to reduce food loss and SEED programme initiatives.

### Action Areas
- **Inclusion:** Include women engaged in FAD fisheries and others along the value chain. While women are not currently present as FAD fishers in the region, training and capacity building will be open and rolling out to ensure that women have access to information on FAD fishing, including information about job opportunities accessible and open to women.
- **The Programme:** The GCF programme will support the development of a regional Action Group (AGF) to reflect the diversity of country realities, programme impacts, and related nuances. Each country will identify their priorities and aspirations in line with national gender equality goals in the context of FAD fishing. The Programmes can provide an opportunity for change the narrative about gender inequality in the fishing sector and support women’s economic empowerment.

## Gender Action Plan (GAP)

### 197
A Programme-level Gender Action Plan has been developed to encourage women's participation, representation, and inclusion within the FAD building supply chains (Annex 86). This will seek to discourage gender biases among Programme participants and beneficiaries and/or ensure gender inequalities are not exacerbated by the Programme. These activities will be designed in a way that ensures that the gender equality goals of the Programme can be mainstreamed in each country's context, responding to each's varied risks & opportunities. This encourages achievement of the GCF’s Gender Equality Indicators (GEI) and other indicators design with clear and quantifiable performance indicators. Some activities that the GAP recommends include:

- **Increasing awareness:** Increase awareness among women about FAD fishing and increase gender-awareness of men and women's roles and safety in the FAD tuna fishing supply chain.
- **Integration into national policies:** Integrate gender considerations into national policies, including workers for country-level FAD scale-up and national natural disaster response mechanisms.
- **Inclusion of women in all programme components:** Considerations, monitoring activities, training, and workshops will be designed to ensure that women are included as participants in the Programme.
- **Ensuring the Advanced Gendered Risk Reduction Mechanism (AGRIM) (regional & national) are gender-responsive:** The AGRIM will be used to identify and address gender-related risks and vulnerabilities related to GEI-concerns/concerns.
- **Providing training opportunities:** Providing training courses for women in coastal communities and urban communities to improve the preservation and hygiene of FAD-caught fish, including marketing opportunities and access to markets. This will also provide information on the challenges that women may face as fish processors/marketers.
- **Communicating the risks and benefits of FAD-caught tuna:** Gender is often recognized as a risk for women in household food preparation and decision-making.
- **Producing lessons learned documents from the Programme that showcase lessons and successes regarding gender equality.**

### 198
The GAP includes indicators with baseline and targets for each activity to track progress, which are aligned with the GCF GEI Indicators:

- Number of women who participate in Programme activities.
- Number of women & men who receive direct benefits from the Programme.
- Number of plans/strategies that integrate gender considerations.

### 199
The Regional AGF will have a GES specialist to cover environmental and social safeguards and gender. Their primary role will be to ensure that environmental and social risks are considered (section **[G1]**). At the national level, each national implementation team/unit will have national officers who will have a range of functions, including gender considerations. The regional AGF specialist will be responsible for ensuring that they are included in the terms of reference of the national technical specialists, and they will be supported by the regional GES specialist. This will ensure that gender is mainstreamed into all aspects of the Programme, including socializing the regional AGF with the national staff.

G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

201. Citi as the Entity will follow financial management and procurement for this Project in line with relevant, established CI policies and procedures, as set out in the Agreement in the Acquired Master Agreement (AMA) signed between Citi and the GCF.

202. Citi as the Entity is a GCF Accredited Entity and therefore, was deemed by the GCF to be compliant with its fiduciary standards. Furthermore, Citi has assessed SPIC and has determined that it has robust financial and procurement standards in place to meet Citi and GCF fiduciary standards.

Financial management

203. The AE, CI uses Uniti Business World software suite as its enterprise resource planning (ERP) system for financial accounting and monitoring in all its global operations. This system supports financial monitoring, reporting, human capital management, project management, and other functions of the organization. The AE maintains separate ledger accounts to separate and track income and expenditure by donor, project, and sub-donor. CI also provides a coding system that is used for tracking projects and their related expenditures in its ERP system. CI uses Uniti Business World software suite as its enterprise resource planning (ERP) system for financial accounting and monitoring. This system supports financial monitoring, reporting, human capital management, project management, and other functions of the organization and project. SPIC also has a coding system and established procedures for monitoring and reporting of projects.

204. SPIC and its sub-grantees will be required to submit periodic financial reports that will be systematically reviewed by the AE and CI, meeting accounting standards, including those set out in the GCF’s Procurement Policy, which includes standards.

205. Annual audits of the Project will be conducted by an independent external audit firm with experience internationally recognized auditing and accounting standards and furnished to the GCF according to Citi’s AMA.

206. Disbursements made to CI and SPIC will be recorded in the CI ledger account for this GCF Programme, which can be reconciled against the disbursement records maintained by CI and SPIC as per their internal financial systems in line with BPC financial standards.

207. CI and SPIC have procedures in place of controls in order to prevent the risk of payment practices, including MUI/FT (collusion), where a donor agent may apply for multiple OFTMA screens to all CI funding recipients. CI and SPIC also assess grant recipient PPI risks and include mitigation measures in its grant agreements. All AE staff will undergo training on the GCF’s Anti-Fraud Policy and Guidelines for Investigations, and all grievances related to payments (used by the EE and sub-grantees) must include language on compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices. The EE and sub-grantees are required to implement and maintain mitigation measures as part of project monitoring, including site visits throughout programme implementation.

208. Violations of PPIs, fraud, or financial misuse may be reported anonymously online or by telephone through the GCF’s hotline. Any such reports will be investigated in accordance with CI’s Anti-Fraud Policy & Guidelines for Investigations, and all grievances reported to SPIC will follow its internal procedures.

209. Procurement of goods and services is supported by a grant management system that is integrated across its donor management, accounting, and budgeting systems. This system provides notifications for due dates related to the procurement process. The system also tracks the status of procurements in real-time, including any audit findings (if applicable), and identifies risks so that project supervision is informed and documented. SPIC has a comprehensive Procurement Policy that sets out its policies on procurement and includes specific guidance and tools for SPIC application of these policies for the large-scale sub-granting of this Programme will be further defined in a Programme granting manual during implementation and verified by the AE during implementation.

Procurement

210. All procurement for this project must follow the Procurement Process Standards as defined in the CI Procurement Policy, which requires that all procurements of goods or services ensure the best value and comply with all donor funding requirements, including those set out in the GCF’s Procurement Policy, the GCF’s Anti-Fraud Policy & Code of Ethics and suite of anti-fraud policies. Citi’s procurement standards are in line with GCF fiduciary standards.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP259

Proposal name: Adapting tuna-dependent Pacific Island communities and economies to climate change

Accredited entity: Conservation International Foundation

Country: Cook Islands, Fiji, Kiribati, Marshall Islands, Micronesia (Federated States of), Nauru, Niue, Palau, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Solomon Islands, Tonga, Tuvalu, Vanuatu

Project size: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

### Strengths
- Tuna plays an important role in the Pacific, especially for small island states that are tuna-dependent on selling licences for tuna fishing.
- This proposal aims to increase efficiency and effectiveness; it proposes a strategic approach instead of a project-by-project approach. The programme covers 14 Pacific countries while addressing issues such as food security and stabilizing the benefits from the sale of tuna fisheries' licences at the same time.

### Points of caution
- The Pacific Community (SPC) is the sole implementing agency for this proposed programme.
- While it has never implemented stand-alone programmes in this region, SPC has implemented programmes of a larger volume in sum. Conservation International as an implementing partner has built the capacities of SPC and included mitigation measures to ensure the inclusion of sufficient staff in the programme design. However, this remains a risk which needs to be closely monitored during implementation.

The proposal and its activities are embedded in existing regional cooperation structures of the Pacific, with a proven track record of successful collaboration between Government SPC, a regional member/country-owned and governed organization that has strong ties with the Pacific Island states and a physical presence in many. This design increases the likelihood of long-term and sustainability of the measures even beyond the project lifetime.

The programme will sustainably strengthen national programmes for the deployment of fish aggregating devices (FADs). To


---
**Page 113**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### Project background

The GCF Regional Tuna Programme (RTP) supports 14 Pacific Small Island Developing States (SIDS), which are among the most climate-vulnerable countries in the world.

#### Ocean warming and acidification are causing coral reef ecosystems to degrade. Coastal communities heavily depend on coral reef fish for their protein intake. The projected decrease in the biomass of these species will have a significant impact on the nutritional security required to provide the recommended protein intake required for nutrition security.
- Ocean warming and acidification are causing coral reef ecosystems to degrade. Coastal communities heavily depend on coral reef fish for their protein intake. The projected decrease in the biomass of these species will have a significant impact on the nutritional security required to provide the recommended protein intake required for nutrition security.

#### Project aims
The project aims to build resilience in the tuna-dependent communities through two main components:
- Deploying food security by strengthening national government programmes to deploy nearshore, anchored FADs to increase access to tuna and other pelagic fish for coastal communities. This will also reduce the risk of loss or damage from extreme weather events at ports during trans-shipping to support the food security of urban populations; and
- Reducing economic risks to Pacific Island economies by delivering an advanced warning system that provides early warnings of El Niño and La Niña events, as well as forecasts of tuna redistribution that allow countries to adapt to climate-induced changes in the distribution of tuna biomass.

#### The total cost of the project is US$ 156.2 million, with a GCF investment of USD 107.4 million in grants.
- The total cost of the project is US$ 156.2 million, with a GCF investment of USD 107.4 million in grants.

#### Project impact
The project aims to directly benefit 389,000 people directly and 2,000 people indirectly.
- The project aims to directly benefit 389,000 people directly and 2,000 people indirectly.

#### Environmental and social safeguards (ESS) category for this project is C.


---
**Page 114**
---

2.2 Component-by-component analysis

The project is structured in two components and activities as follows:

### Component A: Adaptations to harness tuna to improve food availability of Pacific Island communities threatened by resources degraded by climate change (total cost: USD 52.7 million; GEF cost: USD 51.4 million in grants)

- **Activity 1.0**: To increase the availability of tuna and other marine resources for subsistence consumption, including through (i) strengthening and investing at scale in national programs through grants and technical assistance to support sustainable fishing practices by small-scale fishers; (ii) supporting communities to catch and process tuna and other pelagic species to improve access to dietary protein in rural and peri-urban communities; (iii) providing training for women in tuna populations going Omega-3; and (iv) to design and implement tuna-trasnshipment mechanisms that will provide urban and peri-urban communities with greater access by to catch from the tuna fishing industry.

### Activities for Outcome 1 are to provide technical and logistical support to strengthen national programs through grants and technical assistance to support sustainable fishing practices (activity 1.0); and to strengthen post-harvest practices and improve market opportunities for FAD-caught fish (activity 1.3).

- **Activity 2.0**: To implement strategies to develop more tuna-trasnshipped and unloaded by-catch and tuna to urban/peri-urban communities (activity 2.1); to strengthen and develop the capacity of national programs to support sustainable fishing practices by catch and tuna from trans-shipping and unloading operations into urban/peri-urban communities (activity 2.3).

### Component B: Adaptations to reduce risks to Pacific Island economies from climate-driven tuna redistribution (total cost USD 92.7 million; GEF cost: USD 87 million in grants)

- **Component B includes a set of activities to establish a region-wide AWS to gather and analyze data on tuna stocks, including the development of an early warning system that more accurately as stocks respond to ocean warming due to climate change. The evidence generated will support the Pacific Island countries (PICs) to maintain their access to shifting tuna stocks and maximize economic benefits regardless of the movement of fish. The data from the AWS will also inform national policies on fisheries management (harvest levels, conservation and management measures) made by the Western Central Pacific Fisheries Commission (WCPFC), which is responsible for managing tuna stocks in the region, including under impacts of climate change.

### Activities under component B are to develop and deliver an AWS for tuna redistribution (activity 3.1); to assess the impact of tuna biomass redistribution identified by the AWS on national economies at all levels (activity 3.2); and provide AWS-related training to national fisheries management authorities in the region (activity 3.3). The project will also provide information relating to impacts of climate change on the tuna resources historically caught in their EEZs; and provide information on the potential economic benefits from these historical catches, including the socioeconomic benefits previously derived from their tuna resources, regardless of climate-driven changes in the location of tuna stocks.

Project management (total cost: USD 19.8 million; GEF cost: USD 5.1 million in grants)

- This activity refers to the support provided to a dedicated project management unit (PMU) which is responsible for overseeing day-to-day execution activities under all components and activities of the project. The PMU also conducts regular monitoring and evaluation of all activities implemented by the project.


---
**Page 115**
---

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

- For millennia, coral reef fish have been a cornerstone of nutrition in the Pacific region.
- Climate change is expected to have severe implications on the availability of these fish, affecting the food security of coastal communities in the PICs.
    - The Pacific Islands Community RTF will help maintain fish availability for good nutrition by strengthening national fish aggregating device (FAD) programmes. Beneficial impacts include: 1) increasing catches and improving livelihoods; 2) expanding FAD programme activities to improve the distribution of by-catch and tuna are expected to increase food availability for 290,000 individuals, providing up to four additional tuna meals per month in urban and rural communities.
    - Climate-driven redistribution of tuna threatens the economies of Parties to the Nauru Agreement (PNA) and other PICs. The PNA has experienced a decline in tuna catches by an average of 20 per cent and fishing access fees by about USD 90 million annually by 2050, significantly affecting government resources for essential services.
    - Component B of the programme will support the development of national fisheries policy-making (investment options, national harvest strategies for fish resources) and to negotiate the right to maintain fishing access in areas outside their EEZs. By delivering improved data through the AWS, the programme will impact 2.5 million indirect beneficiaries.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

- The transition to making a connection of nutritious food availability for PICs marks a paradigm shift in the fisheries management pathway.
    - As climate change, reduced fishery resources, increasing shipping operations, and population growth threaten fish availability and increased reliance on low-quality processed foods, the programme aims at reducing high fish consumption by enhancing national FAD programmes and promoting trans-shipping of tuna to urban and coastal communities. This approach leverages tuna resources sustainably while improving health, livelihoods, and food security in PICs.
    - The programme will provide timely climate-related tuna distribution data, enabling PIs to adapt, secure adequate supplies, and improve the nutritional value of fish for their populations. It will also enable PICs to address climate-induced challenges, ensuring sustainable and nutritious food sources.

- The programme aims to replicate FAD technology and improve the utilization and distribution of tuna resources in PICs by strengthening national fisheries management through enhancing technical and financial capacity and strengthening institutions at various levels.
    - It will convert between 100 and 250 FADs per year, with a focus on small-scale fishers. This will be achieved via various training and awareness activities, and initially implement FAD interventions at selected sites, with the aim of scaling up to national level in subsequent years.
    - The programme-wide AWS system will cover all 18 programme countries, with data application mechanisms supporting multilateral and access agreements for tuna trade in zones. Ensuring lessons learned and improved practices are applied across the region, it aims to establish a programme-wide application and serve as a model for small island developing States in other regions.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: Medium to high

- The interventions planned under the RTF aim to generate positive economic, social, environmental, and gender-responsive developmental outcomes, divided into two main outcomes


---
**Page 116**
---

and two additional co-benefits. Outcomes 1 and 2 focus on boosting food availability by ensuring reliable access to tuna and other fish in a nutritious dietary protein, thus improving public health. Co-benefit 1 aims to enhance livelihoods by diversifying income sources and market opportunities for coastal communities through the development of value chains that support women's participation in various fishery activities. Co-benefit 2 involves enhancing ecosystem service sustainability by improving the management of oceanic tuna and other fish stocks across the Pacific.

The programme emphasizes gender equality within fishing supply chains, with three specific goals: ensuring equal access to resources; promoting equal opportunities for pre- and post-harvest tuna processing and marketing; raising awareness of gender roles in coastal fisheries communities. The programme also aims at leadership development related to the fishery value chain. The monitoring and evaluation framework will track co-benefits throughout the programme's implementation, coordinated by the heads of fisheries in each country and supported by the GCF.

The programme also contributes to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 2: zero hunger; SDG 5: gender equality; SDG 10: decent work and economic growth; SDG 12: responsible consumption and production; SDG 13: climate action; and SDG 14: life below water.

## Needs of the recipient

### Scale: High

A recent Notre Dame University Global Adaptation Initiative survey assessed nearly 200 countries' vulnerability to climate change across six life-supporting sectors: food, water, health, ecosystems, energy, and infrastructure. The study found that 148 out of these 200 countries fall within the most vulnerable quartile, indicating a high need for measures to combat climate change.

Many of these PIs are small economies lacking adequate financial resources to develop robust systems for well-coordinated national FAD programmes and a regional AWS for equitable sharing of economic benefits from fisheries. The programme aims to provide initial financial support to help PI countries establish or strengthen their national FAD programmes, launch the AWS, and help participating countries incorporate costs into national budgets for sustained long-term actors.

The project will need to strengthen institutions and capacity within PIs to understand and mitigate the impacts of climate change on coastal and urban populations. Knowledge about climate change is critical in developing appropriate adaptation strategies. The programme will address challenges such as understanding adverse fishing impacts, maximizing and equitably sharing socioeconomic benefits from fisheries, and promoting participation of women in fishery value chains. It will also support PIs to develop policies that protect marine and livelihoods.

All non-participating PIs are small island developing States, with limited economic development due to their small economies, remoteness from major markets, and economic non-diversification. Of the 13 countries, 9 are heavily reliant on tuna for economic well-being. Budgetary constraints in these countries make it difficult to implement national FAD programmes. Although a single programme cannot fully address these systemic issues, the RFP can contribute to overcoming some of them by providing funding and developing a regional AWS to help PIs collect licensing fees from industrial tuna fishing operations.

## Country ownership

### Scale: Medium to high


---
**Page 117**
---

29. Co-governance is fundamental to the RTP design and implementation. The innovative approaches to promoting environmental, economic, and social improvements in response to climate change effects on coastal fisheries require engagement with national and regional stakeholders at all stages of programme development and implementation. The engagement process began during conceptualization of the programme and development of the funding proposal and continues through implementation of the programme.

30. Regional and national governments, as well as communities and stakeholders living in the target countries has been crucial in shaping the RTP. This process allowed for a better understanding of the needs of the coastal communities to adapt and appreciate the funding nature for climate change adaptation. National stakeholders' views and requirements have significantly influenced the programme framework and activities.

31. The PICS is an innovative approach that integrates national fisheries management plans, and policies of PIGA, and is featured in several PFG Country Programmes. Of the 14 participating countries, nine are implementing PICS as a means to protect critical habitats in their nationally determined contributions. SPC, as the sole executing entity (EE), leverages its strong relationships and regional technical capacity to support sustainable development of tuna fisheries and climate change adaptation activities through collective action against climate change threats.

32. The programme is designed specifically to address the migratory nature of tuna and specific country needs. Strong country ownership is expected through co-financing activities, personnel commitment, and mainstreaming climate change adaptation into national policies. A strong monitoring framework has been developed, demonstrating the PICS's commitment to climate resilience. The programme's established structure allows for a phased approach in implementing the tuna component (approximately 38 per cent), ensuring comprehensive feedback and support for effective implementation of the tuna adaptation programme.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

33. The total cost for this project is USD 150.6 million, with GCF contribution of USD 107.4 million or approximately 71 per cent (USD 107.4 million x USD 92.9 million).

34. The economic analysis is conducted from the perspective of the national economies of the 14 participating countries. The analysis results that the FAD, by-catch, and AWS activities support a total annual benefit to the countries of approximately USD 508 million, representing significant benefits relative to cost. The financial analysis examines the financial implications for each of the four components of the programme: Component A, B, C, and D. Key aspects include maintaining national FAD programmes, improving by-catch distribution, and developing AWS. The analysis indicates minimal government expenditure will be needed post-programme implementation to maintain the benefits in all countries except for two countries, totalling approximately USD 4.0 million annually.

35. Component A provides a direct economic benefit of USD 12 per person annually to national fishers for around 790,000 people by 2035 at a cost of approximately USD 12 per person annually. Component B enables nine countries to prevent significant revenue losses estimated USD 96 million per year by 2040. This is achieved through the development and implementation of national FAD programmes within 5 to 14 years. Moreover, the programme strengthens SPC's capacity as a key scientific service provider for tuna fisheries.

36. Overall, the proposed regional programme is efficient in building resilience among the 14 countries that heavily depend on tuna.


---
**Page 118**
---

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The proposed programme is categorised as Category C for environmental and social impacts, in accordance with the GCF revised Environmental and Social Policy and the AF's accreditation level. Mitigation measures and social risks and impacts are expected from the deployment of the new FADs and the establishment of a small-scale works for FAD anchor blocks and concrete pads for the 20-foot/6-metre containers supporting the programme. The programme will not support infrastructure, construction activities, or any direct financing to industrial projects. The environmental and social safeguards plan (ESSP) includes an accountability and grievance redress mechanism.

The AF also developed a stakeholder engagement plan to ensure continuous stakeholder engagement throughout the project cycle. This plan is intended to serve as the channel for addressing any issues and complaints from communities and other stakeholders, including through the implementation of the ESMP, stakeholder engagement plan and Accountability-Grievance Redress Mechanism.

#### 4.1.5 GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESST (Indigenous Peoples)

The populations of the proposed project area are considered to be indigenous peoples under the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. These include the Marshalls Islands, Nauru, Palau, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Solomon Islands, Tonga, Tuvalu and Vanuatu. The policy applies to all projects that involve or may potentially impact on indigenous peoples at a community level and encompass marginalized and vulnerable groups and individuals. Best practice will be used and free, prior, and informed consent using traditional customary processes will be sought from the affected communities. An Indigenous Peoples Policy was not required for this programme as free, prior, and informed consent has been obtained through the ESMP. This process will ensure the project is low risk given that the proposed activities are consistent with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and will not take place within communities without community support and/or approval.

Additionally, the AF will continue to apply its policies (in particular its ESST) in a manner that meets the GCF requirements and best practices. The AF may also be required to seek further guidance from the Secretariat as outlined in the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and associated guidelines regarding development of the programme. In line with its roles and functions, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy will continue to provide technical assistance and support to the AF in order to encourage shared learning good practices and success stories with the Advisory Group.

### Sex exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment (SEAH)

The AF has identified SEAH risks associated with the programme's activities including training, especially those focused on women, who play a primary role in the fishery value chain, and the potential for inappropriate relationships between staff and beneficiaries. The risk assessment undertaken assessed these risks to be at a low level. To address these risks, the AE has proposed the following measures: (i) the development of a SEAH policy; (ii) the implementation of a SEAH training plan for personnel, such as national and PMU staff working in the programme, and contractual requirements that all personnel must complete the training; and (iii) the establishment of a SEAH risk analyser will be undertaken as part of the national programme investigation and documentation of gender risks. The AE will undertake further analysis with beneficiaries and survivors to identify the most local level. Examples of such country-level referral services are listed in the ESMP. The programme-level grievance redress mechanism will


---
**Page 119**
---

# Gender policy

## 4.2 Gender policy

### 41. The AIF provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy.

The AIF has conducted a review of existing Pacific Island countries’ national gender and gender studies documents, including interviews and consultations with a selection of fishing supply chain stakeholders in each country. The review found that while many PIsCs have adopted policies aimed at fostering an enabling environment that promotes gender equality, there is limited evidence of implementation agreements or monitoring. Despite the gender equality efforts put in place by the PIsCs, studies indicate disparities compounded by social cultural norms and slow implementation of policy and study recommendations. These include a lack of awareness about women’s roles within their own working conditions and environments, a lack of employment and personal security, and gender-based violence in fishing communities.

### 43. The gender assessment includes findings on the roles, barriers and opportunities for women and men in Pacific fish value chains. The fishing sector in PIsCs is largely dominated by men, while women are often excluded from decision-making processes. Although some countries have rules that keep women’s contribution invisible yet there is great potential in expanding their activities within the fisheries sector. Women can contribute to the sustainability of fisheries as they see immense opportunities to enhance gender equality and safer working spaces within the fish value chain. The assessment also highlights the importance of engaging men in promoting women's contributions, and enhancing collaboration between women and men in fisheries and the sector. There is a need for raising awareness about gender issues among all actors involved in the value chains actors and developing mechanisms to address gender-related risks in the programme and value chain.

### 45. The AIF has developed a gender action plan (GAP) consisting of activities, performance indicators, baseline, targets, budget, timelines and responsibilities. The GAP includes the following actions that will be implemented during the project period: raising awareness on women dimensions of the FAD fishery value chain and women’s roles within the fishery; considering women fishers on needs analysis for improved vessel safety; training women fishers in basic skills such as boat handling, navigation and fishing techniques; and improving market opportunities. The GAP foresees training courses for women in coastal and urban areas to improve their knowledge of the value chain, including processing, packaging, bottling and canning of fish, and to improve hygiene and safe working conditions for sellers of fish, especially women. The AIF will ensure that the gender focal person (GFP) will oversee the implementation of the GAP in coordination with a national-level officer who will be responsible on the country’s behalf for the implementation of the GCF.

### 46. Each project will identify its priorities and actions to contribute to advancing the national gender equality goals in the context of this programme. The AIF is advised to ensure that additional and more detailed information is provided by each PIcS to enable the GCF Secretariat to better assess specific gender contexts and identify actions that are relevant to addressing the needs and priorities of women and men in the fishery value chain in the context of national projects.

**Page 120**

# Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

## Accreditation/Executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

### Project-specific execution risks (low risk)

#### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF concentration risk remains within the monitoring thresholds of the Risk Appetite Statement in terms of results areas, single proposed project, and geographical diversification.

## Compliance risk (medium risk)

As the proposed project, spanning 14 countries and involving a multitude of stakeholders—including CI as AE, SPC as EE, as well as government agencies and vendors—presents a high level of complexity due to the diversity in national laws, cultures, levels of awareness, and geographical diversity, there exists an elevated risk of potential non-compliance with GCF’s AMU/CFT and PP policies. While the probability of these risks is assessed by CI as low, their impact could be significant if they materialize.

### Implementation risks (medium risk)

In terms of these risks, CI as AE and SPC as EE have implemented a variety of controls to mitigate PP and ML/FT risks, aligned with GCF regulatory requirements. CI ensures all program staff undergo AMU/CFT training, covering front-end risk flags and reporting protocols, and mandates that grant recipients report suspicious transactions through the GCF Suspicious Transaction Hotline. Additionally, SPC employs an AMU/CFT policy to screen all fund recipients against international sanctions lists and other relevant databases. The GCF Suspicious Transaction Hotline and SPC’s complaint mechanisms allow all stakeholders to anonymously report concerns about potential non-compliance with GCF policies. These controls are further strengthened by project monitoring, including site visits and in-depth due diligence processes.

Considering the established mitigation strategies and the project's compliance framework, the residual risk level for this initiative is assessed as Medium.


# Fiduciary

The Regional Tuna Programme is spearheaded by CI as the AE and executed by SPC as the EE. CI is responsible for programme oversight, monitoring, and fund disbursement, while SPC manages programme execution, including financial reporting, subgrant administration, and


---
**Page 121**
---

### Results monitoring and reporting

This is an adaptation project that will contribute to GCF adaptation results areas 1 and 2. The project is expected to reach 790,000 direct beneficiaries, and around 2.5 million indirect beneficiaries across 14 countries in the Pacific region.

The project’s funding proposal under the project were refined over four rounds of review and engagement with the AE. Specifically, changes were made to the project Climate Rationale and Project Activities, which are now fully coherent, and linked to the climate rationale and activities proposed under the project.

The project logical framework was also refined after several rounds of review to ensure that the proposed indicators were aligned with the logic behind the project’s activities. In particular, the AE was provided feedback to ensure that beneficiary targets at the level of the adaptation activities are not double-counted or misaligned with other GCF projects or double counting of beneficiaries. It is positive to note that the AE has defined project-level outcome and output indicators, in addition to the indicators against different GCF results areas.

The revised funding proposal was also aligned with the updated Implementation Timetable, Monitoring and Evaluation Plans, and beneficiary calculations. Guidance provided by the AE on how to calculate the number of indirect beneficiaries for indirect beneficiaries, are realistic and aligned with the proposed project rationale and activities.

The AE has responded positively to guidance and refined the funding proposal accordingly.


### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 13 July 2017, and became effective on 17 August 2017. which was amended and restated pursuant to a first amendment agreement dated 15 August 2022, and which became effective on 17 August 2022 (the “AMA”).

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals, and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme.


---
**Page 122**
---

### The proposed programme will be implemented in fourteen host countries. The GCF has signed bilateral agreements on privileges and immunities with nine of these host countries, namely Cook Islands, Kiribati, Federated States of Micronesia, Niue, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Tonga, Tuvalu, and Vanuatu. However, the agreement concluded by the GCF with the Solomon Islands is not effective yet. In the case of the six host countries, namely Fiji, Marshall Islands, Palau, Nauru, and the Federated States of Micronesia, the GCF is not provided privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, which risks need to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of the GCF to make use of its proceeds for projects and programmes in these countries may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.

### To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Signature of a bilateral agreement on a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

## Proposal name: Adapting tuna-dependent Pacific Island communities and economies to climate change

### Accredited entity: Conservation International Foundation

### Project size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview

The proposed project, the Adapting Tuna-Dependent Pacific Island Communities and Economies to Climate Change, or Regional Tuna Program (RTP), has a total cost of USD 156.8 million, including co-financing from the donor community. The co-financing amounts to USD 93.38 million. This co-financing reflects a mix of cash and in-kind contributions, combining both public and private sector funding. A breakdown is as follows:

- USD 224,000 (in kind contributions) from the Fiji Islands Fisheries Association (FIFA), representing access to vessels equipped with suitable instruments for recording tuna data and physical oceanography parameters in support activity 3.1.
- USD 578,960 (in-kind contributions) from the Pacific Island Countries, including USD 93,710,655 for staff costs (for activities 1.1.2, 1.1.3, 3.1, 3.2, 3.3), monitoring and evaluation, project management costs, USD 124,750 for equipment and supplies for Activity 3.1, and USD 689,555 for the Pacific Islands Forum Secretariat (PIFS) activity 11.
- USD 1,262,493 from the Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organisation (CSIRO), representing in-kind contributions supporting the implementation of Activities 2.1 and 3.1, and USD 687,213 for project management costs.
- USD 805,776 (in kind contributions) from the Pacific Islands Fishery Forum Agency (PIFFA), representing access to vessels equipped with suitable instruments for recording tuna data in support activity 3.1.

USD 12,940,000 from the Office of the Parties to the Nauru Agreement (PNAO) and private-sector industrial fishing companies will provide in-kind co-financing support to the Adapting Tuna-Dependent Pacific Island Communities and Economies to Climate Change Program activities in Activity 3.1 by providing observer services, vessel time, and data.

The proposed project has an implementation period of seven years, while its total lifespan is expected to be at least ten years with a potential for long-term sustainability.

Assuming the project is successful, it will have 790,956 direct beneficiaries (50 per cent male, 50 per cent female). It will directly benefit 5.8 per cent of the region's projected total


---
**Page 124**
---

### Theory of change

The RFP theory of change is that "If Pacific Island governments are supported to implement effective programmes for assisting coastal and urban communities to obtain access to tuna resources through improved fishery management and climate-driven redistribution of tuna; THEN Pacific Island nations will be transformed to become more resilient to the impacts of climate change on tuna."

#### 5. The funding proposal has two major outcomes.

The first is improved food availability for vulnerable communities in participating countries (Outcome 1) and the second is strengthened capacity of coastal and urban communities to obtain access to tuna resources through improved fishery management and climate-driven redistribution of tuna (Outcome 2). Outcome 1 will be achieved via two strategic interventions: (a) technical and logistical support, including equipment, to implement safe-at-sea initiatives; and (b) strengthening development of post-harvest practices and improving market opportunities for FAD-caught fish. Outcome 2 will be achieved through three strategies to deliver more trans-shipped-and-unloaded by-catch tuna to urban and peri-urban rural communities and (c) strengthen and develop pilot projects that demonstrate the impact of market-based operations on by-catch and tuna from trans-shipping and unloading operations to urban and peri-urban communities.

### 1.2 Impact potential

Scale: Medium to High

For many generations, coral reef fish have been a cornerstone of nutrition in the Pacific Island region. However, the combined impacts of growing populations and climate change on


---
**Page 125**
---

reel and reef resident fish populations are expected to have severe implications for the food availability of coastal communities in PICs. Climate change is causing warming and acidification of the ocean, changing the suitability of areas for specific fish species, and is also interacting with other stressors such as overfishing and pollution. This is particularly concerning for coral reefs, which are important fish breeding grounds. Ocean warming is causing tuna to migrate towards cooler waters in some regions, while climate change is affecting the timing of spawning seasons and ecosystems are becoming more vulnerable to the cascading effects of climate change.

To address this challenge, Component A of the RTF will assist growing Pacific Island populations with access to sustainable fisheries through the development and implementation of FAD programmes, directly improving food availability for 560,000 individuals. FADs enable coastal communities in PICs to fish in deeper waters where tuna are concentrated, providing a new fishing tool that will be progressively more important in maintaining access to fish for good nutrition as coral reefs are degraded by climate change and populations continue to grow.

The programme is expected to result in an increase of 100,000 tonnes of tuna landings per year. The increased catch and tuna are expected to increase food availability for 290,000 individuals by providing up to four additional meals per week. This will be achieved through the deployment of 330 new FADs, with transshipping of purse-seine catches occurs. Unloading operations by commercial longliners in some ports (e.g. Honiara, Majuro, Nuku Alofa, Port Vila and Suva) also offer the potential to supplement food availability for up to an additional 270,000 individuals through increased local consumption, albeit at a lower level than the offloading of by-catch from purse-seine vessels.

Currently, there are 15 countries in the Pacific region that have signed the Nauru Agreement member countries and the Cook Islands. By 2030 under Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 4.5, preliminary modelling indicates that the climate change impacts on tuna landings will be significant. The model predicts a 70 per cent reduction in purse-seine catch from the combined exclusive economic zones (EEZs) of these nine countries by 2030 compared to baseline levels. This is equivalent to an average of about USD 5 million per year (range USD 40–140 million) at today's prices. Even under a more optimistic scenario, RCP 6.0, the model predicts a reduction in tuna landings by around 20 per cent compared to baseline levels and an annual loss of about USD 3 million per year. Such significant reduction in government finance will have direct impacts on vulnerable communities through reduced revenue from fishing taxes, reduced funding for priority sectors (i.e. health, education, disaster preparedness and post-disaster recovery, and other related essential social services). Component B of the programme will empower countries to use improved fisheries management tools and practices (e.g., sustainable catch limits, selective harvest strategies for fish resources) and to negotiate for the right to retain access to tuna stocks in their EEZs. By providing FADs to communities that are already using data from the AWS, the programme will impact 2.5 million indirect beneficiaries.

It is hoped that the RTF will deliver on the following targets in the 14 participating countries by 2030:

- Deployment of 333 new operational FADs, with replacement of 195 of the FADs deployed during years one to three and an additional 138 FADs deployed over these three years, meaning that FADs deployed during years one to three will need to be replaced during years four to seven. Thus, a total of 528 FADs will be constructed and deployed;
- Training of approximately 600 people in safe and effective FAD fishing methods;


---
**Page 126**
---

## (a) Prevention of between 9 million and 10 million nutrition meals (150 grams for adults and 75 grams for children) for 560,000 people across the region each year through strengthened national FAD programmes. Approximately 290,000 people will be provided with improved access to fish from FADs.

## (b) Improvement of livelihoods of small-scale users through the 333 FADs deployed, and their members of households, totalling 15,000 people (co-benefits).

## (c) Training of 400 representatives of coastal communities in post-harvest preservation methods to apply to tuna and other fish species for their household totals, 2,000 individuals, and establishing a bank of peer-to-peer learning.

## (d) Opportunities for an additional 130 people to sell the increased volume of by-catch in urban communities, improving the livelihoods of these households, totalling 650 individuals (co-benefits).

## (e) Creation or strengthening of 10 micro, small or medium enterprises (MSMEs) to apply post-harvest preservation and processing methods to by-catch in 10 of the countries where FADs are deployed.

## (f) Provision – for about 670,000 people – of information on climate change impacts on coral reef fish and the need to consume tuna as an alternative to reef-associated fish resources.

## (g) Development of improved meteorological and natural disaster forecasts for about 2.5 million people in coastal communities who are at risk from extreme weather events and hazardous sea conditions when planning fishing trips to FADs, and timely warnings that are easy for communities to receive will benefit communities within 1 km of the coast.

The AWS-linked interventions will provide short-term coping strategies, such as inputs for fishers (e.g. nets, lines) and information on climate change impacts at the EEZ level to support quick responses and (ii) operational planning and facilitation of near-term decision-making for fishing activities and resource management (e.g. harvest strategies).

The AWS-linked interventions will provide long-term coping strategies through a platform of climate change impacts, where it will inform strategic adaptations to long-term changes in tuna distribution and abundance. The platform will also include an early warning system with data to help them plan for shifts in fishing revenues and licence structures over time.

The ITPF will greatly enhance the capacity of PIC government-mandated entities (e.g. fisheries management authorities) to manage FADs, through improved information systems and broader operations. The planned allocation of government budget to support FADS and the AWS potentially demonstrates how lessons learned about climate-related initiatives can be replicated across the whole of government.


---
**Page 127**
---

1. **Tuna stock mapping** would be greatly enhanced by using more accurate and close-kine mark-recapture techniques. The AWS would accurately identify and track tuna stocks, facilitating precise monitoring of climate-induced shifts in biomass.

20) See funding proposal annex 26-G Tuna Technical Studies V3.


---
**Page 128**
---

Enhanced tuna-related climate models. Improvements to the SEAFOOD model would increase spatial resolution and incorporate climate variables such as ocean warming and prey dynamics, offering more accurate predictions of tuna distribution under changing conditions.

Industry collaboration. Partnering with industrial fishing companies for data collection would validate global climate models and deepen understanding of how tuna prey availability is affected by climate change.

Operationalizing the AWS. Conducting regular assessments to forecast sustainable tuna catch levels within PIC EEZs and the high seas would inform the refinement of WCPFC conservation measures, including the establishment of new areas and zones.

Knowledge-sharing about the AWS. This would foster global knowledge-sharing and deliberations on how best to manage tuna resources in a changing climate. Through the AWS would be shared during WCPFC deliberations, contributing to a collective understanding and responsibility among all members to manage tuna resources effectively under climate change.

This comprehensive approach would not only transform fisheries management in the Pacific but also serve as a model for integrating climate resilience into fisheries governance globally.

The AWS would be a transformative tool that enabled PICs to negotiate effectively for equitable access to tuna stocks, while simultaneously enhancing their negotiation capacities. It would ensure PICs are better positioned to retain economic benefits from tuna fisheries amid climate change.

The TEFAP believes that the paradigms shift potential as follows:

As a multi-country initiative, the program's success hinges on strong collaboration among PICs. Weak or problematic cooperation would pose a significant risk to achieving the program’s goals and objectives. The TEFAP is committed to working with all parties to maintain unity and alignment among stakeholders.

The TEFAP believes that there is also a perceived risk that the ETP initiatives be perceived as an attempt by some countries to shape their approach to climate adaptation. Transformational adaptation would extend beyond merely maintaining tuna access to bolster terrestrial food security, which is critical for long-term food security amid the challenges of climate change. To mitigate this risk, TEFAP will work with all parties to ensure that the ETP initiatives are tailored to each country. These dialogues could explore additional transformational adaptation strategies that involve economic system shifts, such as developing marinecrop or aquaculture sectors.

Overall, the paradigms shift potential is assessed as medium to high.


### Sustainable development potential

The funding-proposal ratio contributes several of the SDGs, including but not limited to the following: SDG 1 (Climate Action), which "intrinsically linked to all 16 of the other goals of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development" (UN, 2015); SDG 2 (Zero Hunger); SDG 3 (Good Health and Well-being); SDG 4 (Quality Education); SDG 5 (Gender Equality); SDG 8 (Decent Work and Economic Growth); SDG 9 (Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure); SDG 10 (Reduced Inequality); and SDG 17 (Partnerships for the Goals).

Climate Action. Available at www.un.org/sustainabledevelopment/climate-action

**Page 129**
---

# Socioeconomic co-benefits

The potential for the development of business value chains for the tuna and by-catch from FADs and trans-shipments, including its processing and distribution to local markets, can provide economic opportunities for small-scale local communities. The food processing of this by-catch and tuna will enable increased resilience of local livelihoods in terms of food security, nutrition, and income generation, boosting household incomes and creating economic opportunities for both rural and urban populations.

## Environmental co-benefits

While the funding proposal mentions the impact of global high GHG (greenhouse gas) emissions on the ocean and coral reefs, it does not consider GHG mitigation strategies. The potential impacts of climate change, differentiated by type of fishing activity, or according to the fuel used for each type of fishing operation, could have been considered in the proposal. In addition, the proposal does not address mitigation of GHG emissions from fishing (longline, purse seine and coastal), processing (cold chain logistics, refrigeration, etc.), and transport (fuel consumption). The proposal needs to address GHG emissions to prevent unwanted consequences, or maladaptation. All of these areas could have offered opportunities to attract additional carbon or mitigation finance in support of the tuna sector.

## Gender-sensitive development impact

The funding proposal promotes gender-responsive fisheries management as a key element of its proposed strategy for fostering greater gender equality across fisheries value chains. It also proposes opportunities for women to participate in and benefit from fisheries-related activities, such as enhancing their role in decision-making processes within the value chain by improving processing and marketing, and raising awareness of gender dimensions in coastal communities managing tuna resources. The proposal could have provided more information on how it is addressing gender issues in decision-making processes within the value chain. By integrating gender considerations into fisheries management strategies, women can gain greater access to fishing opportunities, increased household incomes and more inclusive economic opportunities, ensuring that women have equitable access to the benefits of sustainable fisheries development.

## Overall

Overall, the sustainable development potential is assessed as medium.


---
**Page 130**
---

# Country ownership

## Scale: Medium to High

### 1.6 Country ownership

The RFP is a multi-country initiative. For it to be successful, there must be strong country-level commitment and capacity of the delivery partners to bring their communities to fruition; failure to fully achieve this is the biggest risk. The RFP is supported by a robust team of AE, including representatives from all 15 Pacific Island countries.

### Conservation International (CI)

Serving as the international AE, CI has been active in Pacific Ocean conservation and sustainability for over 30 years. Notable initiatives include the Climate Change Adaptation Program (2018–2024) and the Pacific-Europ Union Marine Partnership programme (2018-2025). CI also leads projects aimed at improving tuna fisheries management in the Western and Central Pacific Ocean, such as enhancing longline tuna fisheries in Fiji and New Caledonia.

### Secretariat of the Pacific Community (SPC)

As the AE for SPC and a direct member entity, SPC is an intergovernmental organization that serves 26 Pacific island countries. Established in 1947 to address the development and the scientific and technical needs of the Pacific region, SPC provides policy advice on fisheries management and sustainable tuna consumption. Its existing memorandums of understanding with regional fisheries management organizations facilitate effective coordination and communication regarding tuna stock status and rebuilding.

### Pacific Islands Forum Fisheries Agency (FFA)

As a programme implementing partner, FFA is an intergovernmental organization established in 1982 under the Pacific Islands Forum Agreement (PIF) and the PIF Secretariat Act of 1982. FFA has provided economic, legal, fisheries management and development assistance to its 15 member Pacific Island countries and a single territory. Its mandate includes assisting member states with sustainable tuna fishing practices in line with international agreements beyond PIC EEZs. The FFA is involved in this programme as also FFA members.

### Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organisation (CSIRO)

The national science agency of Australia, CSIRO, will assist SPC in implementing Activities 2.1 and 3.1. Renowned for extensive ocean research and participation in regional and international


---
**Page 131**
---

scientific collaborations, particularly in sustainable fisheries, CSIRO employs advanced methods for stock assessment and management, focusing on tuna species. In this collaboration, CSIRO will provide updated projections on how to address protein needs through by-catch and tuna reserves. The project will also assess the impacts of climate change on tuna populations using genomic-based fisheries management technologies, and develop advanced models to predict future trends.

### Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). Through its Subregional Office for the Pacific Islands (FAD-SAP), FAO will provide technical support for Activity 2.1. This includes capacity building in areas such as aquaculture, fisheries, agriculture, rural development and safety-of-sea initiatives. It offers extensive technical expertise on fishery management and conservation, including the application of scientific knowledge based on fishing vessel data, supported by professional fisheries staff and ongoing regional fisheries programmes. The FAO will also assist with the implementation of safe refrigeration and related activities within the project.

### Office of the Parties to the Nauru Agreement (PNA). PNAo will provide technical services in areas such as aquaculture, fisheries, agriculture, rural development, and sustainable tuna purse-seine fishing. PNAo will assist in updating regional observer and port programmes to estimate locally rediced tuna by catch. Additionally, PNAo will utilize the AWS to support the implementation of the Nauru Agreement (NAU). The lack of a constitution limits the body's ability to receive external funds, it will contribute as a co-financer of the project.

42. These organizations, with their extensive experience and demonstrated collaborative efforts in the Pacific region, form a credible and reliable team to implement the RFP effectively.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The TEAP recommends this funding proposal for approval by the Board.

The proposed project is well prepared and documented. Its interventions cover the short-, medium- and long-term needs of the PIGs in relation to the impacts of climate change on the countries' primary economic resource - tuna. The AIF, the AB, and the implementing partners


---
**Page 133**
---

show governing relationships with the PICs, and it is hoped that the PICs will maintain their collaboration with the AE, IAE and implementing partners.

54. To enhance the impact potential, paradigms shift potential and effectiveness of the proposed project's interventions, the iTPC recommends the following to the AIE:

* **Creating resilient ecosystems:** FADs should be designed for resilience. Options include dynamic mooring techniques, choice of species, floating systems, redundant anchoring mechanisms, and careful placement to avoid shallow water.

56. Facilitating transformative adaptation dialogues. Include budgeted national dialogues on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction to develop specific transformative adaptation strategies. Where feasible, integrate these strategies into project implementation or support the development for inclusion in adaptation plans.

* **Capacity building and stakeholder engagement:** Conduct tailored workshops and training programs to address specific stakeholder needs. Improve preparedness with real-time decision-making tools, early warning systems, and information sharing opportunities for participation in policy dialogue, decision-making, and training on sustainable practices and local adaptation. Enhance governance and enforcement by providing resources and training to local governments and agencies to improve coordination between national and regional agencies.

* **Promoting business innovation:** Develop incentives for PICs to adopt sustainable practices. Support MSMEs in diversifying income streams through mariculture, aquaculture and/or value-added processing.


---

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP259)

## Proposal name:
Adapting tuna-dependent Pacific Island communities and economies to climate change

## Accredited entity:
Conservation International Foundation

## Countries:
Cook Islands, Fiji, Kiribati, Marshall Islands, Micronesia (Federated States of), Nauru, Palau, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Solomon Islands, Tonga, Tuvalu and Vanuatu

## Project size:
Medium

### Impact potential - Medium to High
CI notes ITAP’s positive assessment with thanks.
CI acknowledges the risk of limited PIC stakeholder capacity to effectively manage climate change adaptation projects. CI will address this by strengthening the implementation approach of national FAD programmes (Sub-activity 1.1d), improving local skills to make better use of tuna and bycatch transshipped/unloaded by industrial tuna fleets (Activities 2.3 and 2.5), embedding gender considerations in all activities, and developing a market-based approach for the export of enatail fish for food security (Sub-activity 1.3d), and building capacity to understand/utilize AFA data and tools (Activity 4.6).

The Programme seeks to address the risk of major cycles by incorporating rapid FAD replacement in the most cyclone-prone Participating Countries.

### Paradigm shift potential - Medium to High
CI notes ITAP’s positive assessment with thanks.
The identified risks to achievement of paradigm shift are well noted. Collaboration between PICs is essential for successful implementation through the Programme's governance structure, transparent information sharing, and by implementation led by SPC – the Participating Countries' technical organisation with extensive experience working on fisheries-related issues.

The ITAP recommendation to support national dialogues in each country to support additional adaptation measures that are not currently being implemented include interventions like those currently led by SPC’s FAME Division to develop small pond aquaculture to increase access to fish in rural communities.

### Sustainable development potential - Medium
CI notes ITAP’s positive assessment with thanks.
ITAP's recognition of the sustainable development potential across SDGs is appreciated, as well as the achievement of the Programme’s emphasis on socio-economic benefits and focus on gender-responsive development.


---
**Page 135**
---

The content on environmental co-benefits are well received. CI notes that this Programme responds to national priorities by focusing on climate-adaptive support to vulnerable communities and national economies - reducing the GHG emissions profile of industrial tuna fleets is outside the Programme's mandate. However, the Programme will develop small vessel designs that improve fuel-efficiency and robust emissions.

Needs of the recipients - Medium to High

CI notes ITAP’s positive assessment and appreciates the accurate summary of how the Programme design was genuinely country-led and that the organizations involved in Programme implementation are well placed to effectively interact with and serve the Participating Countries. Ongoing engagement with and guidance from countries during implementation will take place through regular meetings such as those held at SPC’s Commission, SPC’s Regional Fisheries meetings, and FFA’s Forum Fisheries Committee meetings.

The ITAP identified risk of limited participation / consultation with numerous other stakeholders during Programme design. This risk has been minimized through the extensive consultations planned by Programme proponents and captured in the Stakeholder Engagement Plan.

Country ownership - Medium to High

CI notes ITAP’s positive assessment with thanks.

Efficiency and effectiveness - Medium to High

CI notes ITAP’s positive assessment with thanks.

CI recognizes the importance of collaboration between Participating Countries, and with ongoing and planned interventions in the region. CI will support these efforts during implementation to avoid duplication and build complementarity.

CI thanks ITAP for its review of SPC’s existing exit strategy, which was a significant focus of Programme design.

CI also notes the risks of cyclones to FAD contractors and potential impacts to beneficiary support. These risks can be mitigated by rapid re-deployment of damaged FADs and through capacity building of national FAD programs to effectively respond to climate-driven natural disasters.

Overall results from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

CI thanks ITAP for thorough review of the Funding Proposal package and the overall positive assessment of the Programme. The assessment document provides an excellent summary of the Programme against the GCF’s six investment criteria.

CI sincerely appreciates ITAP’s recommendation to the GCF Board to approve this Funding Proposal.

**Page 136**
---

CI also notes and welcomes (ITAP)'s recommendations on Programme design and is confident that the points raised will be effectively addressed during implementation.
