# Introduction

## Component A: Strengthening National Programmes to Enhance Fish Aggregating Devices (FADs)

### Objectives:
- **Activity 1.1:** Provide technical and logistical support to strengthen National FAD programmes.
- **Activity 1.2:** Align national FAD-sea-ice activities with regional priorities.
- **Activity 1.3:** Strengthen post-harvest practices and improve market opportunities for FAD-caught fish.

### Success Criteria:
- Increased access to tuna and other pelagic fish for coastal communities and vulnerable households.

## Component B: Adaptation to Reduce Risks from Climate Change

### Objectives:
- **Activity 2.1:** Develop a Climate Information System (CIS) to provide the data needed to more accurately determine future abundance and distribution of tuna as the fish respond to ocean warming.
- **Activity 2.2:** Facilitate the development of regional agreements, designed to enable tuna-dependent Pacific Island Countries (PICs) to maintain their access rights to shifting tuna stocks and the associated economic benefits.

### Gender Action Plan (GAP)
- Informed by the Gender Assessment and designed to support women and men in sharing Programmes for gender equality.
- Activities will be gender responsive, defined as: "identifying and understanding gender gaps and biases; then acting on them; developing and implementing actions to overcome challenges and barriers toward improving and achieving gender equity."


---
**Page 139**
---

- **Avoiding gender-related risks** and promoting gender equality in the Programme will be through capacity building and institutional development, facilitating gender analysis of FADs and women's participation, and capturing and reporting on gender outcomes through the Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Plan.
- Noting that each country has different levels of infrastructure, resources, cultural norms and levels of commitment to gender equality goals, the following broad strategies are proposed to address gender disparities and advance equity & equality in the Programme’s activities:
  - **Awareness raising**: Raising awareness of gender and related gender dimensions on all levels of the Programme to ensure that women's voices are heard in, and benefit equitably and meaningfully from, the Programme's outputs, outcomes, and activities. This includes raising awareness on gender dimensions in the coastal communities who manage FADs and women’s roles.
  - **Capacity & skills strengthening**: Support and encourage women’s participation in practical decision-making and implementation of programme activities that are meaningful to them. This includes technical capacity building on post-harvest techniques, processing, marketing, and value addition; financial literacy training; and gender-based violence (GBV) awareness raising, as well as capacity building on leadership and management skills and peer mentoring for women in the local fishery sector.
  - **Contribute to knowledge sharing**: Link gender equality outcomes to the broader Programme outcomes, indicating how these contribute to the achievement of the Programme’s goals. This includes sharing lessons learned from the implementation of GBV awareness raising activities; good practices and success stories related to gender-responsive FAD deployment. This type of material is useful for the countries, the region, and the broader fishery FAD community to influence and support greater uptake of gender-responsive considerations.
  - **Track and report on efforts**: Mainstream gender in the Monitoring and Evaluation Plan of the Programme through the collection of sex-disaggregated data and information as a basis for evaluating and monitoring gender-related Programme outcomes and its effectiveness in achieving gender equality goals.
- **Address potential gender risks**: Link resources and support systems at the national level to reduce and respond to gender-based violence (GBV) and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH) project-related risks. Please also note both the GBV and SEAH incident reporting procedures and the AE’s role in responding to GBV and SEAH incidents (training provide by investigator Qualification Training Scheme), and the AE is prepared to lead the investigation if a GBV incident escalates to the AE level.
- **Details on gender mainstreaming**: Implement gender-inclusive approaches to engage FAD fishers and others along the value chain. While women are not well represented as FAD fishers in the region, training and capacity building should still be open to individuals who wish to participate in FADs. Support awareness on project activities and opportunities and ensure it is accessible and open to women, is therefore important.
- **Scope of the Gender Action Plan**: This Gender Action Plan is designed to set out regional priorities and actions to which the national programmes will align and contribute, recognizing the need for national-level tailoring and variety based on context. It is recommended

that national-level workshops be designed to a gender responsive manner to guide country-specific activities and ensure alignment with and response to, the original GAP. To further, once the exact locations for FAD Deployments are selected and adjacent communities identified, more detailed and appropriate timelines, consultations and development of specific qualitative indicators will be considered for that specific community. The regional GAP is managed and reported on by the SPC (SPC).

## Roles and Responsibilities
At the EE level (within the PMU), this GAP is overseen by two SPC Gender & Environmental and Social Safeguards(Inclusion) officers (GESI Officers) who will provide backstopping, training, support, and coordination for National Fisheries Agencies by training one programme staff in Gender & Environmental and Social Safeguards to act as the GESI focal points to the programme. The GESI Officers will also be responsible for ensuring that the gender considerations are integrated into all aspects of the programme including the development of the project proposal, implementation, monitoring and evaluation. This is supported by the Fisheries Ministries/Departments at the start of implementation included in the overall programme budget (1 programme coordinator and 2 GESI Officers). The GESI officers will be responsible for ensuring that the gender considerations are integrated into all aspects of the programme including the development of the project proposal, implementation, monitoring and evaluation. While the PMU GESI Officers are ultimately responsible for implementing the GAP, national GESI FPs are responsible for the national-level tailoring and supporting implementation of the GAP, including the implementation of specific gender considerations for the country- level Programme.

## Timeline
The GAP starts "ongoing" in alignment with phases of project activities to which these contribute* in the timeline column below, given the highly integrated nature of the activities and recognizing that timelines will likely adjust. It is expected that the GESI officers will be well integrated on the PMU and be able to ensure specific GSI activities are implemented at the right time.

# Executive Summary

The fisheries sector is largely considered to be a male domain, with the role of women in the sector generally confined to support roles (e.g., post-harvest). Women's roles are often invisible, yet they can be quite significant for household food security and income generation, as well as natural resources and local food security.

This assessment provides a high-level overview of gendered roles, opportunities, and barriers within the context of fisheries in the Pacific region. This assessment provides contrast and analysis of the power dynamics, relative vulnerabilities, and varying roles and responsibilities of men and women working in the sector, who are involved in, and impacted by, Programme activities in each participating Pacific Island country.

The assessment highlights five targeted gender equality regional objectives for the Programme's gender approach, noting that these will become more specific at country level:

- Provide opportunities to improve the working conditions of women fishing in unhealthily and unsanitary conditions, including investments that support institutional strengthening and increasing access to resources. This includes providing women with access to several options for support, for example: shade, cold storage, lighting, handwashing facilities.
- Provide opportunities to improve the working conditions of women selling fish at affordable prices for urban and peri-urban communities.
- Provide increased supply of FAD fish at an affordable price for urban and peri-urban communities.

These regional objectives provide enough flexibility to be adapted to the individual country local realities and nuances. Each country will identify what their priorities and opportunities are to advance national gender equality goals in the context of this programme.

# Background and Introduction

## 2.1 Background and Objectives

The Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan is a necessary safeguard requirement by the Green Climate Fund (GCF). Under the GCF Regional Tuna Project Proposal: Adapting Tuna-dependent Pacific Island Small-scale Fisheries to Climate Change, the gender assessment will examine how women and men are currently engaged in the value chain of the fisheries sector in the Pacific and the role they play. The project proposes that the shift from tuna fishing grounds off reefs to offshore tuna. The shift in fishing efforts from coral reefs to tuna fishing will require the deployment and monitoring of Fish Aggregating Devices (FADs) by national/provincial fishery officers in coastal communities. This is a significant change for many Pacific Island small-scale fisheries, as climate change has come about due to a decline in the availability of reef fish for human consumption due to the increase in sea surface temperature and ocean acidification in the Western Central Pacific Islands region, and all of this is exacerbated by subsequent climatic changes and storm events.

Over the last few decades there has been a large shift towards using FADs to attract tuna and other pelagic fish, and this has become one of the major fishing modernisation strategies. FADs are artificial structures that mimic the appearance of natural reefs in order to attract fish to it. Its expected that the use of FADs can help increase the supply of fish for food security to local fishers and growing coastal communities. However, there is a need to ensure that this programme will not compromise the sustainability of healthy fish supply for local consumption to decrease imports of unhealthy and unsustainable products such as high fat meats (e.g., lamb and mutton rags, turkey tails etc.) and is also environmentally supportive.

While there are many benefits to the use of FADs, they do not come without challenges and FADs will have impacts on fishing, marketing, and consumption of the catch. Because of this, and the gendered nature of fisheries in the Pacific Islands region, it is important that women and men fisherfolk are included in decision-making processes related to FADs. In particular, the value chain of small-scale tuna fisheries, implementation of this Programme will have a significant impact on women and men fishers. The risk of overfishing from this programme will be reduced through risk avoidance or mitigating/inactivizing activities. The programme also provides an opportunity to change the narrative about gender inequality in the fisheries sector in the Pacific Islands region. This is because, as this assessment demonstrates, greater recognition of women’s roles in fisheries in the Pacific Islands.

Gender equality is about equal opportunities, rights and responsibilities for women and men fishers and how they are engaged in the value chain of the fisheries sector. Gender inequality is a result of unequal power distribution between women and men, exacerbated by ongoing discrimination, exclusionary laws, policies and institutions and social norms that perpetuate gender inequalities. Experience over decades has shown that gender equality, economic growth and development are mutually reinforcing and significantly correlated. This acknowledges that one cannot occur effectively without the other.

While fisheries are generally considered a male domain, the Pacific Islands region has made significant progress in this area in both regional (currently the leading regional fisheries management organisation) and national level. The Pacific Islands region is also a leader in gender equality by women) and national) scales (https://fame.spc.int/publications/bulletins/womenandfisheries/39). This Gender Assessment demonstrates that both women and men are involved in the sector at different levels, from fishing to marketing and consumption of fish.


---
**Page 154**
---

# Gender Assessment

Page 7 of 14

## Methodology and scope of the assessment

To develop this gender assessment and highlight local realities and characteristics of the Pacific Island countries (PICs), a targeted approach was undertaken. Note that this assessment is focused on gender issues specific to the proposed Programme, rather than a broader consideration of gender dimensions in general.

Further from the data limitations in terms of gender characteristics in some Pacific Island Countries (PICs) (no territories are participating in the Programme) an assessment based on available gender related information from each targeted Pacific Island has been considered in the context of the current GCF Gender Action Plan. The focus is on highlighting key gender issues and nuances, and this is only captured broadly in the current gender assessment. A short summary highlights some of these key gender issues and nuances for each country.

The following table provides an overview of the gender related dimensions of each Pacific Island country. When project countries are identified at the next phase of the GCF Readiness Programme, the gender dimensions will be integrated into the project documentation and mainstreamed in all relevant components. These will be tailored according to the country nuances and current gender mainstreaming efforts.

The methods used for this gender assessment included:

- Literature review of available statistics and information including national censuses, International Labour Organization (ILO) data, World Bank Living Income and Expenditure surveys, Health and Education Statistics and other available data.
- Review of regional and national gender policy and strategy documents.
- Interviews with key stakeholders in the fisheries sector, particularly fishing supply chain stakeholders. That is: fish farmer; fish sellers in stalls and markets; consumers of fish who purchase from these outlets; and women involved in processing and selling fish.
This included fishing communities from 13 PICs (the RMI chose not to participate in Component A) with a total of approximately 100+ interviews; and
- Consultation with key experts and projects within the region that may provide a glimpse into local realities (referenced in annex).

National consultants were recruited from each of the PICs to conduct community consultations using an approved survey to understand the gender issues of the fishery sector of their country. A line of questioning with a combination of qualitative and quantitative questions was developed to provide

a glimpse into national gender dimensions for the consideration of FADs. The information from questionnaires and interviews have been analyzed and reported herein in line with above project objectives in Chapter 6 Country Gender Profiles.

# 3 Gender and Development Issues in the Fisheries Sector

It is pertinent to commence this section highlighting the keynote speech by the recently elected Prime Minister of Samoa (Faima Mata'afa) at the first Pacific Islands Forum Women Leaders Meeting (PPWLM) in 2022. She noted that to pursue gender equality across the Pacific, it's time for leaders "to move beyond rhetoric and to focus on the process required to strengthen outcomes for women and girls, leaving no one behind."

## 4.2 International policies

There are several international agreements, commitments and overarching principles that seek to improve gender equality and parity across all facets of development in the Pacific. Momentum is occurring to recognize women's role in the fishing industry, but more needs to be done. These include:

- Convention for the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)
- Universal Declaration of Human Rights 1948-Gender Equality was made part of international human rights recognized that,

"All human beings are born free and equal in dignity and rights" and that "everyone is entitled to all the rights and freedoms set forth in this Declaration, without distinction of any kind, such as race, colour, sex, language, religion, ... birth or other status."

### Current stance on gender equality for the Pacific Island Region

Many Pacific Island countries have adopted policies aimed at working towards greater gender equality in accordance with international agreements and commitments. These include: the Convention for the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) (2005 to 2015); the Pacific Plan; the Pacific Women and Gender Equality (2005 to 2015); the Pacific Plan; the 42nd Pacific Island Forum commitment to increase the representation of women in leadership and decision-making; and the 46th Pacific Island Forum commitment on sexual and reproductive health.

In 2012, PIs governments committed to the Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration that stands tall to the status of women in the Pacific and empower women and girls as active participants in economic development and social life. These include commitments towards responsive programmes and policies to advocate for:

I. Improved production and use of sex disaggregated data and gender analysis to inform government policy.

II. Increased representation of women in decision-making as part of private and local and national governance (e.g., advocacy groups, political parties, school boards and market committees).

III. Economic empowerment through improved access and equal opportunity to employment opportunities and a reduction in discriminatory pay conditions for women and improved facilities that reduce markets to increase profitability and efficiency and support for women entrepreneurs.

IV. Ensuring the protection of women's rights (e.g., education, health, counseling, legal) for women and girls who are survivors of violence; enact legislation regarding sexual and gender-based violence and impose appropriate penalties for perpetrators.

V. Encourage education on reproductive health and awareness of programmes and gender parity in informal, primary, secondary, and tertiary education and training.


Despite these commitments, the progress towards achieving gender equality remains slow. Many studies and reports have been undertaken about women's participation in the tuna industry highlighting impacts, costs and benefits, constraints, and opportunities. However, it appears that little has been done to address the underlying issues of gender inequality in the Pacific Island tuna industry. The women's role in the fishing industry remains obscure, even invisible. There is some progress towards improved education for girls and some positive initiatives to address violence against women.

### 4.4 Characteristics of Pacific Island Communities in Gender Equality

Gender equality in Pacific island countries is reportedly limited by several factors including representation in decision-making positions, access to education and training opportunities, violence against violence, lack of women's rights over their sexual and reproductive health (e.g., birth control and abortion) and fewer opportunities for economic independence (e.g., access to public and private financial services), and the distribution of domestic roles and responsibilities.

#### 3.3.1 Literacy and Education

**Education Levels and Access**

In general, most PIs are on track to achieve targets for universal primary education and gender equality commitments in primary and secondary schools (see Figure 2: Gross Enrolment Rates and Gender Parity Indexes in Primary and Secondary Education for PICs). Nevertheless, boys are more likely to drop out of school compared to girls, and enrolments to university are also higher in females compared to males. In many cases, women are underrepresented in science, technology, engineering, and male-dominated fields, and girls are often under-represented in scientific, vocational and technical fields.

Although women have had educational opportunities have been demonstrated in PICs, this has not yet resulted in a marked increase in female participation in entrepreneurship.

In the context of this Programme, an opportunity lies in developing or increasing focused support to women entrepreneurs with adding value to fish through canning, barbecuing or smoking fish which can also be an entry point for women into formal roles in the fishing industry. This would require training in leadership and business skills including marketing and financial management.

Women remain tied to their traditional roles and the majority of those who have professional careers carry these out in addition to their traditional home and community roles. This can place a considerable time constraint on those with professional roles, limiting the opportunity to advance into senior positions within the sector.

In the context of this Programme, a risk therefore stems from women not being able to engage in project activities actively or meaningfully due the great burden of other priorities. At the national level, gender equality is often seen as an issue for women and times, locations, strategies that work best for women in the fish processing & marketing sphere.

3.3.2 Women’s Health and Nutrition

A few PICS have made substantial advances in women’s health, yet in other countries there remains a lot to be done. Many of the health challenges are avoidable. These challenges can vary because of both biological factors (e.g., pregnancy-related complications) and social factors. Women are disadvantaged by discrimination rooted in sociocultural factors and subsequently, women’s health suffers. Women are more likely than men to suffer from non-communicable diseases due to susceptibility and because women are more likely to undertake care roles for others with non-communicable diseases. Heart-disease and stroke, diabetes, cancer, and chronic lung disease continue to be major causes of death among women in many countries.

Consequently, many PICS have not achieved the health Millennium Development Goals (MDG) targets.

Furthermore, underlying socio-cultural circumstances are major contributors to maternal and neonatal mortality, high rates of unmet need for family planning (UNFP), low contraceptive prevalence rate (CSPR; some PICs), and high rates of unplanned pregnancy. And yet, improved maternal health: including antenatal care coverage and attendance at births by trained personnel, can result in a decrease in maternal deaths. In 2018, the Pacific region had one of the highest maternal mortality ratios (MMRs) globally with dispersed populations and those living on remote islands or in mountainous areas.

In 2020, it was estimated that 35% of women aged 15–49 years in the Pacific were not using any method of contraception. This is a missed opportunity for improved health outcomes for women’s health issues in the future. Women health workers generally provide a more comfortable and credible environment to support women's and family planning needs, due to their familiarity with cultural norms and having a sound understanding of what other women experience.

In the context of this Programme, there is good opportunity to influence women towards nuts and away from fish-based starchy staples with FADs and away from imported or other less healthy foods. Given that women are usually responsible for household meals, women would be a key audience for these messages.


---
**Page 159**
---

3.3.3 Vulnerability and Poverty

The Pacific Island countries face significant challenges which contribute to their growing vulnerability and the increased poverty experienced across the region. The following are some of the key challenges relating to vulnerability and poverty:

- **Geographical location:** Climate Change
- **Economic concerns:**

Women and children are at a greater risk of poverty in PICs, particularly in urban areas where women work in low-paying jobs or informal sectors. Despite efforts by governments and NGOs, programmes have been introduced in some countries; however, slow economic growth limits the effect of these interventions. Women and girls living in rural areas often have limited access to resources. As inequality and vulnerability are normalised, these populations often overlook their rights and do not seek support from institutions and services that are available.

3.3.4 Gender Based Violence (GBV) and Related Dimensions

Domestic Violence and Sexual Abuse

Violence against women and girls in PICs is amongst the highest in the world. Global evidence demonstrates that domestic violence is a root cause of violence against women and girls. In countries where prevalence studies have been undertaken, up to 60% of women in the Pacific have reported experiencing physical or sexual violence by a partner in their life (See Figure 3: Prevalence and Patterns of Intimate Partner Violence Against Women in the Pacific). The interplay between gender roles and social norms as well as male sexual entitlement are drivers of violence in PICs. In many societies, women are expected to be submissive and obedient to men, and they may feel their responsibility to "behave" in a way that does not anger their partners.109 In some communities' women may accept this way of thinking more than their male parents.

A high level of alcohol consumption in males and childhood exposure to violence, and exposure to violence are also associated with increased rates of domestic violence in PICs.110 While some countries and/or communities are matrilineal, this does not deter the level of violence against women.

As a result of domestic violence, sexual harassment and trafficking women and girls, females are often more vulnerable to poverty than males. The prevalence of intimate partner violence is increasing in some PICs. HIV is considered a low-level epidemic in countries such as Kiribati according to the 2015 Global AIDS report.

In the context of this Programme, there is a risk that activities could contribute to, or exacerbate GBV during the course of implementation. Not necessarily because FAD fishing is known to be a high-risk activity for GBV, but because it is often associated with high rates of GBV in the fisheries sector more broadly. At the same time, there is an opportunity within this Programme to build awareness about GBV, women’s/girls’ rights, and resources that exist to help support survivors.

3.3.5 Employment opportunities and labour force participation

Overview of Employment and Gender Constraints

As described earlier, advances in employment opportunities have not resulted in a marked increase in earnings for women or increased their participation in entrepreneurship11. Gender-based segregation and occupational streaming in vocational training and occupations tends to be gender stereotypical, which can limit both women’s and men’s access to certain jobs. Women are often asked to take on additional unpaid domestic work such as cooking, cleaning, preparing food and caring for family and children which limits their participation in the labour force.

Employment Status and Labour Force Participation

Aside from reports from Solomon Islands and Fiji, there is very little information on employment opportunities for women in the fisheries sector. Overall, there is a lack of job opportunities for women in paid employment, including within the fisheries sector. Most women occupy lower-level positions in the public sector and the number of women who hold senior public service positions is very small. Women are also under-represented in private sector employment. This limits their low earnings and no protection. Women are most frequently home-based and reliant on subsistence farming, marketing agricultural products and petty trading12. Whilst this is the case, the fishing industry remains a predominantly male dominated area. Section 4 discusses the gender gap.

# Gender and Fish Aggregating Devices in the Pacific Region

## Brief overview of fish aggregating devices in the Pacific Region

FADs have the capability of improving food security through increased availability and accessibility to localized aquatic animal protein, increasing the reliability of income from fishing and creating employment in coastal areas through fish trading and processing. Over the last 10 years, FADs for both artisanal and industrial fisheries have been deployed across the Pacific region.

While FADs are already located across Pacific Island states, these are in varying conditions ranging from effective to washed up on reefs. The proposed Programme seeks to disseminate further FADs across the region, with a focus on areas where there is currently no or limited access to FADs, including the Cook Islands, Federated States of Micronesia, Fiji, Kiribati, Marshall Islands, Nauru, Palau, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Solomon Islands, Tonga, Tuvalu, and Vanuatu.

The proposed Programme seeks to develop more effective FADs that are secure, functional, and more sustainable. This will be achieved by testing the use of a variety of materials for non-reef to supplement the current nutritional sources in each location and reduce fishing pressure on reefs.

Primary stakeholders and target beneficiaries include:

1. Coastal fishers, processors, traders and their families, and communities
2. Government institutions responsible for the administration, management, and development of coastal fisheries at local, district and national levels

These stakeholders are important to consider with the implementation of the regional tuna programme in an area where they have a significant role.

## Gender in the fishers sector in the Pacific

Women play a crucial role in value chains of fisheries sectors. This is particularly true in industrial fishing particularly in processing factories, in the field of seafared roles as well as management of the fishing vessel and post-harvest handling. The importance of women in these areas is reflected by the fact that women are involved in many aspects of fishery activities. The crucial role of women in different areas of fishing activities is reflected in various international conventions, agreements, and policies. Examples of these international agreements can include:


---
**Page 162**
---

- United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea 1982 (UNCLOS) – considered the Constitution of the Sea.
- International Health Regulations (2005).
- Pacific Regional Ocean Policy.

Gender dimensions shape the differential identities of women and men, their norms, roles and responsibilities. It influences people’s (unequal) access to resources and decision making and people's agency. Fisheries are generally considered a male domain.


Although FADs are a common fishing gear in many countries, they are not accessible to the entire community. FADs can only be directly used by those who have access to boats (mostly men), therefore the benefits are not always equally shared. Women’s roles in fisheries – along the value chain, are in sustaining the fishery and providing food security for their families. They are involved in fishing gear making, harvesting, selling, and trading. In a Gender Mainstreaming study1 done by WWF in Fiji's offshore tuna industry, women were found to be 30% of the workforce employed on board Pacific Tuna Company (PACCO) in Fiji. Fiji was 37% more than the participation of men. While this is commercial fishing it is also reflected in other countries such as Kiribati, Solomon Islands and Tuvalu where women are involved in small scale fisheries.


Women face persistent gender-based discrimination and marginalization in the tuna value chain of the fisheries sector that is often defined by diverse social context. Women, youth, and other marginalized groups have limited access to resources and decision-making processes over natural resources and which may impact their ability to gain benefits from marine resources equitably2.


In commercial fisheries, women are more commonly oversrepresented in vulnerable categories of employment. They generally lack access to training opportunities and decision-making positions as shown by the women selling fish along the roadside often in dusty unhealthy conditions. They also have limited access to services like healthcare, childcare, education and transport. Women in fishing communities are often disproportionately impacted by sexual violence, prejudices, and other forms of harassment.


Across the Pacific, women are less represented in fisheries’ associations, cooperatives, and unions. They play a critical role in the household economy but their contributions are not recognized and their lives and livelihoods. Lack of quantitative data further undermines women’s role in fisheries.


This Programme thus presents opportunities to create sustained change within the fisheries sectors of these countries through the gender-sensitive (perhaps beyond) horizon3. For example, the designation of a gender focal person at the national level or the establishment of a gender unit within the Ministry by 2 full time GESI officers from the PMU could significantly contribute to institutional strengthening on gender in fisheries policy making.


Further, a primary aim of this programme (and the project level activities most directly impacting women) is to focus on capacity building & training in post-harvest methods for processing & marketing fish caught around FAGS and in MSMEs. This will include basic supplies and equipment to apply these skills. The project will also provide training on creating more gender-inclusive workplaces (such as shade). Additional productive assets are outside the scope of this project.

### 4.2.1 Fish Aggregating Device Investments can overlook women’s needs and priorities.

#### Gendered Roles in Fisheries in The Pacific

*Fishing* tends to be narrowly viewed as only catching fish at sea, which often leaves women out of sight or out of mind. While men are involved in primary fishing activities are predominantly carried out by men in the Pacific, women are involved in supportive activities in some forms of fishing such as the post-harvest processing of fish and some smaller-scale styles of fishing i.e. *kai* fishing in Fiji and shortline net fishing in Solomon Islands.

Women’s roles in fisheries are crucial to Pacific livelihoods. Their role in the sector is much higher than the international average, with women contributing 50% or more of the total catch in many countries.
A reason is that women mainly work in the less visible fishing roles, such as: shoreline fishing and picking shellfish; mending nets and processing and selling fish, doing the accounting; and roles in processing and marketing.

Women working in the fishery sector can face poor working conditions and environments, lacking job and even personal security. Some roles are either under or unpaid making them invisible within the industry. Women also have fewer opportunities to access training and education for new opportunities to advance in the industry, having less access than men to resources such as technology, loans, insurance, industry networks and information. Women’s traditional household and childcare responsibilities further demot what they can do in the industry both timewise and knowledge related.

Historically women's needs and priorities and women in the fishing sector have had limited access to services such as health care, education and legal aid.

#### Gendered Roles in Markets-Sale for The Safe of Fish from FAO

There are safety and security issues related to selling fish in many markets across the project countries. The health of women, who are the primary sellers of fish, may be compromised while selling fish at markets. Women often sell fish in crowded and unsanitary conditions that may not be than hygienic locations at the roadside and the women are subjected to dust, fumes and other risks such as bites from insects and snakes. Women also face a higher risk of contracting illnesses) while handling the fish or have their safety compromised at markets and while travelling. Women and girls in PIsCA are often disproportionately impacted by sexual violence, abuse and harassment when selling fish.

There is an increasing awareness among women about the need to increase safety and security issues when selling tuna (vs. other fish), however there is an opportunity for increased training and security of women fish sellers through capacity building and awareness about support services.

#### Gendered Governance of Local Fisheries Resources and Fishing Grounds

Government controls access to natural resources, local tenure arrangements, levels of education, wealth, and the ability to influence decision-making in place to ensure the entire population benefits from fisheries and aquaculture. Women have limited roles in governance for local fisheries and fishing grounds, making it difficult for women to advocate for safe working conditions and other issues that affect their livelihoods.

In the context of this programme, there is a risk that women’s specific needs and priorities may not be well represented in project governance, while there is also an opportunity within this programme to increase women's representation and interests in local fisheries governance.

FAO programmes and projects need to integrate gender dimensions to maximize their potential for positive impacts and ensure greater gender equality.

### Positive impacts/opportunities to advance gender equity

- **Opportunity for fish sellers to improve the hygiene and safety of fish, while adding value (e.g., BSC certification on fish):** Fish sellers trained in post-harvest handling can maintain shelf life of tuna.
- **Opportunity to influence diet towards tuna as a source of protein and away from impacting women’s less healthy foods:** Given that women are usually responsible for household meals, women would be a key audience for these messages.
- **Opportunity within this programme to raise awareness about GBV, survivors’ rights, and resources that can support survivors:** Community engagement should include links to GBV resources and clarity on women's rights.
- **Opportunity to support and encourage women to enter into the FAD fishery if they choose:** Ensure that FAD training is open and welcoming to all. Further it is primarily women who handle post-harvest activities, so any future training or focus will be geared towards women in the fishery.
- **Opportunity to improve the safety and security of women fish sellers:** Tailored capacity building and awareness about safety and security for fish sellers (e.g., GBV support services).

From this standpoint each country can prioritise specific activities or approaches that consider local realities, resources and mainstream gender dimensions into these to enhance gender equality. # 5 Recommended Strategies for Regional and Country Level Gender Equality Approach

There are several drivers behind the impetus to encourage Gender Equality in fisheries. For example, fisheries organizations view gender equality as a pathway towards more healthy fish stocks and improve community nutrition (instrumental); development agencies view gender equality as a core value or principle (inherent); fishers see gender equality either as a greater source of reliable labour for fishing activities (instrumental) or as a means to increase their bargaining power (value sensitized). Obviously increasing gender equality needs to be managed and may vary by country and even within countries. The strategies described below are intended to be applied most effectively through a partnership and multidisciplinary approach. This requires working collaboratively in to bring together the business case and the altruistic case (Mangubhai et al., 2022).

## 5.1 Recommended processes and activities to advance Gender Equality in fisheries

Several recommendations have been highlighted throughout this Assessment to encourage greater access to healthy tuna stocks for men and women in communities. It is important to emphasise the approach and related processes required to strengthen gender equality in the fishing sector. Furthermore, it is important that the impact of these strategies are monitored and evaluated through benefits measured intermittently through country defined indicators. Some cross-cutting activities include:

### Upskilling women and men to improve fish products and services

- **Opportunities for training provided to both men and women in various aspects of fishing including gender dimensions:** fisheries extension activities; safety dimensions in all phases of the fishing cycle.
- **Provide basic supplies and training to maintain shelf life of fish and provide training to women on the processing and marketing of tuna.** This includes targeted capacity building on post-harvest handling, storage and marketing of tuna products with a focus on women as this post-harvest dimension of the fishery is mainly done by women.
- **Provide training for communities in the preparation of tuna dishes that are suitable for all households.**
- **Provide training for communities to better understand and thus manage effects of climate change on coral reefs, reef fish and in communities.** For example, consuming more tuna instead of reef fish.
- **Identify and encourage activities that women and men and collective communities undertake to protect and sustain their fish resources particularly the health of the reef.**

### Gender Sensitisation – building an awareness

- **Upskilling of project stakeholders in Gender and GBV dimensions to encourage greater awareness on gender equality issues, especially those related to the fishing sector such as better family nutrition, and encourage participation of women in non-traditional work opportunities.**
- **Identify the gender-related issues that can be faced by men and women in the industry and develop strategies to address these issues including GBV concerns, health and safety concerns, GBV and SEH issues and other behavioural risks. These are included in activity 1.1 in this document.**
- **Build greater awareness of women’s contribution to family livelihoods and food security roles in the tuna fishery industry within the artisanal tuna fishery sector.**

### Improve capacity and collaboration


---
**Page 167**

# Understanding and encouraging Role diversity

Men and women don't have to do the same roles in fishing to promote gender equality. However, there are several ways to encourage greater inclusion of both genders:

- Inclusion of a training for greater recognition of the roles both women and men play in the fishing industry, both coastal and offshore fishing.
- Through training, accreditation and communication, encourage women to participate more fully in the fisheries sector.

## Encourage women to pursue careers in the fisheries sector through scholarships, recruitment, encouragement and improved workplace conditions – for all stages of the supply chain.

### Actively implement inclusive approaches to engaging FAD fishers and those along the value chain

- Provide resources and training for both men and women in fisheries development and management such as leadership training, extension services and conservation activities.
- Peer mentoring, networking and capacity building for women in the tuna fishery sector will encourage their participation in non-traditional roles.

## Monitor improvements in gender awareness and equality

### Improve gender awareness and equality by:

1. Improving recognition of women's roles in the industry both directly (fishing and post-harvest processing) and indirectly (nutrition aspects).
2. Motivating and shifting skills in knowledge and skills developed by women and men relating to fish catches and how they are using these to strengthen nutrition and food security outcomes.
3. Measuring benefits and empowerment (personal viability) that men and women receive working in the project encouraging gender inclusion to be a 'norm'. For example, fostering working relationships between women and men through training and capacity building; mentoring and peer mentoring for emerging women leaders in the tuna fishery sector.

These priorities, strategies and recommendations will be tailored in each country, as resources and socio-cultural characteristics at the country level will vary. Try these offer some practical and viable insights into how to promote gender equality in the TFP.


## An intersectional analysis of gender and Ethnicity

Intersectionality offers an analytical framework which can consider how societies treat people based on their race, ethnicity, class, age, gender, sexual orientation, disability, and sexuality.

Depending on those identities, a person may be privileged or oppressed (discrimination), productive, provider, gender relations and identities in fishing through a critical intersectional perspective. Socially constructed femininity: An Unfettered but Essential Theoretical Framework for Social Psychology [Springerlink]


---
**Page 168**

How this is important to the Regional Tuna Programme

The intersectional framework helps to systematically address the largely 'invisible' but significant contributions of women in fisheries and related family, communities and industries in all Pacific countries (Trangoez and Gerrard, 2018; Phap et al., 2013). In recent years there has been a greater consideration of gender equality and women's empowerment within the fisheries sector nationally, regionally and nationally in the Pacific. Yet what is important to recognise these contributions, there has been little attention to how women's changing roles and practices are managed within unchanged systems and structures that have not been designed with their needs and circumstances in mind.

The basic premise requires women to consider their roles and activities from their own perspectives and experiences. This is a significant departure from the traditional approach which has given little attention to the identities and activities of women’s lives in fishing and women's knowledge is often ignored in research and policy. This can serve to cement the invisibility of women.

While women's roles and responsibilities in fisheries are often different from men's, they still have an active and important role. For example, women harvest and sell seafood and seaweed in shellfish markets, collect and process fish for sale or consumption, engage in fishing and fish processing in large numbers.

There was little included in the country questionnaires and responses regarding the benefits of the intersectional approach to gender and fishing for several reasons:

- Little to no information available on the intersections between gender and fishing especially in the Pacific wheretheny information covered largely practical benefits and outcomes of fishing activities. This is an important aspect that was unable to be covered in a short time and with available resources.
- An extremely number of responses were collected from a Pacific perspective which provided a baseline of practical roles of women in the fishing industry. This can be broken down at a national level and then aggregated further to consider this strategic approach in terms of project activities and outcomes.

An example of responses which could be further broken down to build on an intersectional approach country specific

| Country | Activities which can further intersectional gender outcomes |
| --- | --- |
| Tonga | Communities need focused training on managing parts of the FAD fishing supply chain – eg ice making machines |
| Cook Islands | Investigate mechanisms to drive community ownership of FADs Strengthen governance and justice systems in transhipping ports to reduce illegal, unreported and unregulated (IUU) fishing from crews of fishing vessels |
| Fiji | Negotiate with government the number of active small and medium-scale enterprises, and the affiliated needs, costs, etc. |
| Samoa | Provide fish storage coolers for operators Ice making machines in key districts |

How the Regional Tuna Programme will seek to integrate an intersectional framework
Consequently, this program will acknowledge the intersectionality of gender dimensions and women’s roles in the fishery. This will allow for a more comprehensive understanding of opportunities and benefits to understand women’s varying and changing roles and practices and how they can intersect with gender relations and identities. Primarily this will be guided through the GAPAction 1.1 which is a framework that ensures that the intersectionality of women in FAD fishery value chains is integrated into the workplans for scaling up of national FAD programmes.
For example, the following are some key considerations to be taken into account when designing projects that both support women’s reproductive roles while they are employed in productive roles in the FAD fishing industry. Key considerations:
• Substituting dust masks with face shields at market locations so children do not need to sit in the dust and face safety issues while their mothers sell fish.
• Training in maintenance of equipment such as ice machines can include women to develop their more technical skills.


6 Country Gender Profiles
As aligned with the SDG gender indicators and targets, the proposed country gender equality briefs cover several thematic areas including: Women’s Rights; Women’s Representation and Leadership; Women’s Economic Empowerment; Education; Health/Sexual and Reproductive Health; Ending Violence against Women and Girls; and Gender and Protection in Humanitarian Action. While these are all important areas for consideration, however, their roles are not well articulated, hence many do not consider themselves fishery professionals.

Key information that can strengthen the use of FADs and benefit local communities can include:
• Country specific agendas that can deliver a baseline of sex disaggregated information as it applies to FAD fishing and to further inform proposed activities to ensure that both women and men benefit from the RTP.
• Improving the data in the industry data which provides greater recognition of women’s roles in the industry both directly (fishing) and indirectly (food preparation aspects).
• Opportunities for women to participate in training and capacity building within fisheries including gender dimensions; safe and effective FAD fishing activities; safety dimensions in all phases of the fish supply chain.

A brief assessment of key gender dimensions has been prepared for each respective country in the proposed Programme. However, some of the data is not consistent between countries, it is important to identify any gaps in information.


6.1 Cook Islands
International Gender Policies


---
**Page 170**
---

Cook Islands has shown commitment to strengthening gender equality in the fisheries sector through various international commitments and national priorities and policies, including but not limited to the following:

* The Convention on Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) – adopted in 2006.
* The UN Framework Convention on Climate Change and the Convention on Biodiversity. This concerns women’s roles in resource management, including management of rivers, reefs, and oceans.
* Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration signed 2012 - recent report (2016) noted that while women are well represented in some councils they are still under-represented in senior management and decision-making roles.

### National Gender Policies

* **National Policy on Gender Equity and Women's Empowerment** together with a 5-year strategic plan action 2011-2016. Recognises women and men are equal partners in the development of the Cook Islands. Also established the Gender and Development Division under the Ministry of Social Services.
* CEDAW Law Reform programme - began in 2008 with revisions noted to a few acts recognising women’s rights, including the Family Protection Act and recently the Family Protection and Support Law Bill in 2017 – to end child abuse and domestic violence.
* Enactment of the Employment Relations Act 2012 which provides maternity leave benefits for women in the private sector and provisions for the prevention of sexual harassment.

### Outcomes

* Women’s Participation in Decision Making - progress in increasing women's representation and political participation, for example, in 2004 four out of the 55 Island Government Councils (7.3%) were women (Public Service Commission’s Annual Report).
* The Cook Islands National Council of Women (CINCW) is a bipartisan group that advocates for gender equality through law-making, budgeting, oversight, and representation.
* The percentage of women in parliament is 17%, women in management roles at 48% with many women occupying senior government officials, corporate and general manager positions.
* At the local level, numerous initiatives provide capacity building and support for women candidates in island council elections. o Cook Islands National Council of Women, Cook Islands Gender Equality SPC o Cook Islands National Council of Women, Cook Islands Gender Equality SPC o 2016 Blockade of the Gender Mainstreaming Capacity of Pacific Island Governments

### Some Bottlenecks

Sexual offences are defined in the 1969 Crimes Act. While updates have been under discussion since 2017 a new Crimes Bill has yet to be accepted and passed into law. This updated Bill:

* seeks to remove exemptions related to marital rape
* criminalises sexual contact with a person under the age of 15 if married to the child in question.
* decriminalises same-sex social contact between adult men upon mutual consent.
