Key input from national consultations

30 men and 6 women respondents were interviewed in focus group discussions, aged between 30 to 70 years of age. This was broken down into 23 from Mangaia (5 females) and 8 from Rarotonga (12 males, 1 female) villages in July 2023.

Of these respondents, 13 respondents from Rarotonga and 15 respondents from Mangaia were married. Note that some people had multiple work roles. Most head of households (HoH) women - 17 in Mangaia compared to 1 female HoH in Rarotonga. Interestingly there was a different response with 5 FHs compared to 8 males.

Roles and Responsibilities

The main roles for men and women in fishing as reported in the survey are as follow:

- Offshore fishing – male dominated – no female respondent is a fisher in this type of offshore fishing including FAID fishing. While many respondents did not consider there were constraints, those that did cited lack of time and resources as well as time commitments make up the main reasons women do not participate in offshore fishing.
- Coastal fishing – 100% men carry out coastal fishing
- Boat handling – 95% of men are involved in these tasks – apart from one woman in Mangaia
- Polynesian handline – predominantly men, however a significant number of women are also involved in polynesian handline - 28.5% respondents in Mangaia and 46% respondents in Rarotonga. Women are more likely to be involved than men in this type of fishing handling.
- Fish cooking for home or selling use is cited to be the role of both men and women fairly equally.
- Cleaning fish is carried out by both man and women.
- Selling fish is the role of both men and women.

6.2 Federated States of Micronesia

Given that no FAID fisheries supply chain surveys were able to be carried out in FSM, this section has drawn on information from the limited number of pilot survey carried out in FSM in recent years, while some are becoming dated, they still provide a coverage of the main gender dimensions in the different States and basically reflect several key local realities.

Context

Approximately 22% of residents live in urban areas of the FSM Island states, this means that ~78% of the population live in rural areas. Different Levels of Governance exist in the nation with the overall national government forming one level of governance and each of the four states Chuuk, Yap, Kosrae and Pohnpei forming another level of governance. The national government provides some support for women’s rights but there are also many constraints to actions geared towards strengthening gender equality in the country. This is reflected in the following:

While there is some political will at the national level towards encouraging greater gender equality, there is limited action to actively address issues pertaining to gender equality. Gender sensitisation has been undertaken but more is required with adequate allocation of resources.

International Commitments

Gender Assessment
Page 24 of 54


---
**Page 172**
---

FSM has shown commitment to strengthening gender equality through several international commitments and national priorities and policies, including the following:

* The 1995 Beijing Platform for Action (BPFA)
* The 2000 Millennium Development Goals (MDGs)
* The Pacific Plan of Action for the Advancement of Women and Gender Equality 2005–2015 (RPPA)
* Pacific Community (revised in 2007)
* The Convention on Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) – acceded to in 2008. FSM maintains a few reservations to CEDAW, including in respect of Articles 15 and 16, which relate to the right to vote and stand for election; benefits, and Articles 20, 5, and 16 on the elimination of discriminatory cultural stereotypes.
* Convention on Biological Diversity
* The UN Framework Convention on Climate Change and the Convention on Biodiversity. This convention includes women’s roles in resource management, including management of rivers, reefs, and oceans.
* Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration signed August 2012 – recent report (2016) noted that while women are well represented at village councils they were still underrepresented in senior management and decision-making roles.

### National Gender Policies

Legislation in FSM is shared between the National Congress and the four state parliaments.
* **National Constitution** and the constitutions of the four states protect the legal rights of women, all prohibiting discrimination on grounds of sex. Article IV of the FSM Constitution (GoFSM 1978) provides for a Declaration of Rights. Section 4 of this article confirms that equal protection of the laws may not be denied or abridged by the States in respect to any person; however, The Constitution contains no definition of discrimination against women.
* **States** have their own constitutions and laws that can protect women’s human rights, including:
    * Article 26 of the Chuuk State Constitution, section 2 – rights to be enjoyed by a person irrespective of, among other things, sex
    * Article 17 of the Kosrae Constitution, subsections (b) and (c), set out provisions for women’s rights.
* **Article** 4, section 3 of the Pohnpei State Constitution and Article 4 of Yap State Constitution provide that no law shall be inconsistent with the FSM Constitution.
* All constitutions, apart from Kosrae, contain provisions through which a person who has experienced discrimination can seek redress.

### National Gender Policy

National gender policy is reflected in the national strategy for gender mainstreaming in fisheries management, development and enforcement at national and state levels. The following are some of its key elements:
* **The FSM Strategic Development Plan (SDP) 2004–2023** (GoFSM 2004:525) includes a strategic goal to mainstream gender issues into decision-making, policies, and strategic development.
* **Women’s Participation in Decision-Making – progress in increasing women’s representation and political participation**, for example, in 2010 four of the 55 Island Government Councilors (7.3%) were women (Public Service Commission’s Annual Report).

### Some bottlenecks

Institutional challenges include:


---
**Page 173**
---

* Many government agencies with overlapping mandates and responsibilities in coastal fisheries management, as defined in their respective legislation.
* Coastal fisheries management is carried out at national, state and local/municipal government levels, which are characterized by different levels of governance adding complexity to fisheries management and development and requires strong coordination.
* However, from a gender mainstreaming perspective, the management of coastal fisheries at a community level can also provide an entry point for stronger engagement and visibility of organized women.

* Gender analysis of the fisheries sector in FSM coastal waters (this includes territorial seas and exclusive economic zones) under the responsibility of MOF, and the national government has the role of coordination.
* There is a lack of interest from women wanting to move into roles that have traditionally been dominated by men. Leadership at all levels of the state, and particularly leadership on traditional forms of local leadership, in which women are not highly represented.
* There is also a need for better data collection and analysis, including disaggregated data and gender indicators across sectors to provide the basis of positive gender mainstreaming.

### 6.2.1 Literature Summary of the Federated States of Micronesia

Gender relations in FSM are shaped by cultural norms and practices which vary across the four states. While detailed information is available, it is pertinent to include some cross cutting gender related issues.

#### Education
* There is close parity between male and female student numbers at schools.
* However, at tertiary level there tends to be stereotypical fields of study (2008 FSM Statistical Yearbook). For example, 60 boys compared to 14 girls in marine science. The lack of women in technical and professional positions in employment reflects this situation.
* Traditional gender roles can limit girls' and women's choices in education and careers options.

#### Employment
* The number of female wage and salary earners was on average less than half that of males (2019: 4,514 vs. 3,260) (GSOF 2022).
* Furthermore, women in the paid workforce, are concentrated at the lower levels of the hierarchy.
* Formal employment in the FSM fisheries sector consists of only about 250 people working for wages, the majority of these being men. Overall, less than half of all wage earners come from female workers (GSOF 2019). Women workers stand to benefit from proposals by the government to support the development of tuna-planting farms in Pohnpei and Chuuk.
* As far as women's roles in the National Fisheries Corporation (NFC), they are employed in the office, but not in the field. They are also not involved in decision-making processes.

#### Employment
* Twenty-five per cent of male employment comes from FSM Maritime Training College, which offers an entry point for training on prevention of sexual harassment and domestic violence.
* Although maternal mortality rates (MMR) have fallen significantly, FSM still has one of the highest in the Pacific region (206 in 2003) (GSoF and UNDP 2009).
* All four states report high teenage pregnancy rates (SPC Stocktake 2012). Teenage pregnancy is a major reason for girls dropping out of high school and college.


---
**Page 174**
---

Access to water

In 2010 data suggested that only 41 per cent of the population has access to an improved water source and about 45 per cent to improved sanitation (ADB 2010). This is of particular concern for women, who in turn have a role in food preparation and their hygiene requirements.

Gender Based Violence

GBV in all its forms including sexual harassment is a common cause of physical and mental harm to women and children and a major contributor to social problems (2004 Situational Analysis Report). Some examples include:

* Until recently, the age of consent in the four states has been 13–16 years of age, with Chuck recently increasing the age of consent to 18 and Yap State planning to do the same (UPR 2010/11).
* While there is mandatory prosecution of sexual offences, bail can be granted for defendants even where there is a risk of violence to survivors. Furthermore, customary practices of ‘topping up’ or ‘reconciliation payments’ are often used as an alternative to prosecution.

Actions need to integrate both legal and cultural practices that strengthen the protection of vulnerable women and children. Some actions undertaken include:

* As a pilot project in 2016, the Education Department conducted a domestic and sexual violence awareness and education programme at some of the elementary schools in Pohnpei.
* Women’s groups in the states have lobbied extensively for legislative protection. Pohnpei passed the Family Protection Act in 2017, addressing some gaps in protection of women and children (UPR 2010/11). In Yap State, in April 2018, a bill was introduced that provides for women from domestic violence and marital rape. Yap and Chuuk have yet to instigate specific legislation that provides for the protection of women against domestic violence and related issues.

Land ownership

While FSM is largely a matrilineal nation, most decision making related to land ownership and land use is managed by male members of the family.


Gender and Decision Making

Women rarely hold traditional leaders, deferring to men in community affairs. No woman has held one of the 14 positions in the FSM National Congress. However, there has been some representation of women at national cabinet and State Congress levels.

6.F.G

In Fiji, women reportedly provide around 80% of the seafood catch for their communities’ annual subsistence needs (Mangubhai 2002). However, the role of women in fisheries is often unrecognized, not recorded or poorly understood, leading to their overall limited engagement in fishing and decision-making in fisheries management.

International Gender commitments

Fiji has shown commitment to strengthening gender equality in the fisheries sector through several international commitments and national priorities and policies, including but not limited to the following:

* Progresing women’s equality in fisheries by building strategic partnerships with development organisations Sangeeta Mangubhai A.S.T., Sarah Leesoo, Anna Cooley et. Jayneen R. Mangubhai et al., Mercy J. Williams


---
**Page 175**
---

* The Convention on Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) – ratified in 1995.
* The 2030 Sustainable Development Agenda (SDG: 5) on gender equality and women empowerment.
* Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration signed 2012.

# National Gender Policies and Strategies

* Fijian National Gender Policy which should be reflected in both coastal and offshore fishing.
* Fiji’s Women in Fisheries Network (WFN): an interest group of women in the sector.

A few key points from the interviews:

* A few respondents indicated that the fisheries policy aimed at creating employment, women's contribution to the offshore fisherfolk sector labour force is impacted by cultural beliefs, traditional norms, and gender stereotypes. Many local fishing vessels are over 40 years old and have no safety equipment. Women who work in the industry are reluctant to take female on their vessels since they do not want to be accountable to any sexual harassment or violence that may occur.
* Women’s activities include fishing for subsistence purposes, as well as other activities to coastal areas, mainly harvesting (e.g., reef gleaning), seafood processing, and food preparation for home consumption.

However, women are increasingly participating in economic opportunities such as marketing or small businesses related to the handling, capture and sale of marine resources. For example, there is greater participation of women in the Management/Administrative areas and in processing.

## Key inputs from national consultations

34 Participants composed of 10 women and 24 men were interviewed. Ages of men ranged from 25 to 60 years old; ages of women ranged from 28 to 79. This was broken down into 18 men and 8 women from Natawalu and some Nakaliva as well as Lami villages in July 2023.

Three interesting insights came out of the surveys in terms of women’s roles and opportunities:

* Most head of households (HOD) were men – 3 FHFs.
* People live in extended families, with about 6-12 people per household.
* Most in the community own their own home. This will be the same in all other traditional fishing communities in Fiji.

Natawalu village & Nakaliva in the Ba Province in Western Viti Levu are settlements that comprise people who are originally from other Provinces. People spoken to were from Vanua Levu, Kadavu, Lau and Taveuni. The majority of the respondents were men (28) compared with those women who have no traditional mechanism of governance, no traditional clans (mataqalis) with their different messages. In Natawalu village there would be a chief, his spokes people clan, the carpenter clan and the traditional fisher’s clan. This is not the case in this settlement.

## Fishers Cooperatives/Fishers Association

Some of those interviewed are part of the Natawalu Fisherman Association (NFA) set up in the Community. This Cooperative is a collaboration between the Ministry of Fisheries and the


---
**Page 176**
---

Department of Cooperative Business (DCB), within the Ministry of Industry, Trade and Tourism (MITT), which is the regulatory and promotional authority on cooperatives.

In March 2023, the Deputy Director-General of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) visited the Natawau fishing community in Ba, Fiji to present fishing families with Fish Aggregation Devices (FADs).

- Foods consumed are almost the same with most households relying dominantly on fish and marine products. This will be the same for all other locations where FADs had been deployed.
- General fishing is done by both men and women using a variety of gear such as nets, lines, hooks, and spears in shallow waters and offshore reefs while women fish in mangroves, sand flats and inshore reefs. This will be the same for communities in Lautoka, Vatulele, Ra Yawasa and other locations where FADs had been deployed.
- House designs - There is not much difference in house designs in this community and those in rural Fiji land. This will be the same in outer islands and other locations where FADs had been deployed.
- Markets - Markets for fish are at the Ba and Lautoka municipal markets, Ba and Roadside stalls or mobile sellers buy from the communities and sell independently.

### Employment

Main livelihoods among community residents

There are a few people employed in these communities as most are dependent on fishing and farming as their main economic income sources. Two only work for the government, and one is a tradesman. This is typical of outer islands where most FADs were deployed. Only 6 respondents (18%) have other jobs. Most women are engaged in unpaid work and selling of fish is the main occupation. This means "unpaid work" in this context includes cooking for family, caring and carrying out domestic chores. However, all these activities also include fishing processing and marketing and distribution of men's catches.

Both men and women carry out fishing, women are engaged in coastal fishing as well as run a small-scale fishing trading business. Roadside stalls are community owned as people sell together- and now with the FADs, they have more fish to sell.

When women are carrying out shoreline fishing or are selling fish at the market, the men then assist with these domestic roles. Hence the family can operate as a unit undertaking complementary roles.

Most fishers work full time unless there is bad weather when there is no fishing. Seasonal fishing takes place for certain fish species along coastal areas and reefs, which women often target.


| Respondents employment status (n=34) |
| --- |
| Paid work | Unpaid roles |
| 6% | 94% |

Income Levels


---
**Page 177**
---

The average weekly wage from fishing is $150–$300. When the weather is good, a 6-day fishing week can earn them up to a thousand dollars. If it is bad weather, there is no fishing and thus no income. Many male fishers also market their fish by the roadside.

**Expenditure**

On average weekly expenditure is approximately $200, spent mainly on food items.


## Impacts of climatic events on fishers

The effects of climate change and increasing changes in weather and increasing number of storms, hinder fishing. Key impacts are as follows:

- There is also a lot of coral dying (bleaching) so it is harder to catch fish when diving.
- Safety concerns - the increase in stormy weather makes safety an issue when fishing when storm warnings are in place. There are sometimes unusually bigger waves that catch fishers by surprise and safety is becoming more an issue for fishers.
- Higher fuel costs - this has a direct impact on the cost of fishing trips as well as costs to move.
- Health concerns - longer hours fishing for men and women-and can affect health especially during pregnancy.

Many men are divers, and they are affected when the weather is cold. 

Food availability - Because of depletion of resources from reefs-diving there are less fish. Other factors include: changes in water temperature and salinity levels. These cause certain fish to be in certain areas, but now they do not appear or may come months later. Hence, traditional knowledge about fishing has they know and when to fish for certain species may in the future not be practical or useful.

## Fishing Practices

### Reef fishing (onshore) - 90% of fishers fish on reeds and free dive.

### Reef fishing (offshore) - 20% of men diving. 

Bottom trawling – rarely mentioned, although may be used. But is dying in preferred.

**FAD fishing:** When a FAD was installed - it was used for about 2 months when the fisher was out and then they would go back to free diving. Now there are less FADs, these can only last at the FADs was new. The FAD was deemed too far-and the community only has a few boats which are used for inshore fishing- it is pricey (fuel) and too time consuming to go out to the FADS.

Gleaning Eighty percent of women gleaned and used cast nets and fishing line. Some women also dive and use gleaning as an additional activity.

Crabbing and collecting sea cucumbers are among the fishing activities that women undertake.


## Seasonality and location of fish

Women also know seasonality of fish – that is where to fish for certain species and when to fish (seasons). There are times in the year when there is not enough fish for them due to bad weather.

Role of men and women in fishing industry

Fishing is for subsistence livelihoods and for income. All family member fish, yet roles may differ between women and men as highlighted below:

- Boat operation – men
- Offshore fishing including FADS - men
- Boat repair/maintenance – men


---
**Page 178**
---

* Post-harvest handling – women
* Cleaning of fish products – women
* Use of fish for food or sale – both men and women
* Selling fish at markets – both men, reef, and offshores – both men and women
* Catch coastal fish and other sea products – both men and women
* Sell fish at markets – both men and women
* Cook fish for home use – Women and men sometimes
* Cook fish for sale, e.g., restaurants, stalls, etc. – women

## Health issues

The major health issues suffered in the household are related to the colder weather including flu and COVID. Some people also suffer from NCDs.

COVID posed a considerable impact due to all markets and towns closing. People could not travel around to buy produce and fish. This was because all markets and towns closed.


## Nutrition

Households prefer fish over meat, which is considered to take more effort to prepare. Fish are sourced from their fishing activities. Meat (mainly chicken) is eaten about 2 times a week, whereas fish is eaten daily. In this context fish includes fish, crabs, shellfish, and seaweed.


### 6.4 Kiribati

#### General information[19]

Kiribati has a population of over 108,000 as of 2020 census and comprises 33 islands. It is one of the world's largest exclusive economic zones (EEZ) at 3.5 million km² and has a coastline of 1259km. There are 3 main island groups and more water area than land, therefore the communities of Kiribati rely heavily on fishing activities for subsistence and commercial purposes.

Fish and seaweeds contribute to GDP as was estimated an 13.6 million USD in 2015. Estimated exports of fish/seaweed products were valued at 121.4 million USD, tuna being the major fish exported from Kiribati[20].

The total, with more than half living on Tarawa atoll[21]. The capital, South Tarawa, is the most populated area. Kiribati is at the forefront of islands impacted by threats associated with climate change and sea level rise. It is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change impacts such as rising sea levels and increased frequency of extreme weather events[21]. All survey respondents were involved in some part of the fishing supply and demand chain, be it fisher, seller, or consumers.

#### Gender policies

Kiribati is a member of a number of international agreements
* 1979 United Nations Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and includes 1995 Optional Protocol
* 1995 Rio Declaration on Environment and Development, Agenda 21
* 1995 Beijing Platform for Action of UN World Conference on Women
* Millennium Development Goals (MDG 3.5) – Gender equality and WASH


[19] Fisheries and Aquaculture Division, Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations [viewed 23 July 20]
<https://www.fao.org/fishery/country-profile/kiribati/en>
[20] https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiribati
[21] https://thecommonwealth.org/news


---
**Page 179**
---

### Key input from national consultations

The fisheries sector was consulted through a mix of desktop information and complemented by a community engagement survey in August 2023. Key considerations focused on fish consumption, fishing activities, health, and nutrition (HAN). Thirty-eight respondents participated in the focus group discussions held in August 2023. The group consisted of 14 males and 17 females. Traditionally, fish is the main dish in Kiribati, showing the "true cultural identity of Kiribati people."

Women are traditionally not involved in the offshore fishing; however, are active participants in the shoreline fishing and gleaming. They also participate in the selling of fish in the roadside or main market and are responsible for marketing and selling the catch to the fishers.

Fish sellers indicated that they earned AUD$300-450 a month whereas fishers noted that income depended on catch which was usually under $300 a month.

### Fish consumption – health, nutrition and food security

There is a significant reliance on marine resources for the community's livelihood; government revenue from fishing licenses accounts for one of the highest per capita fish consumptions in the world at 76.3kg (2013) per person annually.

The 2015 census demonstrated that 12,196 households (67% of households) had at least one commercial fishing license and 84% of these were from outer islands compared to only 15% in those on outer islands. The census also demonstrated that 75% of households fished for consumption only, 19% for sale and sale and 4% for sale.

• Many islands have refrigeration for storage of local sale and shipment to Tarawa. However, catch from outer islands is often sold at the roadside or in markets where they are mainly sold by women vendors from insulated ice boxes. It is likely that the limited cold-chain facilities may contribute to foodborne diseases and other health issues affecting the health and safety of the community, contributing to communicable infectious diseases.

• Much of the catches are transhipped to other islands and overseas. As such, the purchase of reef fish from outer islands for frozen export may jeopardize long-term and future food security.

### FAD Fishing

Kiribati's fisheries sector has two main categories: coastal fisheries (subsistence and small-scale commercial – nearshore areas) and offshore fisheries – predominantly industrial scale fisheries.


---
**Page 180**
---

Coastal fishing is primarily carried out for subsistence purposes and for sales in local markets and only some coastal fisheries export.21

In 2021 the Fish FAD project deployed 4 FADs in the waters of Tarawa. It was expected that the FADs would support the livelihoods of Tarawa's large urban fisher population, increasing catches of larger fish, improving food security and reducing poverty. However, feedback from respondent groups from Betio and Taaruarua indicated that the FADs were no longer operational.

## Safety in the fisheries industry

### Fishing boats23)

The total number of artisanal fishing vessels in 2015 was estimated to be 4,766. Respondents indicated that most boats available are reliable for near shore fishing and there are skilled boat operators.

Subsistence and small-scale commercial fishing is conducted using traditional canoes, plywood canoes powered by outboard motors and larger outboard-powered craft.

From 2005 to 2010 there was an increase in the number of boat-owning families. To a certain extent catch size is dependent upon the number of fishing vessels licensed by Kiribati.

### Fishing Infrastructure24)

The majority of Kiribati's offshore catches are exported and therefore transferred to other ports. Catch from nearshore fisheries are mostly landed in South Tarawa and smaller catches are landed at villages throughout Kiribati.

## Climate Change

Fishers are aware of the changes in climate and safety can become an issue when there is a storm. Safety and health are key challenges. Furthermore, if they cannot fish then may not be enough food to feed their families.

## Training

Fishers have reported having a few trainings including:

- PPE
- First Aid
- Flares and appropriate safety equipment
- Basic first aid
- Radio Communication systems – eg GPs.

A number of measures to address gender equality in RMI have been adopted over the last 10 years. Some of these International and others National are summarized below. For example, two significant Agreements have included:

* **Ratification of CEDAW**
* **Ratification of CRC**

### National Gender policies

The National Gender Mainstreaming Policy, a critical policy that seeks to "engage adolescents and youth as strategic groups for ending the cycle of domestic violence." According to the study, RMI has adopted strategic measures for addressing gender issues in areas where they have been disadvantaged. The Ministry of Internal Affairs (MOIA) has adopted strategic measures for addressing gender issues (figure XX). These measures seek to eliminate discrimination against women and girls and promote gender equal rights in civil, political, economic, social, health, and cultural spheres.

* **Comprehensive National Study on Family Health and Safety 2014.** This study (FHSS) was initiated by the Ministry of Internal Affairs and conducted by Women United Together Marshall Islands (WUTM).
* **Dedicated Office in the Ministry of Internal Affairs with an operational budget.**

### Civil society organizations

Women United Together Marshall Islands (WUTM) is an organization representing the voices of women's clubs in RMI. WUTM's focus includes but not limited to - women empowerment, gender equality (young women), skills development, raising awareness on gender-based violence and child abuse, while protecting and strengthening Marshall values and culture.

### National gender-targeted legislation in RMI

Key gender-equality legislative efforts include:

* **Domestic Violence Prevention and Protection Act of 2011 to address gender equality and GBV**
* **Child Rights Protection Act of 2015**
* **Human Rights Committee Act of 2015**
* **Rights Persons with Disability Act of 2015**
* **Birth, Death, Marriage Registration Act of 2016**
* **Prohibition of Trafficking in Persons Act of 2017 (ADB 2020)**
* **National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan.** The plan outlines gender – gender sensitive strategies for climate change responses.

An issue relates to implementation and enforcement of the law which have been challenging, and addressing political, economic, social, and cultural barriers pose significant barriers to progress.


### 6.8 Nauru

#### Gender Policies

Nauru has committed to strengthening gender equality in the fisheries sector through international commitments and national priorities and policies, including but not limited to the following:

* **The Convention on Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) – efforts to ensure women benefit from resource conservation and development especially in rural areas. Ratified under the auspices of Nauru's relationship with NZ prior to 1994. However,**

Gender Assessment
Page 34 of 54


---
**Page 182**
---

the country has not independently ratified CEDAW or incorporated it into domestic law. Nue does not have legislation to prevent discrimination based on sex (non-compliant in this area).
• The UN Framework Convention on Climate Change and the Convention on Biodiversity. This considers women’s roles in resource management, including management of rivers, reefs, and oceans.
• Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration signed 2012 – recent report (2018) noted that while women are involved in local government councils they were still under-represented in senior management and decision-making roles.
• Being Declaration and Platform for Action signed 1995 – Nue has maintained good maternal health services.
• Nue Treaty on Cooperation in Fisheries Surveillance and Law Enforcement in the South Pacific (2013) – Nue is a signatory. The treaty covers Forum Fisheries Agency members about monitoring, control, and surveillance of fishing.
• 2015 Draft National Policy on Gender Equality - need for greater awareness and increased budgetary allocation to gender equality. Currently only five percent of the national budget allocation goes to the Department of Women. Four priority areas that include:
o Healthy, safe, and harmonious families.
o Decent employment opportunities for women and men for economic development and food security.
o Women and men participate equitably in decision making bodies and leadership positions in all sectors.
o Gender-responsive government policies and programmes are implemented in all sectors.
• Draft Family Law Bill – process defined for dealing with GBV through legal system - still in draft stage.

### Overview of Gender Dimensions in Nue
There is a good representation of women in the National Parliament (25 per cent) – the highest in the Pacific (apart from Australia and New Zealand).
Women are under-represented as managers and decision-makers in the public sector.
Strong gender stereotypes as to what constitutes women’s and men’s roles have created an inequitable participation in the labour force and food production.
Police statistics show that 50 per cent of all reported cases of domestic and psychological abuse are in Nue. Local response continues to be largely managed within the village or extended family networks, with a lack of awareness about the existence of formal services and can be considered as private family matter. This can discourage victims seeking help, with an accompanying lack of services available for victims, e.g., safehouse or counseling.
More than 90 per cent of land in Nue is held under customary tenure and only seven percent of households own the house they live in.
In 2017, 27.75% of households were headed by women.
The Nue National Disaster Plan of 2010 does not mention gender or the vulnerability of any group.

# Effects of Climatic Events and Fishing Activities

Most participants indicated that climatic events affected their ability to carry out their work. The following characteristics of FAD and reef fishing were mentioned:

- FADs were accessible via Niu canoes however weather events prevented regular fishing at FADs.
- Fishermen were more likely to reef fish onshore.
- Tools used included fishing rods, lines, hooks, sinkers, soft bait and traditional fishing rods. Bottom trawling was not a common method of fishing.
- Women were more likely to engage in gleaning compared with men. One interviewee indicated she did not appreciate FADs being used as a model for attracting fish as she felt that the fish attracted by FADs were different from those onshore. One respondent however indicated that FADs attracted different fish that were not normally present.
- Cast netting was not a method used by any of the interviewees nor was it considered an approach to fishing.

Interviewees who owned boats and canoes noted they had access to reliable boats suitable for nearshore fishing and they were in good working order. Operators of boats were also highly experienced.

There has been significant investment in marine education in recent years which has helped FAD fishermen and/or reef fisherfolk

### Gender policies

Nauru has committed to strengthening gender equality in the fisheries sector through international commitments and national priorities and policies, including but not limited to the following:

- Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) (Accession 1994)

* Optional Protocol to the CRC on the involvement of children in armed conflict (Signed 2000).
* Optional Protocol to the CRC on the sale of children, child prostitution and child pornography (Signed 2000)
* International Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Racial Discrimination (Signed 2001) • Convention on the Elimination of All forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) (Accession 2003) • Convention to Abolish Slavery and Its Related Practices (Accession 2012)
* Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (Accession:2012)

* Nauru is a signatory to the 2011 Joint Statement on Ending Acts of Violence Related Human Rights Violations Based solely on Sexual Orientation and Gender Identity at the UN Human Rights Council
* Nauru is not included within the World Risk Index on disaster risk or the Global Climate Risk Index due to its small size and lack of relevant data.

National legislation

* Nauru National Women's Policy 2014-2019 2014. Policy reviewed in 2021
* 2016 Crimes Act criminalises rape and sexual offences within marriages and de facto relationships, and provides that evidence of physical resistance is not necessary to prove non-consent.
* Under the Public Service Act 2016, women in the public service who are taken in service for an employment period of 12 weeks or more are entitled to maternity leave which is entitlement paid paternity leave
* Nauru has adopted the Framework for Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Reduction (ROAAdapt) recognises the gender-differentiated impact of disasters on the vulnerability of households and individuals, and states that the empowerment of both men and women is necessary in order to build resilience now and future.
* The definition of domestic violence in the Domestic Violence and Family Protection Act 2017 includes financial abuse, which is defined as any form of coercive control, economic and financial abuse, stalking, and cruel and degrading treatment.

## Palau

### Gender policies

An approximate 70% of Palauans rely on ocean resources for food, livelihoods, and recreation. On average, Palauans prepare about 50% of their own seafood within the household, indicating a high reliance on local marine resources for food security. This indicates the importance of sustainably managed and accessible marine resources.

Palau is a matrilineal and matrilocal society whereby lineage and titles are inherited from the mother's side of the family. Women traditionally hold positions of power and respect and hold central positions in Palauan society.

#### International Gender policies

Palau is committed to strengthening gender equity in the fisheries sector through several international conventions and agreements, including but not limited to:

- The Convention on Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) – signed in 2011, but they are yet to sign the Optional Protocol.
- The UN Framework Convention on Climate Change and the Convention on Biodiversity. This convention highlights women's roles in resource management, including management of rivers, reefs and oceans.
- Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action signed 1995 – Palau has introduced a National Gender Mainstreaming Policy in 2018 and established a Bureau of Aging, Disability and Gender.

#### National Gender policies

Palau’s Constitution prohibits discrimination based on sex, race, place of origin, language, religion or social status. Any form of discrimination of gender identity is not explicitly prohibited.
- Family Protection Act 2012 provides protection for victims of GBV.
    - Provides support to victims of GBV.
    - All women and men have the same opportunities to earn incomes and fulfill needs.
- Voluntary sterilization program in areas that needed improvement in Palau:
    - Universal mandated maternity leave
    - Legislation to address sexual harassment at the workplace.
    - Expanded childcare facilities
    - Increased access to services for victims of GBV.
    - Removal of gender discriminatory marriage and inheritance laws.

Gender Assessment

Page 39 of 54


---
**Page 187**
---

### Violence against women is considered by many in Palau to be a private family matter that should be dealt with within the family, not through the courts, which prevents women's ability to access services.

#### Health and nutrition

Many Palauans currently have a diet rich in seafoods, but also eat a range of unhealthy tinned meats such as Spam. There is the local interest to consume even more seafood and reduce this intake of unhealthy canned meat (ansumale) (Ferguson and Singh, unpublished data).

Large-scale studies on non-communicable diseases (NCDs), including cardiovascular disease, diabetes, and chronic kidney disease, have been conducted in Palau. For example, in 2017 and 2018, 36% of school age girls and 40% of school age boys were overweight or obese. In 2018, 36.3% of men reported that they currently smoke cigarettes compared to 57% of women. Women are more likely than men to add tobacco to betel nut.

#### Gender Roles

As recorded by the 2015 Census, 39.7% of households were headed by women. Eighty-one percent of the population reside in urban areas (not disaggregated by sex). Twenty-two percent of women and 5.6% of men were unpaid family workers helping with household duties in 2015 (Census 2015).

The 2015 Census identified that women undertaking tertiary education were more likely to specialise in business, administration, and law (34.0% of women and 15.7% of men); education (15.2% of women and 6.8% of men), while engineering and technology (11.2% of women and 3.0% of men) comprised 77% of students undertaking Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM) majors were men (Census 2015).

#### Employment

Data for the census 2014/2015 show that 79.1% of women and 84.8% of men were engaged in informal employment in the non-agricultural sector. Informally employed workers do not benefit from national labour legislation or social protections (UN Women, 2022).

Gleaners depend heavily on their catch for income and food and rely on inshore coastal resources that can accommodate a boat. Thus women are vulnerable to food insecurity and loss of sources of income from unsustainable coastal developments such as dredging, sand mining, and land reclamation, including polluted, and unproductive fishing grounds (Gender and Natural Resources Report 2019). Women also face the same threats to their resources as men do, with the added effects of climate change on their marine resources (Gender and Natural Resources Report 2020).

Gleaners and fishers reported the same four leading threats to their resources:

- climate change,
- overfishing,
- sedimentation, and
- plastics pollution.

Solutions to these challenges require global action; overfishing must be addressed locally.


## Papua New Guinea

Considerable efforts have been made to mainstream gender equality across all sectors in PNG. Sadly, GBV is widely accepted and "culturally condoned". While challenging this remains a commitment of the Government. Key international and national policies are summarised:

International Gender policies

PNG is committed to achieving gender equality across sectors including the fisheries sector through a number of international commitments:


---
**Page 189**
---

* The Convention on Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) – ratified on 12th January 1995.
* Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action signed 1995.

### National Gender policies

* PNGs Constitution provides for gender equality and equity, but customary law, recognised by the constitution, discriminates against women in relation to rights and property.
* The National Policy for Women and Gender Equality, 2011–2015 – the government’s mission to promote improved equality, participation, and empowerment of women.
* The Medium-Term Development Plan (MTDP), 2010-2030 and the Medium-Term Development Plan (MTDP), 2011-2015. These outline broad aspirations about gender equality, providing what should be done but this requires implementation institutions.

#### Key input from national consultations

Undertaking the community surveys poses some challenges in PNG related to the size of the country and population distribution across each location, and selection of a representative sample that provides an example of local reality differences. However, four locations were selected, and surveys were undertaken in July 2023. Locations surveyed were as follows:

* Karasau Island
* Vakuta Island
* Kol Island
* Kof Island

Twenty seven respondents were interviewed from different stages of the fishing supply chain from fishers, market vendors and consumers. Of these respondents 21 were male and 6 were female. Interviewees were aged between 27 and 53.

### Employment and income

Only one respondent had a full-time job while the remainder 26 carried out fishing and home duties. Fishing was carried out for subsistence and excess fish catches were sold. Earnings reported per month are ST$105 – $300 a month. Local Government earnings reportedly were ST$200 a month.

### Consumption of fish

All respondents consume fish every day and indeed favour fish over meat. Respondents indicated that they eat meat when there are fish shortages such as bad weather when the fishers cannot go out in their boats. The fish are caught on the reef and deep waters around each island.

gleaning activities, at least 9 women surveyed do consider themselves fishers. Some of these female fishers also carry out other roles in the household such as care giver. This is different from a 2018 SPC Survey[1] the aquaculture study that found that women seldom self-identify as agriculture farmers. Women are more likely to be involved with fishing activities related to processing and marketing. This recognition can enable women fishers to also receive necessary training and support if they have previously been excluded.

Despite this gradual change in thinking regarding the roles of women in fishing, when this is broken down further the roles remain much as they have always been with women having extra home roles on their fisher roles.


The main roles carried out by men and women in fishing as reported in the survey are as follows.
* Offshore fishing – male dominated – no female respondent is a fisher in this type of offshore fishing including FAD fishing. While most respondents consider the constraints to women carrying out FAD fishing, home commitments and lack of interest are the main reasons women do not carry it out.
* Coastal fishing – 100% men and 45% of women reportedly carry out coastal fishing.
* Boat handling – 98% men and 23% of women reported this role.
* Post-harvest handling – predominantly men, however a significant number of women are also involved in this activity.
* Fish cooking for home or selling use is predominately the role of women, however a number of men also undertake this role.
* Cleaning fish is shared equally between men and women.
* Selling fish is the role of both men and women.


### Solomon Islands

#### International Commitments
Solomon Islands has committed to strengthening gender equality in the fisheries sector through several international and national commitments, priorities and policies, including but not limited to the following:
* CEDAW – acceded 2002 – efforts to ensure women benefit from resource conservation and development especially in rural areas.
* The Paris Agreement on Climate Change and the Convention on Biodiversity. This considers men’s and women’s roles in resource management, including management of rivers, reefs and forests.

#### National Commitments
* Solomon Islands National Development Strategy – Objective 2 – highlights 'the need for the benefits of development to be more equitably distributed for the whole of Solomon Islands which includes improved gender equality and targeted actions to empower and include everyone.'
* National gender equality and women’s development policy, enabling policies and initiatives that facilitate gender mainstreaming - enabling policies and initiatives.

Women participants mentioned they are part of household decision making.

### Health

Many respondents and their families have been affected by the flu and colds, even COVID which has meant that respondents cannot earn while they are sick. Some market vendors were also affected by the flu and colds.

During COVID the market was closed and there was no income for families. Even after COVID respondents noted that fish selling is not as good as previously – reportedly the only good days to sell fish are Thursday to Saturday, and on Sunday for the UGM market.

### Nutrition

Respondents all noted that they prefer fish over meat. Respondent families eat one meal once a week or less which is mainly chicken wings. However, families eat fish from 2-3 times a week and tin fish every 5-6 days.

Most respondents indicated that it is faster to cook fish than meat. They also noted that fish prices are higher now than 3 years ago. With a family of 5/6 people it costs more than SBD 50 to feed the family fish. Furthermore, the size of the fish sold is smaller compared to 3 years ago.

Respondents indicated that the main reasons for not eating more fish relate to the following:

- Cost
- Cost of fishing
- Quality / freshness of fish concerns
- Access to fresh fish
- Fear of food poisoning. Many fish sellers at the market sell salted fish from fish that are not fresh

### Employment and earnings

All respondents interviewed work in the informal sector as fishermen or fish sellers. When there are no fish due to bad weather and other issues, they sell vegetables, clothing, cooked food, and other products in the market. While bad weather can affect sales, all respondents work full time.

Each respondent earns over SBD 600 a month.

### Expenses

Fish market respondents reportedly spend around SBD 250 a month whereas the Fishing village report around SBD 550 a month.


---
**Page 193**
---

Climate change effects

All respondents noted that climate change affects their work. They noted that bad weather and strong winds affect safety, cost to attain and sell their fish, personal health concerns as well as broader access to services and commodities.

Fishing activities are dictated by cultural taboos, especially in communities. Only men allowed in some communities. Respondents indicated that at times women know how to fish better than men, but cultural norms limit them to specific roles. ### Tonga

Gender policies

Tonga has committed to strengthening gender equality in the fisheries sector through several international commitments and national priorities and policies, including but not limited to the following:

- The UN Framework Convention on Climate Change and the Convention on Biodiversity. This considers men's and women's roles in resource management, including management of ocean resources.

Tonga is one of six countries in the Pacific that have not ratified CEDAW: National Commitments to Gender Equality

- The Constitution of Tonga states, "No laws shall be enacted for one class and not for another class, but the law shall be the same for all the people of the Land". Laws were always applied consistently.
- Tonga's Strategic Development Framework – Strong inclusive communities, by engaging districts/villages committees in meeting their prioritised service needs and ensuring equitable access to services.
- National Women’s Empowerment and Gender Equality Tonga Policy and Strategic Plan of Action 2019–2025.

# Climate change effects

All respondents noted that climate change affects their work. They noted that bad weather and strong winds affect safety to ataxi and sail their fish, personal health concerns, food availability as well as broader access to services and commodities.

## Health

Many respondents and their families have been affected by the flu and colds and even COVID which has meant that respondents cannot earn while they are sick. During COVID the market was closed but now there is no income for families. Even after COVID respondents noted that COVID affected the way the marketing operates, and it has been slow to re-establish normal operations and generate incomes.

## Nutrition

Respondents all noted that they prefer fish over meat. Respondent families reported eating meat daily, and this includes many lamb chops or chicken thighs or wings. However, families eat fresh fish 2-3 times a week in Eua and weekly in Ha'atafu. The main fish consumed are reef fish and tuna from longline fishing vessels. Most respondents indicated that it took longer preparatory time cooking fish than meat.

Respondents from both Ha'atafu and 'Eua noted that cost and limited access to quality fish were the main reasons they did not eat more fish.

## Fishing gender roles

Fishing activities tend to follow traditional patterns whereby offshore fishing and more laborious roles are carried out by men. Women fish and glean in coastal areas and carry out some of the less physically demanding roles such as cleaning and selling the fish. See the table below indicating main fishing activities of men and women in Tonga.

### 6.13 Tuvalu

#### Gender Policies

Tuvalu has committed to strengthening gender equality in the fisheries sector through a number of internal commitments and national priorities and policies, including but not limited to the following:

- The Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) – ratified in 2000 – efforts to ensure women benefit from resource conservation and development.
- The Convention on the Rights of the Child ratified in 2000.
- The UN Framework Convention on Climate Change and the Convention on Biodiversity. This concerns sustainable fisheries management, including management of rivers, reefs and oceans.
- Tuvalu National Gender Policy - opportunities for women in the fisheries sector.

Understandably, especially with many vulnerable households, "safety" is the dominant concern. This was followed by health, costs of climate-induced impacts, access to services and food availability.


Roles and Responsibilities

Constraints of traditional working roles highlighted fishing sector roles and responsibilities. Women do not carry out offshore fishing but are responsible for selling the fish. This is the same situation in both Funafuti and Nukualofa. Respondents (96%) indicated that there are constraints to women being involved in fishing activities. The main reasons given were lack of access to boats, lack of transport, and lack of knowledge. The main reasons men do not participate in offshore fishing is because they have other responsibilities.

# 6.14 Vanuatu

## Gender policies

The Vanuatu Government recognises that gender equality is part of the fundamental right and duty enshrined in its National Constitution of Vanuatu (National Gender Equality Policy 2015-2019).

The future of Vanuatu is shaped by active participation and meaningful contribution of all citizens.

### International commitments

Vanuatu is a signatory to several international and regional agreements on gender equality and the advancement of women. These include:

* Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and Optional Protocol;
* Millennium Development Goals;
* Beijing Platform for Action (BPFA);
* Accra Agenda for Action and the Busan Partnership for Effective Development Cooperation;
* International Conference on Population and Development (ICPD) Programme of Action;
* Pacific Plan;
* Revised Pacific Platform for Action on the Advancement of Women and Gender Equality;
* Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration

### National Commitments

* Constitution recognises the rights and freedoms of all individuals without discrimination on the grounds of sex, race, place of origin, religion or traditional beliefs, political opinions or language.
* The Vanuatu National Policy on Gender Equality affirms the Vanuatu Government's commitment towards gender equality across all sectors and at all levels of society and the elimination of discrimination against women and girls.
* the 2006 National Women’s Forum;
* the National Plan of Action for Women 2007-2011;
* Policy on Gender Equality in Education in Torba, Sarma, Penama, Malampa, Shefa and Tafea provinces, 2012-2013; and
* the 2014 National Strategy for Gender Validation towards men and women.
* the Comprehensive Reform Programme (CRP) and later in the Priorities and Action Agenda (PAA) 2006 – 2015.

### Key input from national consultations

28 responses from Vanuatu were surveyed from islands of Esete, Pele, Nguna, Ermao, Mioss and Lelepa. Ages of respondents were between 23 and 66. There were seven females and 21 male respondents.

What is shown clearly here is that most (58%) of respondents live with elderly family members. This indicates a high dependency rate which can increase household vulnerability.

## Home Ownership

- 16 respondents owned their own home, while
- 12 either lived with relatives or rented.

| Type of Home Ownership | Number of Respondents |

Fish is important to both the culture as well as the diet, as shown in Figure xx. Eighteen respondents (64%) of respondents eat fish daily. In comparison to 28% or 7 respondents who ate fish once a week.

Only one respondent preferred meat over fish.

85% percent of respondents indicated that women are constrained from fishing. The main constraints to women actively fishing is transport and lack of interest (96.5% of respondents). However, 75% of respondents indicated that women sell fish. Eighty-six percent of respondents noted that there were times when there was not enough fish to sell.
